CHAPTER 1 INCOME TAXES Purpose of income taxes-The Sixteenth Amendment-Courts' 

attitude toward income taxes-Administrative regulations-Definition of income-Taxable income What is the dominant purpose of income tax laws, according to Chief ice Stone of the Supreme Court of the United States?

The dominant purpose of the revenue laws is the taxation of income to se who earn or otherwise create the right to receive and enjoy the beneof it when paid. The tax laid by the 1934 Revenue Act upon income rived from... wages, or compensation for personal service, of whatever and in whatever form paid, ; also from interest . . ." therefore canfairly be interpreted as not applying to income derived from interest ompensation when he who is entitled to receive it makes use of his power ispose of it in procuring satisfactions which he would otherwise procure by the use of the money when received. 

Stone, J., [now C.J.] Helvering v. Horst, 311 U.S. 112, 1940.

How is the Sixteenth Amendment to the Constitution of the United States preted by the courts?

The Sixteenth Amendment declares that Congress shall have power to and collect taxes on income, "from whatever source derived" without ortionment among the several States, and without regard to any census numeration. It was not the purpose or effect of that Amendment to g any new subject within the taxing power. Congress already had er to tax all incomes. But taxes on incomes from some sources had held to be "direct taxes" within the meaning of the constitutional reement as to apportionment. Article I, section 2, clause 3; section 9, se 4. The Amendment relieved from that requirement and obliterated listinction in that respect between taxes on income that are direct taxes those that are not, and so put on the same basis all incomes "from whatsource derived." "Income" has been taken to mean the same thing as in the Corporation Excise Tax Act of 1909 in the Sixteenth Amendand in the various revenue acts subsequently passed. After full conation, this Court declared that income may be defined as gain derived à capital, from labor, or from both combined, including profit gained ngh sale or conversion of capital. And that definition has been adhered [1]

2

to and applied repeatedly. In determining what constitutes income substa rather than form is to be given controlling weight. 

Butler, J., Bowers v. Kerbaugh-Empire Co., 271 U.S. 170, 1926. 

How does the Supreme Court consider income taxes?

That the recipt of income by a resident of the territory of a ta sovereignty is a taxable event is universally recognized. Domicile itself fords a basis for such taxation. Enjoyment of the privileges of residend the state and the attendant right to invoke the protection of its laws inseparable from responsibility for sharing the costs of government. "T are what we pay for civilized society." A tax measured by the net inc of residents is an equitable method of distributing the burden of govern among those who are privileged to enjoy its benefits. The tax, which is portioned to the ability of the taxpayer to pay it, is founded upon the tection afforded by the state to the recipient of the income in his powe his right to receive the income and in his enjoyment of it when recei To them and to the equitable distribution of the tax burden, the econo advantage realized by the receipt of income and represented by the po to control it, bears a direct relationship.

Stone, J., [now C.J.] New York v. Graves, 300 U.S. 308, 1937. 

May an income tax be made retroactive?

As respects income tax statutes, it long has been the practice of Cong to make them retroactive for relatively short periods so as to include pr from transactions consummated while the statute was in process of en ment, or within so much of the calendar year as preceded the enactm and repeated decisions of this Court have recognized this practice and tained it as consistent with the due process of law clause of the Constitut 

Van Devanter, J., in U. S. v. Hudson, 57 S.Ct. 309, quoted by Parker, J., in White Packing Co. v. Robertson, 89 F. 2d 

775, 1937.

How do the United States Courts try to interpret Federal tax matters? 

This Court has often observed that taxation is concerned with real and that, in considering tax matters, we are controlled by matters of stance and not of form. Cf. Dodge Brothers v. United States 118 F. 2d 

1941.... As Mr. Chief Justice Stone recently stated in United States v. Pel

March 3, 1941, 61 S.Ct. 659,-: "But as we have often had occasion to p out, the revenue laws are to be construed in the light of their general purp to establish a nationwide scheme of taxation uniform in its application.. 

Dobie, J., Staunton I. L. Corp. v. Com., 120 F. 2d 930, 1941.

3 Do the courts in construing the federal income tax law endeavor to give it a iform application throughout the country?

In dealing with the meaning and application of an act of Congress enacted the exercise of its plenary power under the Constitution to tax income d to grant exemptions from that tax, it is the will of Congress which conols, and the expression of its will, in the absence of language evidencing a ferent purpose, should be interpreted "so as to give a uniform application a nation-wide scheme of taxation." Burnet v. Hughes, C.J., Lyeth v. Hoey, 59 S.Ct. 155, 1938. 

How do courts consider exemptions from taxation provided in statutes?

It is a familiar rule that statutes exempting property from taxation should strictly construed and one contending that his property is exempted from ch tax must show clearly that he is within the exception provided by tute. The same rule should be applied to a statute exempting certain acies from an inheritance tax. To be exempt from an inheritance tax, a acy must come within the strict letter of the statute. 

Good, J., In re Estate of Rudge, 114 Nebra. 335, quoted by Eberly, J., in In re Robinson's Estate, 292 N.Ŵ. 48,

Nebra. 1940. 

How do the courts consider Treasury regulations relative to tax questions?

Treasury regulations and interpretations long continued without subntial change, applying to unamended and substantially reenacted statutes, deemed to have received Congressional approval and have the effect of 

Helvering v. Winmill, 305 U.S. 79, quoted by Chesnut, J., in United Pocahontas Coal Co. v. U. S., 117 F. 2d 175, 1941. 

What is the usual effect of administrative regulations?

Administrative regulations are not conclusive, but are at most decisions ich can be changed, and afford to the courts in the ultimate tests nothing re than persuasive rules of construction. It has never been said that adnistrative action removes the statute from the field of judicial construction. 

Judge L. Hand remarked in F. W. Woolworth Co. v. U. S., 91 F.2d 973: t most, administrative practice is a weight in the scale, to be considered, not to be inevitably followed. . . . To suppose that Congress must parlarly correct each mistaken construction under penalty of incorporating nto... the statute appears to us unwarranted; our fiscal legislation is deed and specific enough already. While we are of course bound to weigh iously such rulings, they are never conclusive...." 

Biddle, J., Janney v. C. of I. R., 108 F. 2d 564, 1939.

Are the words of a statute imposing a tax ever extended by implication? 

In statutes levying taxes the literal meaning of the words employed most important, for such statutes are not to be extended by implication b yond the clear import of the language used. If the words are doubtful, t doubt must be resolved against the Government and in favor of the ta payer. Gould v. Gould, 245 U.S. 151. The rule is stated by Lord Cairns

Partington v. Attorney-General, L.R. 4 H.L. 100: 

"I am not at all sure that, in a case of this kind—a fiscal case-form is amply sufficient; because, as I understand the principles of all fiscal leg lation, it is this: If the person sought to be taxed comes within the letter the law he must be taxed, however great the hardship may appear to t judicial mind to be. On the other hand, if the Crown, seeking to recov the tax, cannot bring the subject within the letter of the law, the subject free, however apparently within the spirit of the law the case might othe wise appear to be. In other words, if there be admissible in any statute, wh is called an equitable construction, certainly such a construction is not a missible in a taxing statute, where you can simply adhere to the words of t statute." 

Sutherland, J., United States v. Merriam, 263 U.S. 179, 1923.

Why is the payment of taxes the most important of all debts of a taxpaye The principle underlying tax priority is ancient and well-established. is one of the many illustrations of the maxim "salus populi suprema lex est Mr. Justice Story gave it expression and effect over 100 years ago. "The right of priority of payment of debts due to the government, i prerogative of the crown well known to the common law. It is founded m so much upon any personal advantage to the sovereign, as upon motives public policy, in order to secure an adequate revenue to sustain the pub burdens, and discharge the public debts. The claim of the United Stat however, does not stand upon any sovereign prerogative, but is exclusive founded upon the actual provisions of their own statutes. The same poli which governed in the case of the royal prerogative, may be clearly trac in these statutes; and as that policy has mainly a reference to the public goo there is no reason for giving to them a strict and narrow interpretation. Li all other statutes of this nature, they ought to receive a fair and reasonab interpretation, according to the just import of their terms." United Stat v. State Bank of North Carolina, 6 Pet. 29, 35, 8 L.Ed. 308.

This admonition has been followed in the overwhelming weight of a thority. 

Clark, J., In re Wm. Akers, Jr., Co., 121 F. 2d 846, 1941. 

What is meant by income? "Income may be defined as the gain derived from capital, from labor, from both combined," provided it be understood to include profit gaine

Doyle Case (Doyle v. Mitchell Bros. Co.) 247 U.S. 179.

S.Ct. 155, 1938. Is the definition of income that is "gain derived from capital, from labor or oth combined" adequate?

Brief as it is, it indicates the characteristic and distinguishing attributes E income essential for a correct solution of the present controversy. The overnment, although basing its argument upon the definition as quoted aced chief emphasis upon the word "gain" which was extended to include variety of meanings; while the significance of the next three words was ther overlooked or misconceived. "Derived-from-capital"; "the-gain derived-from-capital," etc. Here we have the essential matter, not a in accruing to capital; not a growth or increment of value in the investent; but a gain, a profit, something of exchangeable value proceeding from e property severed from the capital, however invested or employed, and oming in, being "derived,"—that is, received or drawn, by the recipient for s separate use, benefit or disposal-that is income derived from property. 

Doyle v. Mitchell Bros. Co., 247 U.S. 179, quoted by 

Coleman, J., in Hilgenberg v. U. S., 21 F. Supp. 453, 1937. 

How may the courts consider "income" for tax purposes?

The income that is subject to a man's unfettered command and that he is ee to enjoy at his option may be taxed to him as his income, whether he es fit to enjoy it or not.

Corliss v. Bowers, 281 U.S. 376. 

It is in the public interest that no one should be permitted to avoid his st share of the tax burden except by positive command of law. 

Stone v. White, 301 U.S. 532.

An income can be derived from many sources. From invested capital, om labor, from the exercise of skill, ingenuity or sound judgment, earnings om a calling, interest, receipts of a profession, business or occupation; venue, royalties, salaries, rents, and from other sources but it is the manist intention of the people of Florida that a tax on income shall not be vied by the State of Florida or under its authority. 

Chapman, J., State v. Keller, 191 So. 542, Fla. 1939.

What is the difference between an income tax and a property tax? 

The tax levied on income is not a property tax, but is a percentage laid the amount which a man received, irrespective of whether he spends it, astes it, or invests it. 

Featherstone v. Norman, 170 Ga. 370.

The incidence of a tax on income differ from that of a tax on proper Neither tax is dependent upon the possession by the taxpayer of the subje of the other. His income may be taxed, although he owns no property, a his property may be taxed, although it produces no income. 

New York ex rel Cohn v. Graves, 300 U.S. 308, both quotations used by Cardozo, J., in Hale v. Iowa St. B. of A. & 

R., 302 U.S. 95, 58 S.Ct. 102, 1937.

How does a tax collector usually consider income as compared with courts and Congress?

To the tax collector, all receipts are income. An exception to even enthusiasm used to but no longer prevails. It is amusingly put by an Engl writer "Pure acts of grace and favor, the outcome of goodness of hea perhaps done at time of rejoicing should not be taxed." T. Hallat Fry 

Income Tax, page 10. The economists' definition is, as might be suppos inclusive "the money value of the net accretion in one's economic pow between two points of time," Seligman, The Income Tax (2d Ed. 1914 

The interpretation of both the Congress and the courts has been narrow and as narrower more complicated, than either of the other two. The cour unless Congress orders otherwise fall back upon old friend “ordinary mea ing." Merchants L. & T. Co. v. Smietanka, 255 U.S. 509. 1921 (on some rath illusory theory that the state legislatures who ratified the 16th Amendme had some idea of an "ordinary meaning" for such an economic abstraction Congress has usually tried to follow the same conception. 

Clark, J., Union T. Co. v. C. I. R., 115 F. 2d 86, 1940. 

Must income to be taxable be money?

While it is true that economic gain is not always taxable as income, it settled that the realization of gain need not be in cash derived from the sa of an asset. Gain may occur as a result of exchange of property, relief fro a liability, or other profit realized from the completion of a transaction. T fact that the gain is a portion of the value of property received by the ta payer in the transaction does not negative its realization. 

Here, as a result of a business transaction the taxpayer received back land with a new building on it, which added an ascertainable amount to value. It is not necessary to recognition of taxable gain that he should able to sever the improvement begetting the gain from his original capit If that were necessary, no income could arise from the exchange of propert whereas such gain has always been recognized as realized taxable gain. 

Roberts, J., Helvering v. Braun, 309 U.S. 461, 1940.

Is the realization of the income the event that is taxed or the acquisition the right to receive it?

From the beginning the revenue laws have been interpreted as definir "realization" of income as the taxable event, rather than the acquisition the right to receive it. And "realization" is not deemed to occur until t

INCOME TAXES 

7 acome is paid. But the decisions and regulations have consistently recogized that receipt in cash or property is not the only characteristic of realiation of income to a taxpayer on the cash receipt basis. Where the taxayer does not receive payment of income in money or property, realiation may occur when the last step is taken by which he obtains the fruition f the economic gain which has already accrued to him. 

In the ordinary case the taxpayer who acquires the right to receive inome is taxed when he receives it, regardless of the time when his right to eceive payment accrued. But the rule that income is not taxable until calized has never been taken to mean that the taxpayer, even on the cash ceipt basis, who has fully enjoyed the benefit of the economic gain reprented by his right to receive income, can escape taxation because he has ot himself received payment of it from his obligor. The rule, founded on 

Iministrative convenience, is only one of postponement of the tax to the nal event of enjoyment of the income, usually the receipt of it by the xpayer, and not one of exemption from taxation where the enjoyment is onsummated by some event other than the taxpayer's personal receipt of oney or property. This may occur when he has made such use or disosition of his power to receive or control the income as to procure in its ace other satisfactions which are of economic worth. ... 

Simons, J., C. L. Gransden & Co. v. C. I. R., 117 F. 2d 80, 1941.

May income be realized and therefore taxable which does not add to the onomic worth of the taxpayer?

It has frequently been held that income may be realized upon a change the nature of legal rights held, although the particular taxpayer has enyed no addition to his economic worth. As said in Helvering v. Midland s. Co., 300 U.S. 216: "Where the legal effect of a transaction fits the plain ter of the statute, the tax is held payable unless there is clearly revealed in e act itself or in its history a definite intention to exclude such transactions om the operation of its applicable language." As the property there was e medium through which interest was received, so here the rights exanged operate to extinguish the liability under the contract, and as there served in reliance upon Weiss v. Wiener, 279 U.S. 333, "The income tax vs do not profess to embody perfect economic theory.' 

Simons, J., C. L. Gransden & Co. v. C. I. R., 117 F. 2d 80, 1941.

What is the purpose of a tax return?

The purpose in requiring taxpayers to make returns is to promptly bri to the attention of the Government their taxable income. It is well und stood that the government relies largely on the return for the correctn of the tax, and it is not complete unless sworn to by the taxpayer. Sor courts hold the rule that a fraud penalty cannot be imposed on the ta payer unless the facts would also support a criminal prosecution. I do n so view the act here in question. If there has been delicate suppression vital facts in the return, which is misleading, it is a fraud, and the misrep sentation will support fraud penalties if made recklessly without knowled or if so carelessly done as to lead a trier of facts to believe that the pers on whom the duty rests to disclose has knowingly and intentionally failed do what the law requires. 

Hamilton, J., White v. U. S., 20 F. Supp. 623, 1937. 

Who must make an income tax return? 

(a) INDIVIDUAL RETURNS.-Section 51 (a) of the Internal Revenue Co is amended to read as follows: 

"(a) REQUIREMENT.-The following individuals shall each make und oath a return stating specifically the items of his gross income and the ductions and credits allowed under this chapter and such other informati for the purpose of carrying out the provisions of this chapter as the Co missioner with the approval of the Secretary may by regulations prescribe "(1) Every individual who is single or who is married but not livi with husband or wife, if having a gross income for the taxable year of $7 or over. "(2) Every individual who is married and living with husband wife, if no joint return is made under subsection (b) and if— "(A) Such individual has for the taxable year a gross income $1,500 or over, and the other spouse has no gross income; or [8]

"(B) Such individual and his spouse each has for the taxable year a gross income and the aggregate gross income is $1,500 or over.' 

Internal Revenue Code, Sec. 51(a) as amended by the 

1941 Act. 

Must a minor fill out a tax return? Who must return the earnings of a nor, the parent or the minor?

An individual, although a minor, who is single, is required to render a turn of income if he has a gross income for the taxable year of $750.00. If e aggregate of the net income of such a minor from any property which possesses, and from any funds held in trust for him by a trustee or ardian, and from his earnings which belong to him, is at least $750.00, a curn, as in the case of any other individual, must be made by him or for n by his guardian, or other person charged with the care of his person or operty. If under the laws of a State the earnings of a minor belong to the nor, such earnings, regardless of amount, are not required to be included the return of the parent. In the absence of proof to the contrary, a parent 

Il be assumed to have the legal right to the earnings of the minor and ast include them in his return. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Sec. 19.51-3, page 211, 1940. 

How may the taxpayer get a form and make his return?

Copies of the prescribed return forms will so far as possible be furnished payers by collectors. A taxpayer will not be excused from making a urn, however, by the fact that no return form has been furnished to him. xpayers not supplied with the proper forms should make application thereto the collector in ample time to have their returns prepared, verified, and d with the collector on or before the due date. Each taxpayer should carely prepare his return so as fully and clearly to set forth the data therein led for. Returns which have not been so prepared will not be accepted meeting the requirements of the Internal Revenue Code. In lack of a scribed form a statement made by a taxpayer disclosing his gross income I the deductions therefrom may be accepted as a tentative return, and if d within the prescribed time the statement so made will relieve the taxver from liability to penalties, provided that without unnecessary delay h a tentative return is supplemented by a return made on the proper form. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.51-5, page 213, 1940.

How are joint returns of husband and wife usually held under our income laws?

The provisions for joint returns in section 51 (b) of the Revenue Act of 4 was in substantially the same form as the corresponding provision in prior revenue acts from 1921. The import of that provision is that in king a joint return the husband and wife should report their aggregate ss income and could combine their deductions in reporting their aggree net income upon which the tax was to be computed. That was the struction placed upon the original provisions for joint returns, in the

Revenue Act of 1918, by the Solicitor of Internal Revenue. He said in h ruling: "If a single joint return is filed it is treated as the return of a taxab unit and the net income disclosed by the return is subject to both norm and surtax as though the return were that of a single individual. In cas therefore, in which the husband or wife has allowable deductions in exce of his or her gross income, such excess may, if joint return is filed, be d ducted from the net income of the other for the purpose of computing bo the normal and surtax." We think that this was the intention of Congre in enacting the Act of 1921 and the later acts containing the same provisi for joint returns. We think that it was also the fair import of the Treasu 

Regulations under the Act of 1921 and of subsequent regulations prior 1934.

Respondent places emphasis on the phrasing of Article 401 of Reg lations 62 under the Act of 1921, that "in a single joint return, the tax computed on the aggregate income and all deductions and credits to whi either is entitled shall be taken from such aggregate income." The argume stresses the words "to which either is entitled" and it is urged that ea spouse is entitled only to deduct 15 per cent of his or her separate n income. But we think that this is an inadmissible construction of the statu and is not a necessary construction of the regulation. Such a construction inconsistent with the premise of the solicitor's opinion, above mentione that a joint return "is treated as the return of a taxable unit" and the t is to be laid as though the return were that "of a single individual.” T more specific language of the provision in the Act of 1921, which for t present purpose is the same as that in the Act of 1934, affords a strong basis for this conclusion. It provides specifically for the inclusion of t income of each spouse "in a single joint return" and in that case that "t tax shall be computed on the aggregate income. The principle that the joi return is to be treated as the return of a "taxable unit" and as though were made by a “single individual" would be violated if in making a joi return each spouse were compelled to calculate his or her charitable co tributions as if he or she were making a separate return. The principle of joint return permitted aggregation of income and deduction and thus ove rode the limitations incident to separate returns. We find no indication 

Article 401 of Regulations 62 under the Act of 1921 of any intention to d part from the solicitor's view as to the purport of the statute. 

In 1935, by Article 23 (0)-1 of Treasury Regulation 86, the Depar ment sought to require a husband and wife, whether making "a joint retu or separate returns," to base their deduction for charitable contribution the separate net income of the spouse making them. We are of the opini that under the Revenue Act of 1934, taken with the meaning we think had when enacted, petitioners were entitled to the combined deductions th claimed, and that the departmental regulation to the contrary was ineffecti to deprive them of the right. 

Hughes, C.J., Taft v. Helvering, 311 U.S. 195, 1940.

May a person change the form of tax return after he has filed one? 

The courts have uniformly held that the right of choice or election file one or another sort of return is exercised by filing the return.

It is settled that he cannot afterwards change. This is even so where the payer, under a mistake of law, was unaware that he had the right to Loose. 

Moran v. C. of I. R., 67 F. 2d 601, quoted by Garrecht, J., in Pac. Nat. Co. v. Welch, 91 F. 2d 590, 1937. 

How do the courts consider an amendment to a first return? 

In the Fourth Circuit, in C. H. Mead Coal Co. v. Com., 106 F. 2d 388, e court held that in using the words "first return" Congress intended to clude a first return as properly amended. Any other construction, the urt thought, would, in fairness to the taxpayer, be too narrow. Proled the amendment is timely, and is made in good faith in order to cort a mistake, a first return was held to be no less such because amended. the phrase "timely amendment" we understand that the court meant an ended return made by the taxpayer, as in that case, "immediately upon rning of its mistake, and before the time for filing its return for the next cceeding year. The holding appears to confuse the statutory requirement th the supposed authority or duty of the Commissioner to relieve against stakes. 

Healy, J., J. E. Riley Inv. Co. v. Com., 110 F. 2d 655, 1940.

What is the effect of a filing a second or supplementary return on the ginal return?

From the administrative history the influence is compelling that a second urn, reporting an additional tax, is an amendment or supplement to a ren already upon the files, and being effective by relation does not toll a itation which has once begun to run. Perfect accuracy or completes is not necessary to rescue a return from nullity, if it purports to be a urn, is sworn to as such, and evinces an honest and genuine endeavor to isfy the law. This is so though at the time of filing the omissions or inacracies are such as to make amendment necessary. Even more clearly is it when the return is full and accurate in the beginning under the statutes n in force, but is made inaccurate or incomplete by supervening changes the law, unforeseen and unforeseeable. Supplement and correction in such cumstances will not take from a taxpayer free from personal fault, the otection of a term of limitation already running for his benefit. 

Cardozo, J., Zellerbach Paper Co. v. Helvering, 293 U.S. 172. 

What are the general principles of income tax accounting?

In general the income tax law is concerned only with realized gains and ses; and where the taxpayer's accounts are kept on a cash basis and not an accrual basis, he receives taxable income only when it actually comes o his possession or, if only constructively received, when the amount is initely ascertained and subject to his unrestricted control. But in view of ctical necessities, income tax accounting with the Government must be an annual basis, and, therefore, moneys received by a taxpayer as his n under a claim of right and without restriction as to their disposition

are taxable for the year in which they are received and retained even thou in a later year the taxpayer is obliged to refund them in whole or in pa in which event he would have a claim for deduction in the later year.. 

A cardinal principle of federal income taxation requires annual retu and accounting, and this principle requires the determination of income the close of the taxable year without regard to the effect of subseque events. ...

Constructively received income is taxable when the amount is definit liquidated and available to the taxpayer without restriction. 

Chesnut, J., Penn. v. Robertson, 115 F. 2d 167, 1940.

On what period of acquiring income is the United States tax assessed? 

The net income shall be computed upon the basis of the taxpayer's ann accounting period (fiscal year or calendar year, as the case may be) in a cordance with the method of accounting regularly employed in keeping t books of such taxpayer; but if no such method of accounting has been employed, or if the method employed does not clearly reflect the incom the computation shall be made in accordance with such method as in t opinion of the Commissioner does clearly reflect the income. If the t payer's annual accounting period is other than a fiscal year as defined in s tion 48 or if the taxpayer has no annual accounting period or does not ke books, the net income shall be computed on the basis of the calendar year. 

Internal Revenue Code, Sec. 41. 

What is the period of income on which taxes are based? 

The true meaning of a single section of a statute in a setting as compl as that of the revenue act, however precise its language cannot be asc tained if it be considered apart from related sections, or if the mind be is lated from the history of the income tax legislation of which it is an integ part. The revenue acts since the Sixteenth Amendment have consistent assessed income taxes on the basis of annual accounting periods, either t calendar year or the different fiscal year which the taxpayer may ado From the beginning these periods have been known as taxable years and t provisions of the taxing statutes have been drafted and enacted with prima reference to such normal accounting periods. 

Stone, J., [now C.J.] Helvering v. Morgan's Inc., 293 

U.S. 121.

What is the difference between a calendar year and a fiscal year? 

The return of a taxpayer is made and his income computed for his taxe year, which in general means his fiscal year, or the calendar year if he not established a fiscal year. (See section 48.) The term "fiscal year" ans an accounting period of 12 months ending on the last day of any nth other than December. No fiscal year will, however, be recognized ess before its close it was definitely established as an accounting period the taxpayer and the books of such taxpayer were kept in accordance rewith. A person having no such fiscal year must make his return on basis of the calendar year. Except in the case of a first return for income a taxpayer shall make his return on the basis upon which he made his urn for the taxable year immediately preceding, unless, with the approval the Commissioner, he has changed his accounting period. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.41-4, page 189, 1940.

What is the period in which the gross income must be included? 

42. PERIOD IN WHICH ITEMS OF GROSS INCOME ARE INCLUDED. 

The amount of all items of gross income shall be included in the gross ome for the taxable year in which received by the taxpayer, unless, under hods of accounting permitted under section 41, any such amounts are to properly accounted for as of a different period. In the case of the death taxpayer there shall be included in computing net income for the taxable od in which falls the date of his death, amounts accrued up to the date of death if not otherwise properly includible in respect of such period or a or period.

SEC. 19.42-1. WHEN INCLUDED IN GROSS INCOME.-Except as otherwise pro ed in section 42 in the case of the death of a taxpayer, gains, profits, and ome are to be included in the gross income for the taxable year in which y are received by the taxpayer, unless they are included as of a different od in accordance with the approved method of accounting followed by . (See articles 41-1 to 41-3.) If a taxpayer has died there shall also be uded in computing net income for the taxable period in which he died

amounts accrued up to the date of his death if not otherwise properly i cludible in respect of such period or a prior period, regardless of the fa that the decedent may have kept his books and made his returns on the ba of cash receipts and disbursements. If no determination of compensation had until the completion of the services, the amount received is ordinar income for the taxable year of its determination, if the return is rendered the accrual basis; or, for the taxable year in which received, if the return rendered on the receipts and disbursements basis. If a person sues in year on a pecuniary claim or for property, and money or property is covered on a judgment therefor in a later year, income is realized in the la year, assuming that the money or property would have been income in earlier year if then received. This is true of a recovery for patent infring ment. Bad debts or accounts charged off subsequent to March 1, 1913, I cause of the fact that they were determined to be worthless, which are s sequently recovered, whether or not by suit, constitute income for the y in which recovered, regardless of the date when the amounts were charg off. (See article 23 (k)-1.) Such items as claims for compensation un canceled Government contracts constitute income for the year in wh they are allowed or their value is otherwise definitely determined, if return is rendered on the accrual basis; or, for the year in which received the return is rendered on the basis of cash receipts and disbursements.

Should cash received in a year always be considered as income? 

Cash, or its equivalent, received in a profitable transaction is income year of receipt unless its title is subject to a substantial contingency a where the question arises, each case must be adjudicated on the facts pecul to it. It is settled law that if a taxpayer derives a profit without restriction to its disposition it is income even though there is a contingency which n require him to restore its equivalent in a later year. 

Hamilton, J., First National Bank v. Com., 107 F. 2d 141, 1939.

If a taxpayer receives money which he does not know whether he is leg entitled to retain or not, must he return it as income in the year of receipt? 

Revenue taxation requires that one who received a sum of money subj to his disposition should account for the same as income in the year he ceives it and not at some indefinite future time of his own selection. Sho the practice urged by the taxpayer (or returning it when certain) be lowed, the collection of the revenues of the national government would uncertain, for the government would be taking the chance that the mo is still available for taxation during subsequent years, and that the taxpa is still solvent. 

Brown v. Commissioner, 63 F. 2d 66, quoted by Lindley, J., in Griffin v. Smith, 101 F. 2d 348, 1939.

How does Chief Justice Stone explain when taxes accrue? Only a word need be said with reference to the contention that the tax on munitions manufactured and sold in 1916 did not accrue until 1917. In technical legal sense it may be argued that a tax does not accrue until it s been assessed and becomes due; but it is also true that in advance of the sessment of a tax, all the events may occur which fix the amount of the tax d determine the liability of the taxpayer to pay it. In this respect... the unitions tax here in question did not stand on any different footing than her accrued expenses appearing on appellee's books. 

Stone, J., [now C.J.] U. S. v. Anderson, 269 U.S. 422, quoted by Magruder, J., in Geo. S. Colton Elastic Web 

Co. v. U. S., 116 F. 2d 202, 1940.

Must the taxpayer return all receipts in a year regardless of whether a deal volving the receipt is not terminated and when it is terminated the taxpayer y sustain a loss instead of a gain in income? 

Section 22 of the Revenue Act of 1932, defines gross income as including ins, profits, and income derived from commerce or sales or dealings in operty growing out of the ownership or use of or interest in such propy, or from the transaction of any business carried on for gain or profit, d income derived from any source whatever. Section 41 of that Act proles that the net income shall be computed upon the basis of the taxpayer's nual accounting period in accordance with the method of accounting regrly employed in keeping the books of such taxpayer. Federal income taxes thus laid upon the gains of a statutory annual accounting period of twelve onths. If a taxpayer is in receipt of a net income in one year, it must pay ax upon that income, although if combined with another year the result uld be a loss. 

Holmes, J., Valley Waste Mills v. Page, 115 F. 2d 466, 1940.

May a gain received in one year be charged to income some years later, en discovered?

The charging as income in 1936 of gain actually received in 1926 involves constitutional question. Under the Sixteenth Amendment, that which is income cannot be taxed as such, but there is no requirement as to the r in which income shall be taxed. Congress could fix decennial periods tead of annual ones. It has in general adopted the annual plan, but has horized either actual receipts and disbursements or accrual as the basis of accounting. On the latter basis, there may be a difference of years in ering the items on the account from what would have been true on a basis actual receipts and disbursements. But these two bases are elastic, for in 

Revenue Act of 1926, Sec. 200 (d), 26 U.S.C.A. Int. Rev. Acts, page 146, ich directs income and deductions to be accounted for in the year received d paid or accrued, there is an exception: "Unless in order to clearly reflect income the deductions or credits should be taken as of a different period." nilar language occurs in the other Acts. The clear reflection of income

that ought to be taxed is the main object. See Guaranty Trust Co. v. Cor missioner, 303 U.S. 493, at page 498, 58 S. Ct. 673, 82 L. Ed. 975. With without the aid of a Regulation, there have been many cases of this so-call "restoration to income" of a deduction taken in a prior year. On the accr basis, if a tax, or expense, or other deduction is taken one year as accrue but it turns out in another year that there was no such liability or a less of the matter is corrected by a charge to income as though there had been recovery back. So when on a cash basis a debt is deducted as bad and another year is collected in whole or in part, the matter is corrected not going back to the year of deduction, but by a charge to income in the la year. Askin & Marine Co. v. Commissioner, 2 Cir., 66 F. 2d 776; Putn 

Bank v. Commissioner, 5 Cir., 50 F. 2d 158. And when property is sold be paid for in instalments, and only part of what is paid is returned as ga but the property is gotten back without any refund, so that all that was ceived turns out to be gain, the addition is made to income in the year additional gain appears to be such. Compare Walker v. Thomas, Collect 

5 Cir., 119 F. 2d 58. So when a deduction for an exempt service is taken, a the reserve is released in a later year, the released fund is taxed as income the latter year though not earned then. 

Commissioner's action was just and justified. 

Sibley, J., Sneed v. Com., 119 F. 2d 767, 1941.

If a taxpayer receives money with reference to which a law suit is pendi should he include it in his return prior to the termination of the litigation? The net profits earned by the property in 1916 were not income of year 1922—the year in which the litigation with the government was fina terminated. They became income of the company in 1917, when it first came entitled to them and when it actually received them. If a taxpayer ceives earnings under a claim of right and without restriction as to disposition, he has received income which he is required to return, et though it may still be claimed that he is not entitled to return the mon and even though he may still be adjudged liable to restore its equivalent. 

North American Oil Consolidated v. Burnet, 286 U.S. 417, quoted by Jenney, J., Grand Central Public Market v. 

U. S., 22 F. Supp. 119, 1938.

F. Supp. 119, 1938.

What method of accounting, if any, is recommended by Treasury Re lations?

It is recognized that no uniform method of accounting can be prescri for all taxpayers, and the law contemplates that each taxpayer shall ad

ch forms and systems of accounting as are in his judgment best suited to purpose. Each taxpayer is required by law to make a return of his true come. He must, therefore, maintain such accounting records as will enable n to do so. (See section 54 and section 19.54-1.) Among the essentials the following: (1) In all cases in which the production, purchase, or sale of merchandise any kind is an income-producing factor, inventories of the merchandise hand (including finished goods, work in process, raw materials, and supes) should be taken at the beginning and end of the year and used in comting the net income of the year. (2) Expenditures made during the year should be properly classified as ween capital and expense; that is to say, expenditures for items of plant, ipment, etc., which have a useful life extending substantially beyond the r should be charged to a capital account and not to an expense account; ​(3) In any case in which the cost of capital assets is being recovered ough deductions for wear and tear, depletion, or obsolescence, any exditure (other than ordinary repairs) made to restore the property or long its useful life should be added to the property account or charged inst the appropriate reserve and not to current expenses. 

Treasury Regulation No. 103, Section 19.41-3, page 188, 1940.

May a taxpayer return part of his income on a cash basis and part on an tual basis? ...

It is settled beyond cavil that taxpayers may not accrue receipts and at expenditures on a cash basis or vice versa. Nor may they accrue a pora of income and deal with the remainder on a cash basis nor take deducas partly on one and partly on the other basis. 

Mass. M. L. Ins. Co. v. U. S., 288 U.S. 269, quoted by 

Healy, J., in J. H. Martinus & Sons v. C. I. R., 116 F. 2d 

732, 1940.

If a taxpayer has made his return on an accrual basis will he be permitted to nge at will after the period for making the return has elapsed? 

The taxpayer, having kept its books and returned its income on the accrual s, could not, after the time for filing returns had expired, change from method it had consistently employed. To allow change at will from one hod of accounting and computation to another would require recomation and readjustment of tax liability, and would result in confusion, y, and inconvenience and "impose burdensome uncertainties upon the inistration of the revenue laws. It would operate to enlarge the statutory od for filing returns.' 99 The taxpayer was under no obligation to pay a tax on income it might er receive. But having taken a position and made an election it could not, r the time for filing returns had expired, change its position in view of r experiences and demand tax recomputation on a new basis. 

McCord, J., Elmwood Corp. v. U. S., 107 F. 2d 111, 1939.

What is the meaning of "paid or incurred" and "paid or accrued." "PAID OR INCURRED" AND "PAID OR ACCRUED."—(a) The terms "paid or curred" and "paid or accrued" will be construed according to the meth of accounting upon the basis of which the net income is computed by taxpayer. [See section 48 (c).] The deductions and credits provided for chapter 1 (other than the dividends paid credit provided in section 27) m be taken for the taxable year in which "paid or accrued" or "paid or curred," unless in order clearly to reflect the income such deductions credits should be taken as of a different period. If a taxpayer desires to cla a deduction or a credit as of a period other than the period in which it w "paid or accrued" or "paid or incurred," he shall attach to his return a sta ment setting forth his request for consideration of the case by the Comm sioner together with a complete statement of the facts upon which he rel However, in his income tax return he shall take the deduction or credit o for the taxable period in which it was actually "paid or incurred," or "p or accrued," as the case may be. Upon the audit of the return, the Co missioner will decide whether the case is within the exception provided the Internal Revenue Code, and the taxpayer will be advised as to the per for which the deduction or credit is properly allowable. (b) The provisions of paragraph (a) of this section in general are applicable with respect to the taxable period during which the taxpayer d In such case there shall also be allowed as deductions and credits for su taxable period amounts [except deductions under section 23 (o)] accr up to the date of his death if not otherwise allowable with respect to su period or a prior period, regardless of the fact that the decedent was requi to keep his books and make his returns on the basis of cash receipts a disbursements.

When according to Treasury Regulations should deductions be made credits for dependency be taken, etc.?

Each year's return, so far as practicable, both as to gross income and ductions therefrom, should be complete in itself, and taxpayers are expec

make every reasonable effort to ascertain the facts necessary to make a rrect return. The expenses, liabilities, or deficit of one year can not be ed to reduce the income of a subsequent year. A taxpayer has the right to duct all authorized allowances, and it follows that if he does not within y year deduct certain of his expenses, losses, interest, taxes, or other charges, cannot deduct them from the income of the next or any succeeding year. is recognized, however, that particularly in a going business of any magnide there are certain overlapping items both of income and deduction, and long as these overlapping items do not materially distort the income they y be included in the year in which the taxpayer, pursuant to a consistent licy, takes them into his accounts. Judgments or other binding adjudicans, such as decisions of referees and boards of review under workmen's mpensation laws, on account of damages for patent infringement, personal uries, or other cause, are deductible from gross income when the claim is adjudicated or paid, unless taken under other methods of accounting which arly reflect the correct deduction, less any amount of such damages as y have been compensated for by insurance or otherwise. If subsequent to occurrence, however, a taxpayer first ascertains the amount of a loss susned during a prior taxable year which has not been deducted from gross come, he may render an amended return for such preceding taxable year luding such amount of loss in the deductions from gross income and may è a claim for refund of the excess tax paid by reason of the failure to deduct ch loss in the original return. (See section 322.) A loss from theft or emzzlement occurring in one year and discovered in another is ordinarily ductible for the year in which sustained. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.43-2, page 194, 1940.

If a taxpayer dies during the year, and a return is made for the period he s alive, if a dividend had been declared by a stock company or bank before died but was not paid until after he died, would it have to be included in return?

Section 42 of the Revenue Act of 1934 provides that, when a taxpayer s, the return of income for the period in which the death occurred shall lude amounts accrued up to the date of his death. Under the law of Texas, leclaration of dividends creates a debt owed by the corporation in favor each stockholder which cannot be rescinded. Although the declaration of s dividend provided that the sums thereunder were payable to the stockders of record at such times and in such installments during the year as e directors saw fit, the liability of the company accrued as of the date of declaration, which was prior to the taxpayer's death. He did not alienate right to the distribution, and the remaining dividends unpaid at the time his death were paid to his executrix. 

A dividend ordinarily means any distribution made by a corporation to stockholders out of its earnings or profits accumulated after February 28, 13, and it is presumed to be earned when it is declared. The stockholder ceives the value of a dividend when it is declared, for if it is immediately d he has his stock and his dividend, and if payment is deferred he has e value of his stock increased by the amount of the dividend upon it.

Section 42, supra, was intended to reach items accrued before, but payal after, the taxpayer's death. We think the decedent's entire interest in t dividend declared April 30, 1934, was net income accrued to him at the ti of his death, and that the same was properly included in his 1934 return. 

Holmes, J., Com. v. Cohen, 121 F. 2d 348, 1941.

Although prior to his death respondent kept his accounts on the ca receipts and disbursements basis, his income for the period from January 1 to the date of his death, October 31st, must be determined on the so-call accrual basis. 

Dobie, J., Helvering v. McGlue's Estate, 119 F. 2d 167, 1941.

(a) BY TAXPAYER.-Every person liable to any tax imposed by this ti or for the collection thereof, shall keep such records, render under oath su statements, make such returns, and comply with such rules and regulatio as the Commissioner, with the approval of the Secretary, may from time time prescribe. 

(b) TO DETERMINE LIABILITY TO TAX.-Whenever in the judgment of 

Commissioner necessary he may require any person, by notice served up him, to make a return, render under oath such statements, or keep such r ords, as the Commissioner deems sufficient to show whether or not su person is liable to tax under this title. 

(c) INFORMATION AT THE SOURCE. For requirement of statements, and turns by one person to assist in determining the tax liability of another p son, see sections 147 to 150. (d) COPIES OF RETURNS.-If any person, required by law or regulati made pursuant to law to file a copy of any income return for any taxal year, fails to file such copy at the time required, there shall be due and assess against such person $5 in the case of an individual return or $10 in the c of a fiduciary, partnership, or corporation return, and the collector w whom the return is filed shall prepare such copy. Such amount shall be c lected and paid, without interest, in the same manner as the amount of due in excess of that shown by the taxpayer upon a return in the case of mathematical error appearing on the face of the return. Copies of retu filed or prepared pursuant to this subsection shall remain on file for a peri of not less than two years from the date they are required to be filed, a may be destroyed at any time thereafter under the direction of the Co missioner.

Internal Revenue Code, Sec. 54.

If a taxpayer desires to change his method of accounting what should he do?

change. Permission to change the method of accounting will not be grant unless the taxpayer and the Commissioner agree to the terms and conditio under which the change will be effected. See section 22 (d) and regulatio thereunder with respect to changing to optional method of inventory goods.

Section 44 contains special provisions for reporting the profit deriv from the sale of property on the installment plan. 

The foregoing requirements relative to a change of accounting meth are not applicable if a taxpayer desires to adopt the installment basis of turning income, but are applicable if a taxpayer desires to change from su basis to a straight accrual basis. If the Tax Commissioner requires an inventory how may it be made? 

(c) INVENTORIES.-Whenever in the opinion of the Commissioner the of inventories is necessary in order clearly to determine the income of a taxpayer, inventories shall be taken by such taxpayer upon such basis as Commissioner, with the approval of the Secretary, may prescribe as confor ing as nearly as may be to the best accounting practice in the trade or busin and as most clearly reflecting the income. 

(d) [METHOD USED IN INVENTORYING GOODS.]— 

(1) A taxpayer may use the following method [whether or not su method has been prescribed under subsection (c)] in inventorying go specified in the application required under paragraph (2): (A) Inventory them at cost; (B) Treat those remaining on hand at the close of the taxable y as being: First, those included in the opening inventory of the t able year (in the order of acquisition) to the extent thereof, second, those acquired in the taxable year; and (C) Treat those included in the opening inventory of the taxa year in which such method is first used as having been acquired at same time and determine their cost by the average cost method. during the first taxable year for which the method described in paragraph (1) is to be used. (4) In determining income for the taxable year preceding the taxable year for which such method is first used, the closing inventory of such preceding year of the goods specified in such application shall be at cost. (5) If a taxpayer, having complied with paragraph (2), uses the method described in paragraph (1) for any taxable year, then such method shall be used in all subsequent taxable years unless- (A) With the approval of the Commissioner a change to a different method is authorized; or (B) The Commissioner determines that the taxpayer has used for any period beginning with or during any subsequent taxable year some procedure other than that specified in subparagraph (B) of paragraph (1) in inventorying (for ascertaining income, profit, or loss, for credit purposes, or for the purpose of reports to shareholders, partners, or other proprietors, or to beneficiaries) the goods specified in the application, and requires a change to a method different from that prescribed in paragraph (1) beginning with such subsequent taxable year or any taxable year thereafter. either of the above cases, the change to, and the use of the different ethod shall be in accordance with such regulations as the Commissioner, th the approval of the Secretary, may prescribe as necessary in order that e use of such method may clearly reflect income. [Subsection (d) as ended by Sec. 219 (a), 1939 Act.]

Internal Revenue Code, Section 22 (c) and (d). 

If a person keeps no books or records, how may the income be determined? 

The commissioner and the board determine his gross income by ascerning the annual increase in his net worth for each year and adding thereto sonal expenditures not reflected in the increase. The courts have approved at method of ascertaining income. Gleckman v. U. S., 80 F. 2d 394. 

Since he kept no books or records from which his current income for y of the years involved could be ascertained, the commissioner had the ht to look elsewhere for evidence. His determination is prima facie cort, and the burden of providing that the correct income was in an amount s than that on which the deficiency had been computed was upon the payer. 

Holmes, J., Richards v. C. of I. R., 111 F. 2d 376, 1940.

c. 401. RULES FOR APPLICATION OF SECTION 400. 

"For the purposes of this Supplement- 

"(a) DEFINITIONS 

"(1) 'Married person' means a married person living with husband or wife. "(2) 'Dependent' means a person (other than husband or wife) dependent upon and receiving his chief support from the taxpayer if such dependent person is under eighteen years of age or is incapable of selfsupport because mentally or physically defective, excluding as a dependent, in the case of a head of a family, one who would be excluded under section 25 (b) (2) (B). 

"(b) DETERMINATION OF STATUS.-The determination of whether a per is living with husband or wife, is a head of a family, or is a dependent, I be made as of the last day of the taxpayer's taxable year. 

"(c) SEPARATE RETURN OF HUSBAND AND WIFE.-If a husband and wife ng together file separate returns, each shall be treated as a single person.

"(d) MARRIED PERSONS NOT LIVING WITH HUSBAND OF WIFE.-A mar person not a head of a family and not living with husband or wife shall reated as a single person.

"The election referred to in section 400 shall be considered to have been le if the taxpayer files the return prescribed for this supplement and such tion shall be irrevocable. If the taxpayer for any taxable year has filed a um computing his tax without regard to this supplement, he may not eafter elect for such year to compute his tax under this supplement.

What is the meaning of gross income according to section 22 of the enue Act? (a) GENERAL DEFINITION. "Gross income" includes gains, profits, and me derived from salaries, wages, or compensation for personal service, of tever kind and in whatever form paid, or from professions, vocations, es, businesses, commerce, or sales, or dealings in property, whether real personal, growing out of the ownership or use of or interest in such perty; also from interest, rent, dividends, securities, or the transaction of business carried on for gain or profit, or gains or profits and income ded from any source whatever. In the case of Presidents of the United es and judges of courts of the United States taking office after June 6, 2, the compensation received as such shall be included in gross income; and 

Acts fixing the compensation, of such Presidents and judges are hereby nded accordingly.

This section was amended in 1939, by inserting after the words "comsation for personal service" the following: "including personal service n officer or employee of a State, or any political subdivision thereof, or agency or instrumentality of any one or more of the foregoing." 

Internal Revenue Code, Sec. 22. 

What is the difference in effect between a tax on gross and on net income? 

The difference in effect between a tax measured by gross receipts and measured by net income, recognized by our decisions, is manifest and [ 29 ]

substantial, and it affords a convenient and workable basis of distinction tween a direct and immediate burden upon the business affected and a ch that is only indirect and incidental. A tax upon gross receipts affects transaction in proportion to its magnitude and irrespective of whether profitable or otherwise. Conceivably it may be sufficient to make the ference between profit and loss, or to so dimnish the profit as to imped discourage the conduct of the commerce. A tax upon the net profits has the same deterrent effect, since it does not arise at all unless a gain is sh over and above expenses and losses, and the tax cannot be heavy unless profits are large. Such a tax, when imposed upon net incomes from whate source arising, is but a method of distributing the cost of government, a tax upon property, or upon franchises treated as property; and if ther no discrimination against interstate commerce, either in the admeasure of the tax or in the means adopted for enforcing it, it constitutes one of ordinary and general burdens of government, from which persons corporations otherwise subject to the jurisdiction of the States are exempted by the Federal Constitution because they happen to be eng in commerce among the States. 

Pitney, J., United States Glue Co. v. Oak Creek, 247 U.S. 

321, 1918.

What is included in gross income according to Treasury Regulations 

103 of 1940?

Gross income includes in general compensation for personal and fessional services, business income, profits from sales of and dealing property, interest, rent, dividends, and gains, profits, and income de from any source whatever, unless exempt from tax by law. [See sec 

22(b) and 116.] In general, income is the gain derived from capital, labor, or from both combined, provided it be understood to include p gained through a sale or conversion of capital assets. Profits of citi residents, or domestic corporations derived from sales in foreign comm must be included in their gross income; but special provisions are mad nonresident aliens and foreign corporations by sections 211 to 238 clusive, and, in certain cases, by section 251, for citizens and dom corporations deriving income from sources within possessions of the U States. Income may be in the form of cash or of property. 

If property is transferred by a corporation to a shareholder, or b employer to an employee, for an amount substantially less than its market value, regardless of whether the transfer is in the guise of a sa exchange, such shareholder or employee shall include in gross income difference between the amount paid for the property and the amount fair market value to the extent that such difference is in the nature of compensation for services rendered or to be rendered or (2) a distribu of earnings or profits taxable as a dividend, as the case may be. In compu the gain or loss from the subsequent sale of such property its basis sha the amount paid for the property, increased by the amount of such differ included in gross income. This paragraph does not apply, however, to issuance by a corporation to its shareholders of the right to subscribe t stock, as to which see section 19.22(a)-8.

How is compensation paid in other than cash considered in Treasury ulations?

If services are paid for with something other than money, the fair market e of the thing taken in payment is the amount to be included as income. e services were rendered at a stipulated price, in the absence of evidence ne contrary such price will be presumed to be the fair value of the comsation received. If a corporation transfers to its employees its own stock ompensation for services rendered by the employee, the amount of such pensation to be included in the gross income of the employee is the fair ket value of the stock at the time of the transfer. If living quarters such amps are furnished to employees for the convenience of the employer, ratable value need not be added to the cash compensation of the emees, but if a person receives as compensation for services rendered a y and in addition thereto living quarters, the value to such person of the ters furnished constitutes income subject to tax. How is compensation paid in notes considered in returning gross in by Treasury Regulations No. 103?

Notes or other evidences of indebtedness received in payment for ices constitute income to the amount of their fair market value. A payer receiving as compensation a note regarded as good for its face at maturity, but not bearing interest, shall treat as income as of the of receipt the fair discounted value of the note at such time. Thus, appears that such a note is or could be discounted on a 6 percent basis recipient shall include such note in his gross income to the amount of its value less discount computed at the prevailing rate for such transaction the payments due on a note so accounted for are met as they become there should be included as income in respect of each such payment so thereof as represents recovery for the discount originally deducted. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.22 (a)-4, page 

32, 1940. 

What is meant by gross income from business or trade?

In the case of a manufacturing, merchandising, or mining business, “ income" means the total sales, less the cost of goods sold, plus any ind from investments and from incidental or outside operations or sources determining the gross income subtractions should not be made for dep ation depletion, selling expenses, or losses, or for items not ordinarily in computing the cost of goods sold.

33, 1940.

How is the exemption of the proceeds of life insurance policies consi by the Treasury Regulations? 

[(b) EXCLUSIONS FROM GROSS INCOME.-The following items shal be included in gross income and shall be exempt from taxation under title:] (1) LIFE INSURANCE.-Amounts received under a life insurance tract paid by reason of the death of the insured, whether in a single or otherwise (but if such amounts are held by the insurer under an a ment to pay interest thereon, the interest payments shall be includ gross income);

ART. 22(b) (1)-1. Life insurance-Amounts paid by reason of the of the insured. The proceeds of life insurance policies, paid by reas the death of an insured to his estate or to any beneficiary (individual, par ship, or corporation, but not a transferee for valuable consideration), dir [34]

EXEMPTIONS FROM GROSS INCOME 

35 in trust, are excluded from the gross income of the beneficiary. While it immaterial whether the proceeds of a life insurance policy payable upon death of the insured are paid to the beneficiary in a single sum or in in- Ilments, only the amount paid solely by reason of the death of the inred is exempted. The amount exempted is the amount payable had the ured or the beneficiary not elected to exercise an option to receive the oceeds of the policy or any part thereof at a later date or dates. If the licy provides no option for payment upon the death of the insured, or ovides only for payments in installments, there is exempted only the amount ich the insurance company would have paid immediately after the death the insured had the policy not provided for payment at a later date or ces. Any increment thereto is taxable. 

Act, Sec. 22, and Treasury Regulations No. 103, Sec. 19.22 (b) (1)-1, page 43, 1940. 

How is the portion of each distribution of life insurance that shall be inded in gross income determined?

In any mode of settlement of insurance the portion of each distribution ich is to be so included in gross income shall be determined as follows: (a) Proceeds held by the insurer. If the proceeds are held by the iner under an agreement (whether with the insured or with a beneficiary) distribute either the increment to such proceeds currently, or the proeds and increment in equal installments until both are exhausted, there shall included in gross income, the increment so paid to the beneficiary, or so edited to the fund in each year by the insurer. (b) Proceeds payable in installments for a fixed number of years.the proceeds are payable in installments for a fixed number of years, the ount that would have been payable by the insurance company immediately on the death of the insured (if payment at a later date had not been proled for) is to be divided by the total number of installments payable over fixed number of years for which payment is to be made, and the quotient presents the portion of each installment to be excluded from gross income. e amount of each installment in excess of such excluded portion is to be luded in gross income. For example, if, at the insured's death, $1,000 uld have been payable in a single installment, but 10 equal annual paynts are made in lieu thereof, the portion of the installment received during y taxable year to be excluded from gross income is $100 ($1,000 divided 10). Any amount received as an installment in excess of $100 is to be cluded in gross income. (c) Proceeds payable in installments during the life of the beneficiary.the proceeds are payable in installments during the life of the beneficiary amount of each installment that is to be included in gross income will be termined as in paragraph (b) of this section, except that the number of ars to be used in the specified computation will be determined by the life pectancy of the beneficiary, as calculated by the table of mortality used the particular insurance company in determining the amount of the nuity. (d) Proceeds payable for a fixed number of years and for continued life. 

If the proceeds are payable in installments for a fixed number of years

and for continued life, the amount of each installment that is to be includ in gross income will be determined either as provided in paragraph (b) this section if the fixed number of years for which payment is to be ma exceeds the life expectancy of the beneficiary, as calculated by the table mortality used by the particular insurance company in determining t amount of the annuity; or, as provided in paragraph (c) of this section such life expectancy exceeds the specified fixed period. 

If a mode of settlement has been in effect prior to the first taxable y which begins after December 31, 1933 (or after December 31, 1935, in t case of a mode of settlement described in paragraph (d) of this section the entire amount received and excluded from gross income in such pri years shall be deducted from the proceeds payable upon the death of t insured; the remainder shall be divided by the number of installments unpa at the beginning of such taxable year (whether over the remaining porti of the fixed period or over the life expectancy as of that date, depending the mode of settlement adopted); and that quotient shall be the excludit portion of each installment. As soon as the aggregate of the amounts ceived and excluded from gross income under the methods of computati provided for in this section equals the amount of the proceeds payable up the death of the insured, the entire amount received thereafter in each ta able year must be included in gross income. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.22 (b) (1)-1, page 44, 1940.

Will a court consider the policy of the Congress or Legislature in mak tax exemptions?

It is not the function of a court to determine whether the public pol that finds expression in legislation of this order is well or ill conceived. T judicial function is exhausted with the discovery that the relation betw means and end is not wholly vain and fanciful, an illusory pretense. Wit the field where men of reason may reasonably differ, the Legislature m have its way. Nor in marking out that field will a court be forgetful of p sumtions that help to fix the boundaries. St. 555, 1937. Where an ambiguity occurs in a grant of exemption how is it construed? 

Well-recognized rules of construction in similar cases are applicable. It is primary principle that, in any claim of immunity from taxation under legisive authority, the exemption must be plainly and unmistakably granted d cannot exist by implication only. The intention to exempt must appear irmatively. If the language used creates an ambiguity, then any doubt st be resolved against the grant. But, in arriving at a judicial interpretation the concession the duty of the court is not to determine whether a doubt y be claimed, but whether, in fact, an ambiguity exists. 

Herrick, J., People v. Lake Forest University, 10 N.E. 2d 

667, Ill. 1937. 

How are tax exemptions considered by the courts?

An exemption from taxation is in the nature of a renunciation of sovernty. It relieves one class of persons or property from its obligation to ar its share of the expenses of government, no matter how deserving of istance that class may be, and throws a correspondingly heavier burden on all other classes, thus creating an inequality of taxation. It is for this son that the courts have uniformly refused to favor exemptions, and have variably construed statutes freeing property from the burden of enforced ntribution to the expense of maintaining the government most rigidly ainst the claimant, and have declined to countenance such immunity unless language of the statute is clear and unambiguous, and unless the purpose the Legislature to exempt such property indisputably appears. 

Bd. of Education of Jamestown v. Baker, 241 A.D. 574, quoted by Edgcomb, J., in Herkimer County v. Kerkimer, 295 N.Y.S. 629, 1937.

In enacting provisions for income tax deductions, Congress is only int ested in determining what part of a company's [or person's] gross income believes should be treated as net income for the purposes of income taxati 

McCoach v. Insurance Co. of North America, 1917, 244 U.S. 585, 37 S.

842. To determine this net income, Congress has provided for deductions ordinary and necessary business expenses, interest paid on indebtedness, ta paid, certain kinds of losses, depreciation, depletion, and others not he relevant. It is worthy of note that the basis of these deductions is involunta payment, with the possible exception of a bad debt deduction. Necessa business expenses, interest, taxes, depreciation, depletion and most losses not voluntary expenditures or decreases in value. In one sense a bad de is not a voluntary diminution of income, for the debt was contracted w the expectation that it would be repaid. 

The losses intended to be deductible are, in general, physical losses fire, flood, or other like causes which are not covered by insurance. A expenses to be deductible must have a reasonable relation to the business. 

Mahoney, J., W. F. Young, Inc. v. Com., 120 F. 2d 159, 1941.

May the right to claim deductions from gross income be discontinued any time?

The authority of Congress to discontinue the right to such deduction not open to doubt. Congress has unquestioned power to condition, limit deny deductions from gross income in arriving at the net which is to taxed. Helvering v. Independent Life Ins. Co., 292 U.S. 371. And deductio from gross income are granted only by statute.

The right to a deducti [ 40 ]

What must a taxpayer be able to show in order to claim a deduction from gross income?
