What is meant by "business" as used in tax laws?

In Flint v. Stone Tracy Co., 220 U.S. 107, in passing on a case, the court ined the word "business" as being a very comprehensive term embracing rything about which a person can be employed and as that which ocpies the time, attention and labor of men for the purpose of a livelihood or fit. 

Simons, J., Kales v. Com. of I. R., 101 F. 2d 35, 1939.

What is the meaning of business according to Justice Hamilton of the cuit Court of Appeals?

Business itself is a word of large and indefinite import. It is that which cupies the time, attention and labor of man for profit. The word "busis" in its present connection connotes a commercial or industrial establishnt or enterprise. The distinction between it and "property" must be arly kept in mind in applying the taxing statute here. Business in the sense which it is here used means the activity, the energy, the capacity, the optunities by which results are reached, the doing of the varied commercial

acts and taking of the requisite steps from which result conclusions a conditions.

The mere receipt of income from leased property and its distribution cestue que trusts amount to no more than receiving the ordinary fruits th arise from the ownership of property and does not constitute doing busine 

Hamilton, J., Cleveland Trust Co. v. C. I. P., 115 F. 2d 

481, 1940.

What may be said to be extremes between doing business and other metho of earning a livelihood?

A consideration of the cases suggests extremes in meaning as follow On the one hand, persons, associations and corporations engaged in man facturing, trade and commerce for profit or gain, are beyond question gaged in business. On the other hand, the performance of essential gover mental functions is not business within the meaning of statutes such as th here involved. Persons or agencies so occupied are not engaged in busin but instead are invested with a portion of the sovereign power which th exercise for the public benefit. Between these two extremes it is difficult find universally applicable principles. . . . Under particular statutes it been held that an occupation, in order to constitute a business, must pursued for livelihood or profit, or that it shall be of a nature specified in t statute. ...

Webster's Dictionary defines "business" as "any particular occupation employment habitually engaged in, especially for livelihood or gain." In broadest meaning, therefore, one could be said to be engaged in business if were his habit to rummage through garbage cans in search of food, af the manner of the human derelicts who may be seen thus engaged on t back streets of our large cities. However, rarely do we find the term e ployed so broadly in statutes or cases, although the language of a Ter statute is not far from it, i.e., "any transaction with persons or any tra actions concerning any property situated in this state through any agen whatever." 

Miller, J., Hazen v. National R. A. of A., 101 F. 2d 432, 1939.

Is managing the investments of a trust estate, speculating in stocks, cutti coupons on bonds, considered as "carrying on a trade or business"?

Do such activities constitute carrying on of a business? It seems now be clear that the investing and reinvesting of assets, either by an individ for himself or by trustees on behalf of a trust estate, is pretty definitely termined not to constitute the carrying on of business. Thus Judge Learn 

Hand says in Bedell v. Commissioner, 2 Cir., 1929, 30 F. 2d 622, 624: “M men who have capital change their investments, and may speculate all time; we should hardly call this a business, though the line is undoubted hard to draw." Judge Patterson in the same Circuit in Higgins v. Comm sioner, 2 Cir., 1940, 111 F. 2d 795, 796, says: "By the common speech of m a person who does nothing beyond looking after his own investments a

ceiving the income from them is not conducting a trade or business." The me rule has been applied to the investing and reinvesting of a trustee on half of an estate. In City Bank Farmers Trust Co. v. Commissioner, 2 Cir., 40, 112 F. 2d 457, 458, it appeared that the duties of the trustee in a given ust consisted generally in causing its investment company to review each car its securities comprising the corpus; selling securities and reinvesting the oceeds in other stocks and bonds; collecting interest and dividends; paying penses of the trust; distributing income to beneficiaries; keeping books of count; rendering statements to the interested parties for preparing and ing income tax returns. The court said: "We hold that in caring for the ust estate the trustee was like an individual engaged in investing his funds stocks and bonds and that such a person cannot be regarded as engaged business and, therefore, is not entitled to be allowed any deduction for penses incurred in investing and supervising." To the same effect, see the ear and helpful discussion by the court in Miller v. Commissioner, 9 Cir., 39, 102 F. 2d 476 (also a trustee case) and Kane v. Commissioner, 2 Cir., 38, 100 F. 2d 382. [Holding it does not constitute "carrying on any trade business."] 

Goodrich, J., White's Will v. Com., 119 F. 2d 619, 1941.

Are expenses incurred by a taxpayer in managing his own securities or instments deductible?

Since the first income tax act, the provisions authorizing business dections have varied only slightly. The Revenue Act of 1913 allowed as a duction "the necessary expenses actually paid in carrying on any business." 

1918 the present form was fixed and has so continued. No regulation has er been promulgated which interprets the meaning of "carrying on a siness," nor any rulings approved by the Secretary of the Treasury, i.e. easury Decisions. Certain rulings of less dignity, favorable to petitioner ho had extensive investments in real estate, bonds and stocks, devoted a nsiderable portion of his time to the oversight of his interests and hired hers to assist him in offices rented for that purpose), appeared in individual ses but they are not determinative. Even acquiescence in some Board ruls after defeat does not amount to settled administrative practice. Unless è administrative practice is long continued and substantially uniform in Bureau and without challenge by the Government in the Board and urts, it should not be assumed, from rulings of this class, that Congressional nactment of the language which they construed was an adoption of their erpretation.

While the Commissioner has combated views similar to petitioner's in the urts, sometimes successfully and sometimes unsuccessfully, the petitioner ges that the Bureau accepted for years the doctrine that the management one's own securities might be a business where there was sufficient extent, ntinuity, variety and regularity. We fail to find such a fixed administrae construction in the examples cited. It is true that the decisions are freently put on the ground that the taxpayer's activities were sporadic but it es not follow that had those activities been continuous the Commissioner uld not have used the argument advanced here, i.e., that no amount of

personal investment management would turn those activities into a busines Evidently such was the Government's contention in the Kales case, 101 F.2 

35, where the things the taxpayer did met petitioner's tests, and in Foss 

Commissioner, 75 F. 2d 236, and Washburn v. Commissioner, 51 F. 2d 94 where the opinions turned on the extent of the taxpayer's participation the management of the corporations in which investments were held. 

Petitioner relies strongly on the definition of business in Flint v. Sto 

Tracy Company, 220 U.S. 107; " 'Business' is a very comprehensive term a embraces everything about which a person can be employed." This de nition was given in considering whether certain corporations came und the Corporation Tax law which levies a tax on corporations engaged in bus ness. The immediate issue was whether corporations engaged principally the "holding and management of real estate" were subject to the act. A def nition given for such an issue is not controlling in this dissimilar inquiry. 

To determine whether the activities of a taxpayer are "carrying on business" requires an examination of the facts in each case. As the Circu 

Court of Appeals observed, all expenses of every business transaction are n deductible. The Bureau of Internal Revenue has this duty of determini what is carrying on a business, subject to reexamination of the facts by th Board of Tax Appeals and untimately to review on the law by the cour on which jurisdiction is conferred. The Commissioner and the Board a praised the evidence here as insufficient to establish petitioner's activities those of carrying on a business. The petitioner merely kept records an collected interest and dividends from his securities, through managerial a tention for his investments. No matter how large the estate or how co tinuous or extended the work required may be, such facts are not sufficie as a matter of law to permit the courts to reverse the decision of the Boar Its conclusion is adequately supported by this record, and rests upon a con ception of carrying on business similar to that expressed by this Court for antecedent section. See Van Wert v. Commissioner, 295 U.S. 112.

The petitioner makes the point that his activities in managing his estat both realty and personalty, were a unified business. Since it was admittedly business in so far as the realty is concerned, he urges, there is no statutor authority to sever expenses allocable to the securities. But we see no reaso why expenses not attributable, as we have just held these are not, to carryin on business cannot be apportioned. It is not unusual to allocate expenses pa for services partly personal and partly business. 

Reed, J., Higgins v. Commissioner, 312 U.S. 212, 1941.

Is an executor carrying on a testator's business and employing attorney t assist him, carrying on a business for which he is entitled to deduct expense We recently stated in Higgins v. Commissioner, 312 U.S. 212, (see above that determination of what amounts to carrying on business requires exam nation of the facts in each case. In this case, the record before us contai the findings of fact of the Court of Claims. . . . The court found as a fa that the decedent, prior to his death "was engaged in business as a financi and investor, maintaining an office where he employed an office manager an an average of six clerks.... In general, the operations of the estate continue

66 substantially the same manner after the decedent's death as before. . . ." addition the court found that the attorney employed by the executors "was lled upon to advise them with reference to matters both legal and economic at arose in the business activities of the estate, with reference to federal ate and state inheritance taxes, and also in regard to the acquisition and dissal of the estate's securities and in regard to various matters pertaining to mpanies in which the estate held investments." But the executors might all the things that the court found that they did and still not be engaged 'carrying on... business" within the meaning of the Revenue Act. For we said in the Higgins case, "All expenses of every business transaction not deductible. Only those are deductible which relate to carrying on usiness." Also, we there sustained a holding that an individual who was gaged in financial and investing activities in all ways similar to those of e decedent and his executors in this case were not entitled to a deduction ch as that sought by respondents. There, the finding of the Court of Claims at the executors continued to conserve the decedent's estate as he had when was himself "a financier and investor" falls far short of a finding that the cutors were entitled to a deduction accorded by Congress only to those arrying on... business." Failure of the Court of Claims to make a specific ding on this ultimate and determinative issue deprives that court's judgnt of support. Under such circumstances we are not called upon to weigh different facts set out in the subsidiary findings in order to determine ether or not they would support a conclusion that the executors were arrying on... business" within the meaning of the statute.

When we turn to the opinion of the Court of Claims, it is made clear that ence of such a specific finding was the result of the court's adoption of teria of "carrying on... business" inconsistent with our holding in the ggins case. Since the judgment must be vacated because not supported by quate findings, it is appropriate that we point out this inconsistency. cepting as true the statement of the Court of Claims that a broad definition "business" might be that it is "whatever engages the time, attention, and or of men in order to conserve what they have or to avoid loss" it does follow at all that this is synonymous with the statutory language, "carryon... business." This definition of "business" stems in part from the e of Flint v. Stone Tracy Co., 220 U.S. 107, upon which the Court of ims relied. But however applicable that definition may have been to the e there under consideration, it cannot be accepted as a guide in the sent case. The reasons why it is not applicable to the statutory provision v under consideration were given in our opinion in the Higgins case; its -applicability to specific situations has also been explained in a number other opinions of this Court.

Nor can the judgment of the Court of Claims be supported by that art's statement that the executors were engaged "in the business of conving the estate and protecting its income." Such activities are the traonal duty of executors. Executors who engage actively in trade and busis are the exception and not the rule. Rather obviously, there could be r cases where executors "carry on... business" by continuing to operate ore, a factory or some other well known, well marked type of business vity. But in the absence of evidence showing activities coming within general acceptation of the concept of carrying on a trade or business, it

cannot be said as a matter of law that an executor comes into this catego merely because he conserves the estate by marshalling and gathering t assets as a mere conduit for ultimate distribution. And determination of wh constitutes "carrying on business" under the Revenue Act does n depend upon the size of the estate or the number of people whose servic are required in order properly to conserve it. • • 

Black, J., United States v. Pyne, 313 U.S. 127, 1941..

How are the expenses of carrying on a trade or business which are deductib described in the Revenue Act and by Treasury Regulation? 

Business expenses deductible from gross income include the ordinary a necessary expenditures directly connected with or pertaining to the ta payer's trade or business, except the classes of items which are deductib under the provisions of articles 23 (b) to 23 (s). Double deductions are n permitted. Amounts deducted under one provision of the Act can not aga be deducted under any other provision of the Act. The cost of goo purchased for resale, with proper adjustment for opening and closing i ventories, is deducted from gross sales in computing gross income. [S article 19.22 (a)-5.] Among the items included in business expenses are ma agement expenses, commissions, labor, supplies, incidental repairs, operati expenses of automobiles used in the trade or business, traveling expenses wh away from home solely in the pursuit of a trade or business [see arti 19.23 (a)-2], advertising and other selling expenses, together with insuran premiums against fire, storm, theft, accident, or other similar losses in the ca of a business, and rental for the use of business property. Penalty payme with respect to Federal taxes, whether on account of negligence, delinquen or fraud, are not deductible from gross income. The full amount of the lowable deduction for ordinary and necessary expenses in carrying on business is nevertheless deductible, even though such expenses exceed t gross income derived during the taxable year from such business. As to ite not deductible under any provision of section 23, see section 24. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.23 (a)-1, page 

68, 1940.

What is meant by "ordinary" expenses “in carrying on any trade or busines as used in section 23 (a)?

Ordinary has the connotation of normal, usual, or customary. To sure, an expense may be ordinary though it happen but once in the t payer's life. Yet the transaction which gives rise to it must be of comm or frequent occurrence in the type of business involved. Hence, the f that a particular expense would be an ordinary or common one in course of one business and so deductible under section 23(a) does not nec sarily make it such in connection with another business. One of extremely relevant circumstances is the nature and scope of the particu business out of which the expense in question accrued. The fact that an ol gation to pay has arisen is not sufficient. It is the kind of transaction out

hich the obligation arose and its normalcy in the particular business which re crucial and controlling. 

Deputy v. Du Pont, 308 U.S. 488, quoted by Garrecht, J., in A. Giurlani & Bros. v. Com., 119 F. 2d 852, 1941.

What is the significance of the word "ordinary" in the phrase "ordinary and ecessary expenses" as used in section 23 (a)?

Discussing the significance of the word "ordinary" in the statute, the upreme Court said in Welch v. Helvering, supra, 290 U.S. 113: ... .. Now, what is ordinary, though there must always be a strain of onstancy within it, is none the less a variable affected by time and place and rcumstance. Ordinary in this context does not mean that the payments ust be habitual or normal in the sense that the same taxpayer will have to ake them often. A lawsuit affecting the safety of a business may happen ce in a lifetime. The counsel fees may be so heavy that repetition is unely. None the less, the expense is an ordinary one because we know from perience that payments for such a purpose, whether the amount is large or all, are the common and accepted means of defense against attack. [Case ed.] The situation is unique in the life of the individual affected, but not in e life of the group, the community, of which he is a part. At such times ere are norms of conduct that help to stabilize our judgment, and make it rtain and objective. The instance is not erratic, but is brought within a own type. "Men do at times pay the debts of others without legal obligation or the hter obligation imposed by the usages of trade or by neighborly amenities, at they do not do so ordinarily, not even though the result might be to ighten their reputation for generosity and opulence. Indeed, if language to be read in its natural and common meaning. . .,we should have to say at payment in such circumstances, instead of being ordinary is in a high gree extraordinary. There is nothing ordinary in the stimulus evoking it, d none in the response. Here, indeed, as so often in other branches of the v, the decisive distinctions are those of degree and not of kind. One struges in vain for any verbal formula that will supply a ready touchstone. The ndard set up by the staute is not a rule of law; it is rather a way of life. fe in all its fullness must supply the answer to the riddle." 

Garrecht, J., A. Giurlani & Bros. v. Com., 119 F. 2d 852, 1941.

How are ordinary and necessary business expenses usually determined? 

Here, indeed, as so often in other branches of the law, the decisive disctions are those of degree and not of kind. One struggles in vain for any bal formula that will supply a ready touchstone. The standard set up the statute is not a rule of law; it is rather a way of life. Life in all its 

Iness must supply the answer to the riddle.

Unless we can say from facts within our knowledge that these are ordiy and necessary expenses according to the ways of conduct and the forms speech prevailing in the business world, the tax must be confirmed. 

Welch v. Helvering, 290 U.S. 111, quoted by Sanborn, J., in U. S. v. Jaffray, 97 F. 2d 488, 1938.

If a celebrated actor had to have a particular set of false teeth made so would not hiss when he pronounced certain words may he deduct the cost a business expense from his gross income? 

We conclude, as the Board did, that the $3,500 paid for the teeth was an expense incurred in carrying on a trade or business, within the meaning Sec. 23(a) of the Revenue Act of 1934, but was a personal expense, with the meaning of Sec. 24(a) (1), and that, consequently, no part thereof deductible in computing petitioner's net income. 

The Board's opinion states: "The statute (Sec. 24) expressly disallo 

'personal' expenses. It would be difficult to imagine anything more perso than a set of false teeth. Although the immediate suggestion that the purch of the new upper dentures be made may have occurred, in connection w (his) professional activity, the expenditure was nevertheless so purely p sonal in character as to deny it classification as business expense.' 

Sparkman v. C. of I. R., 112 F. 2d 774, 1940. 

Inasmuch as all income from illegal sources is taxable, may deductions allowed for expenses in illegal undertakings or business?

The writer [Justice Clark] finds no lack of logic in taxing income fr illegal sources, and refusing a deduction for the same type of expens After all, the word applicable to one face of the medal income, is the broad possible, whereas the words applicable to the other have the many limitatio the writer has tried to indicate. Further, the medal as a whole embod policies and a probable Congressional intent which must have consistent a plication. Any discouragement of ill-gotten gains entails a correspondi lack of sympathy with the means of getting them. Thus everything the ras takes in must be taxed, and as little as possible of what he expends must exempted. 

Clark, J., Com. v. Textile Mills S. Corp., 117 F. 2d 62, 1941.

May a deduction be made for lobbying legislative bodies as a busin expense?

The conclusion, that money spent for lobbying purposes is not ductible as an ordinary and necessary business expense within the intent the Revenue Acts is further confirmed by the implication to be derived fro recurrent Treasury Regulations denying to corporations the right to dedu such expenses as donations for business purposes. Thus, Art. 22 of Reg lations No. 74, promulgated under Section 23 (m) of the Revenue Act 

1928 provides in part, that "Sums of money expended for lobbying purpos the promotion of propaganda, including advertising other than trade a vertising, and contributions for campaign expenses are not deductible fro gross income.” 

Biggs, J., Com. v. Textile Mills S. Corp., 117 F. 2d 62, 1941.

What deductions may a taxpayer make for traveling expenses on busin trips, according to Treasury Regulations? 

Traveling expenses, as ordinarily understood, include railroad fares a meals and lodging. If the trip is undertaken for other than business purpos

railroad fares are personal expenses and the meals and lodging are living penses. If the trip is solely on business, the reasonable and necessary travelexpenses, including railroad fares, meals, and lodging, are business exses. (a) If, then, an individual, whose business requires him to travel, receives alary as full compensation for his services, without reimbursement for veling expenses, or is employed on a commission basis with no expense alvance, his traveling expenses, including the entire amount expended for als and lodging, are deductible from gross income. (b) If an individual receives a salary and is also repaid his actual traveling penses, he shall include in gross income the amount so repaid and may duct such expenses. (c) If an individual receives a salary and also an allowance for meals lodging, as, for example, a per diem allowance in lieu of subsistence, the ount of the allowance should be included in gross income and the cost of h meals and lodging may be deducted therefrom. 

A payment for the use of a sample room at a hotel for the display of goods business expense. Only such expenses as are reasonable and necessary he conduct of the business and directly attributable to it may be deducted. taxpayer claiming the benefit of the deductions referred to herein must ach to his return a statement showing (1) the nature of the business in ich engaged; (2) the number of days away from home during the taxable r on account of business; (3) the total amount of expenses incident to als and lodging while absent from home on business during the taxable r; and (4) the total amount of other expenses incident to travel and med as a deduction.

Claims for the deductions referred to must be substantiated, when rered by the Commissioner, by evidence showing in detail the amount and ure of the expenses incurred.

Commuters' fares are not considered as business expenses and are not luctible. 

Treasury Regulations, No. 103, Sec. 19.23 (a)-2, page 69, 1940.

What deductions may a taxpayer make for cost of materials and supplies, ording to Treasury Regulations?

Taxpayers carrying materials and supplies on hand should include in enses the charges for materials and supplies only to the amount that they actually consumed and used in operation during the year for which return is made, provided that the cost of such materials and supplies has been deducted in determining the net income for any previous year. If axpayer carries incidental materials or supplies on hand, for which no ord of consumption is kept or of which physical inventories at the bening and end of the year are not taken, it will be permissible for the taxer to include in his expenses and deduct from gross income the total cost such supplies and material as were purchased during the year for which return is made, provided the net income is clearly reflected by this hod. 

Treasury Regulations, No. 103, Sec. 19.23 (a)-2, page 69, 1940.

What deductions may a taxpayer make for repairs according to Treasu Regulations?

The cost of incidental repairs which neither materially add to the val of the property nor appreciably prolong its life, but keep it in an ordinari efficient operating condition, may be deducted as expense, provided t plant or property account is not increased by the amount of such expen tures. Repairs in the nature of replacements, to the extent that they arr deterioration and appreciably prolong the life of the property, should charged against the depreciation reserve if such account is kept. 

Treasury Regulation No. 103, Section 19.23 (a)-4, page 70, 1940.

What deductions may a professional man, as a doctor, dentist, lawyer, et claim according to Treasury Regulations?

A professional man may claim as deductions the cost of supplies used him in the practice of his profession, expenses paid in the operation and pair of an automobile used in making professional calls, dues to profession societies and subscriptions to professional journals, the rent paid for offi rooms, the cost of the fuel, light, water, telephone, etc., used in such offic and the hire of office assistants. Amounts currently expended for book furniture, and professional instruments and equipment, the useful life which is short, may be deducted. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.23 (a)-5, page 

70, 1940.

What deductions may be made for compensation for personal service, labor, in conducting the business or trade of the taxpayer? 

Among the ordinary and necessary expenses paid or incurred in car ing on any trade or business may be included a reasonable allowance salaries or other compensation for personal services actually rendered. T test of deductibility in the case of compensation payments is whether th are reasonable and are in fact payments purely for services. This test a its practical application may be further stated and illustrated as follows: (1) Any amount paid in the form of compensation, but not in fact the purchase price of services, is not deductible. (a) Án ostensible sala paid by a corporation may be a distribution of a dividend on stock. Thi likely to occur in the case of a corporation having few shareholders, pr tically all of whom draw salaries. If in such a case the salaries are in excess those ordinarily paid for similar services, and the excessive payments o respond or bear a close relationship to the stock holdings of the officers employees, it would seem likely that the salaries are not paid wholly services rendered, but that the excessive payments are a distribution of ea ings upon the stock. (b) An ostensible salary may be in part payment property. This may occur, for example, where a partnership sells out t corporation, the former partners agreeing to continue in the service of corporation. In such a case it may be found that the salaries of the form partners are not merely for services, but in part constitute payment for transfer of their business.

(2) The form or method of fixing compensation is not decisive as to eductibility. While any form of contingent compensation invites scrutiny a possible distribution of earnings of the enterprise, it does not follow that yments on a contingent basis are to be treated fundamentally on any basis fferent from that applying to compensation at a flat rate. Generally speakg, if contingent compensation is paid pursuant to a free bargain between e employer and the individual made before the services are rendered, not fluenced by any consideration on the part of the employer other than at of securing on fair and advantageous terms the services of the indidual, it should be allowed as a deduction even though in the actual workg out of the contract it may prove to be greater than the amount which ould ordinarily be paid. (3) In any event the allowance for the compensation paid may not exed what is reasonable under all the circumstances. It is in general just to sume that reasonable and true compensation is only such amount as would dinarily be paid for like services by like enterprises under like circumances. The circumstances to be taken into consideration are those existing the date when the contract for services was made, not those existing at the te when the contract is questioned. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.23 (a)-6, page 

70, 1940.

Are commissions a necessary expense of carrying on a business? 

It is provided by Section 23 (a) of the Revenue Act of 1934, 48 Stat. 680, 8, 26 U.S.C.A. Int. Rev. Acts, page 671, that, "In computing net income ere shall be allowed as deductions: . . . All the ordinary and necessary exnses paid or incurred during the taxable year in carrying on any trade or siness, ." This is the provision of the taxing act which has direct apication to the present situation. Our principal concern here is whether purchasing and selling commisns paid by the taxpayer, engaged in the business of buying and selling curities, are deductible as ordinary and necessary expenses, or must be ured, respectively, as additions to cost or offsets against selling price. 

Incidental to the Commissioner's authority to administer the income tax w is the power to make regulations for the information of the taxpayers, the idance of the collectors, and the realization of purposes of the taxing acts. ction 62 of certain of the later Revenue Acts (notably, 1934, 48 Stat. 680, 0, 26 U.S.C.A. Int. Rev. Code, $ 62) furnishes specific authority for the blication of such regulations and under that section was published Reguions 86, relating to the income tax under the Revenue Act of 1934. Reguions 86, referring to Section 23(a) of the 1934 Act, quoted above, says, Among the items included in business expenses are... .. commissions,. is simple, general statement, is not decisive, for this article concludes with e sentence, "As to items not deductible under any provision of section 23, e section 24," and Article 24-2 of the same Regulations provides, in part, at: "... Commissions paid in purchasing securities are part of the cost price such securities. Commissions paid in selling securities, when such comssions are not an ordinary and necessary business expense, are an offset ainst the selling price."

The qualifying clause, which we have emphasized, first appeared in Regu lations 77, article 282, covering the Revenue Act of 1932. For several year prior to the publication of Regulations 77, the statement referring to pur chasing and selling commissions appeared in the Regulations substantially a written above, without the qualifying clause. As early as Regulations 33 par. 108 (respecting the Revenue Acts of 1913 and 1916), the following definition was in effect: "Commissions paid in purchasing and selling secur ties are a part of the cost or selling price of the securities and not otherwis deductible. They do not constitute expense deductions in a return o income."

Hutton v. Commissioner, 5 Cir., 39 F. 2d 459, treated, and denied, the deductibility of purchase commissions as ordinary and necessary busines expenses under the Revenue Act of 1921, under which Regulations 62, an 293, provided as in the 1934 Act, with the exception, of course, of the qualify ing clause, not included in those Regulations. The court concluded pu chasing commissions were part of the cost of the securities and not deductibl as ordinary and necessary expenses. 

The provision of Article 23(a)-1 must be read in conjunction with th provisions of Article 24-2 of Regulations 86, applicable here, in order to giv recognition to both and do violence to neither. Undoubtedly, the claus "when such commissions are not an ordinary and necessary business ex pense," refers to that part of Article 23 (a)-1 of Regulations 86, quoted abov It will be noted, however, and it is to be stressed, that the part of Artic 24-2 to which we have reference is specific, as opposed to the general natur of the reference to "commissions" in Article 23(a)-1, and therefore shoul control. Helvering v. Winmill, 305 U.S. 79, 83, 84, 59 S.Ct. 45, 83 L.Ed. 5

An opinion was requested of the General Counsel of the Bureau Internal Revenue on, for all practical purposes, the identical question he involved, under the same section of the 1934 Act and the same Articles 

Regulations 86. The opinion (Internal Revenue Bulletin, Cum. Bull. XIV

p. 59, XIV-38-7698, G.C.M. 15430) said: "The question as to the deductibility of such commissions involves thr classes of taxpayers—(1) a dealer in securities, (2) a trader in securities, an (3) a taxpayer who purchases and sells securities occasionally but not in su quantities or under such circumstances as to constitute him either a deal or a trader. . . . ... "Fundamentally, commissions paid in connection with both purchases an sales of securities, being direct expenses of acquiring and disposing of th property, are direct charges against the profit, or increase the loss, arisin from the transaction. . 

‘. . . there can be no justification for a departure from that method treatment except in those cases where the detail of accounting on that ba is so burdensome as to make its use impracticable. No such accounting di ficulties exist in the case of any of the three classes of taxpayers enumerat above, except the dealer. Both the occasional seller and the trader are quired to account in their income tax returns for the profit or loss on ea individual sale. The right to inventory securities is denied to the trader a to the occasional seller. [See article 22(c)-5, Regulations 86.] . . .' 

The opinion then continues to explain that no difficulty is experienced either of the latter two classes of taxpayers in accounting by adding purchasi

mmissions to the cost of the securities and deducting selling commisns from the selling price thereof and that the dealer, also, experiences no ficulty in adding the purchase commissions to the cost of securities, but at the volume of his sales and the numerous selling transactions make it practicable from the accounting standpoint to match selling commissions ainst each individual sale. Because of this factor, so reasons the opinion, the aler is permitted to inventory securities and "to treat his selling commisns as ordinary and necessary business expenses,' not because they are chnically such but because of practical considerations." No formal recogion of this interpretation was given, says the opinion, until the qualifying use was inserted in Article 282 of Regulations 77. The opinion concludes th this statement: "Selling commissions are an offset against the sale price dare not deductible as ordinary and necessary business expenses, except in e case of a dealer in securities."

We are moved to comment that the interpretation of the regulations by 

Assistant General Counsel of the Bureau of Internal Revenue appears elligent, logical, and reasonable. Moreover, it is but the statement of a g-continued construction of law and regulation by the administrative icers charged with the enforcement thereof. 

Garrecht, J., Spreckels v. Com., 119 F. 2d 667, 1941. What deductions may a taxpayer claim for rentals for carrying on the iness or trade of the taxpayer?

If a leasehold is acquired for business purposes for a specified sum, the chaser may take as a deduction in his return an aliquot part of such sum h year, based on the number of years the lease has to run. Taxes paid by enant to or for a landlord for business property are additional rent and stitute a deductible item to the tenant and taxable income to the landlord, amount of the tax being deductible by the latter. The cost borne by a zee or tenant in erecting buildings or making permanent improvements on und of which he is lessee is held to be a capital investment and not detible as a business expense. In order to return to such taxpayer his intment of capital, an annual deduction may be made from gross income of amount equal to the total cost of such improvements divided by the numof years remaining of the term of lease, and such deduction shall be lieu of a deduction for depreciation. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Sec. 19.23 (a)-10, page 72, 1940.

How is a gain or loss recognized and the amount of it determined according he Internal Revenue Code? 

(a) COMPUTATION OF GAIN OR LOSS.-The gain from the sale or other dis ition of property shall be the excess of the amount realized therefrom er the adjusted basis provided in section 113(b) for determining gain, and loss shall be the excess of the adjusted basis provided in such section for ermining loss over the amount realized. 

(b) AMOUNT REALIZED.—The amount realized from the sale or other dis ition of property shall be the sum of any money received plus the fair rket value of the property (other than money) received. 

(c) RECOGNITION OF GAIN OR LOSS.-In the case of a sale or exchange, the ent to which the gain or loss determined under this section shall be recoged for the purposes of this title, shall be determined under the provisions section 112. (d) INSTALLMENT SALES.-Nothing in this section shall be construed to vent (in the case of property sold under contract providing for payment in

installments) the taxation of that portion of any installment payment rep senting gain or profit in the year in which such payment is received.

How do the Treasury Regulations interpret the provision relative to computation of gain or loss?

Except as otherwise provided, the Act regards as income or as loss S tained, the gain or loss realized from the conversion of property into ca or from the exchange of property for other property differing materia either in kind or in extent. The amount realized from a sale or other disp tion of property is the sum of any money received plus the fair market va of any property which is received. The fair market value of property question of fact, but only in rare and extraordinary cases will property considered to have no fair market value. The general method of comput such gain or loss is prescribed by section 111, which contemplates that fr the amount realized upon the sale or exchange there shall be withdraw sum sufficient to restore the adjusted basis prescribed by section 113 

19.113(b)(1)-1 to 19.113(b)(3)-2, inclusive, (i.e., the cost or other provided by section 113 (a), adjusted for receipts, expenditures, losses, allo ances, and other items chargeable against and applicable to such cost or ot basis). The amount which remains after the adjusted basis has been resto to the taxpayer constitutes the realized gain. If the amount realized upon sale or exchange is insufficient to restore to the taxpayer the adjusted b of the property, a loss is sustained in the amount of the insufficiency. 1 basis may be different depending upon whether gain or loss is being co puted.

Even though property is not sold or otherwise disposed of, gain [incl ible in gross income under section 22(a) as “gains or profits and income rived from any source whatever"] is realized if the sum of all the amou received which are required by section 113(b) to be applied against the b of the property exceeds such basis. On the other hand, a loss is not o narily sustained prior to the sale or other disposition of the property, for reason that until such sale or other disposition occurs there remains the p sibility that the taxpayer may recover or recoup the adjusted basis of property. Until some identifiable event fixes the actual sustaining of a and the amount thereof the Act takes no account of it. The provisions this paragraph may be illustrated by the following example: 

Example: A purchased certain shares of stock subsequent to February 

1913, for $10,000. .On January 1, 1936, A's adjusted basis for the stock been reduced to $1,000, by reason of receipts and distributions described section 113(b)(1) (A) and (D). He received in 1936 a further distribut of $5,000, being a distribution described in section 113(b)(1) (D). This dis bution applied against the adjusted basis as required by section 113(b)(1)( exceeds that basis by $4,000. The amount of the excess, namely, $4,000, gain realized by A in 1936 includible, as a gain from the stock, in gross inco in his return for that calendar year. In computing gain from the stock, a adjusting basis, no distinction is made between items of receipts or distri tions described in section 113(b). If A sells the stock in 1937 for $5,000,

zes in 1937 a gain of $5,000, since the adjusted basis of the stock for the pose of computing gain or loss from the sale is zero. n the case of property sold on the installment plan, special rules for the tion of the gain are prescribed in section 44. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.111-1, page 253, 1940.

What is meant by short-term and long-term capital gains and losses? 

a) DEFINITIONS.-As used in this chapter- 

1) CAPITAL ASSETS.-The term "capital assets" means property held by axpayer (whether or not connected with his trade or business), but does include stock in trade of the taxpayer or other property of a kind which ld properly be included in the inventory of the taxpayer if on hand at close of the taxable year, or property held by the taxpayer primarily for to customers in the ordinary course of his trade or business, or property, in the trade or business, of a character which is subject to the allowance depreciation provided in section 23(1); 

2) SHORT-TERM CAPITAL GAIN.-The term "short-term capital gain" 

s gain from the sale or exchange of a capital asset held for not more 18 months, if and to the extent such gain is taken into account in comng net income; 

3) SHORT-TERM CAPITAL LOSS.—The term "short-term capital loss" means from the sale or exchange of a capital asset held for not more than 18 ths, if and to the extent such loss is taken into account in computing ncome; 

4) LONG-TERM CAPITAL GAIN.-The term "long-term capital gain" means from the sale or exchange of a capital asset held for more than 18 months, d to the extent such gain is taken into account in computing net income; 

5) LONG-TERM CAPITAL LOSS.-The term "long-term capital loss" means from the sale or exchange of a capital asset held for more than 18 months, d to the extent such loss is taken into account in computing net income; 

6) NET SHORT-TERM CAPITAL GAIN.-The term "net short-term capital means the excess of short-term capital gains for the taxable year over um of (A) short-term capital losses for the taxable year, plus (B) the net

short-term capital loss of the preceding taxable year (if beginning after cember 31, 1937), to the extent brought forward to the taxable year un subsection (e); 

(7) NET SHORT-TERM CAPITAL LOSS.-The term "net short-term cap loss" means the excess of short-term capital losses for the taxable year the short-term capital gains for such year; 

(8) NET LONG-TERM CAPITAL GAIN. The term "net long-term cap gain" means the excess of long-term capital gains for the taxable year o the long-term capital losses for such year; (9) NET LONG-TERM CAPITAL LOSS.-The term "net long-term capital l means the excess of long-term capital losses for the taxable year over long-term capital gains for such year.

What is the provision for alternative tax in the case of net long-term capital s or losses?

How is the alternative tax allowed in the case of net long-term capital g or loss, and when is it allowed?

In the case of a net long-term capital gain of a taxpayer other than a c poration, section 117 (c) (1) imposes an alternative tax in lieu of the tax posed by sections 11 and 12, if and only if such alternative tax is less th the tax imposed by sections 11 and 12. This alternative tax is the sum (1) a partial tax, computed at the rates provided by sections 11 and 12, the net income of the taxpayer, excluding therefrom for this purpose amount of such net long-term capital gain, plus (2) 30 percent of the long-term capital gain. 

In the case of a net long-term capital loss of a taxpayer other than a c poration, an alternative tax is imposed in lieu of the tax imposed by secti 11and 12, if and only if such tax is greater than the tax imposed by secti 11 and 12. This alternative tax is the excess of (1) a tax, at the rates provi by sections 11 and 12, on the net income of the taxpayer, but excluding fr the computation of such net income, the amount of the net long-term cap loss over (2) 30 percent of the net long-term capital loss.

How are gains or losses from short sales considered in the Internal Rev Code?

For the purpose of this chapter- (1) Gains or losses from short sales of property shall be considere gains or losses from sales or exchanges of capital assets; and (2) Gains or losses attributable to the failure to exercise privilege options to buy or sell property shall be considered as short-term capital g or losses.

How do the courts construe the provisions allowing deductions for l The federal revenue laws have been consistent in allowing the dedu of only actual losses. To authorize deduction, the loss must be actua present. It must be determined by practical tests. And only losses sust during the year are deductible. In view of the settled policy of the re laws not to allow depreciation of non-business property as a deduction to permit deductions only of actual losses and only in the year sustained hardly reasonable to suppose that Congress intended to allow a dedu as loss for the full cost of non-business property destroyed notwithsta such property has been depreciated through use. 

Parker, J., Helvering v. Obici, 97 F. 2d 431, 1938. 

What is the meaning of "trade or business" for net losses allowed deducted from an income?

The words "trade or business," as used in the statute in connection losses, has been held by the courts to mean and refer to a regular occup or calling of the taxpayer for the purpose of livelihood or profit. 

While it has been recognized and held by the courts and by the of Tax Appeals that a person can be engaged in more than one trade or ness, and that it is not necessary that the trade or business in which duction is sought forms a taxpayer's principal trade or business, it is re that his activities shall be such that they may of themselves be regard an occupation or business. A single isolated activity or transaction sufficient to constitute a business or trade. 

Rogers v. U. S., 70 C. Cls. 159, 41 F. 2d 865, quoted by 

Williams, J., in Atkins v. U. S., 83 C. Cls. 56, 1936.

What is necessary in order to enable the taxpayer to make a deducti a loss to reduce his taxes?

Two basic facts must always be present to authorize the taxpay deduct a loss; first, the amount thereof, second, an identifiable event showing the year it occurred. Generally speaking, the income tax law i cerned only with realized gains and ascertained losses. There is an exc to this rule, however, in the case of losses which are so reasonable in a as to justify their deduction and in certain circumstances even befor are absolutely sustained.

The question whether property becomes worthless during a par year is one of fact. If substantial evidence is presented upon which the of Tax Appeals makes a factual determination, the only question presented in the review is whether the Board's findings are support substantial evidence. 

Rhodes v. Com., 100 F. 2d 966, quoted by Garrecht, J., in 

Jones v. Com., 103 F. 2d 681, 1939.

As long as assets have any value whatever, a gain or loss "results" from eir sale, their "unrealized" depreciation not being "recognized" until then. heoretically, that conception might have been extended to property whose lue had altogether disappeared, but theory has not been pushed so far. A payer suffers a "recognized" loss, if the property becomes altogether orthless as much as though it were physically destroyed. Moreover, such oss may not be reserved and taken in a later year through the device of a e.

How does Chief Justice Stone explain the provisions relative to loss and gains om the sale or exchange of capital assets, with particular reference to loss a payer may sustain upon the foreclosure sale of his interest in real estate? 

In the computation of taxable income Section 23 (e) (2) of the 1934 venue Act permits the individual taxpayer to deduct losses sustained durthe year incurred in any transaction for profit. Subsection (j) provides at "losses from sales or exchanges of capital assets" shall be allowed only to extent of $2,000 plus gains from such sales or exchanges as provided by ction 117 (d). By Section 117(b) [now (a)] it is declared that "capital ets" "means property held by the taxpayer. but does not include stock trade of the taxpayer... or property held by the taxpayer primarily for e to customers in the ordinary course of his trade or business." ... ...

The treatment of gains and losses from sales of capital assets on a different is from ordinary gains and losses was not introduced into the revenue vs by the 1934 Act. That had been a feature of every revenue law beging with the Act of 1921, 42 Stat. 227, and each had defined as capital losses sses from sales of exchange of capital assets." The 1934 Act made no ange in this respect but for the first time provided that "capital assets" uld include all property acquired by the taxpayer for profit regardless of length of time held by him and that capital gains and losses from sales of ital assets should be recognized in the computation of taxable income acding to the length of time the capital assets are held by the taxpayer, varyfrom 100% if the capital asset is held for not more than a year to 30% t is held more than ten years. Section 117(a). Finally, for the first time, statute provided that capital losses in excess of capital gains should be lucted from ordinary income only to the extent of $2,000. Thus by treating property acquired by the taxpayer for profit as capital assets and limiting deduction of capital losses in the manner indicated, the Act materially tailed the advantages which the taxpayer had previously been able to gain choosing the time for selling his property. 

The definition of capital losses as losses from "sales" of capital assets, as have pointed out, was not new. As will presently appear, the legislative ory of this definition shows that it was not chosen to exclude from the ital assets provisions for losses resulting from forced sales of taxpayers' perty. And, if so construed, substantial loss of revenue would result under 

1934 Act, whose purpose was to avoid loss of revenue by the application the capital assets provisions. In drafting the 1934 Act the Committee had

before it proposals for stabilizing the revenue by adoption of the Briti system under which neither capital gains nor losses enter into the comp tation of the tax. In declining to follow this system in its entirety the Co mittee said: "It is deemed wiser to attempt a step in this direction witho letting capital gains go entirely untaxed." It accordingly reduced the t burden on capital gains progressively with the increase of the period up ten years, during which the taxpayer holds the capital assets, and permitte the deduction, on the same scale, of capital losses, but only to the extent th there are taxable capital gains, plus $2,000. In thus relieving capital gai from the tax imposed on other types of income, it cannot be assumed, in th absence of some clear indication to the contrary, that Congress intended permit deductions in full of lossess resulting from forced sales of the ta payers' property, from either capital gains or ordinary gross income, whi taxing only a fraction of the gains resulting from the sales of such propert 

See White v. U. S., 305 U.S. 281; Helvering v. Inter-Mountain Life Ins. C 

294 U.S. 686.

The taxation of capital gains after deduction of capital losses on a mo favorable basis than other income, was provided for by section 206 of th 1921 Revenue Act, as the means of encouraging profit-taking sales of capit investments, House Report, No. 350, 67th Congress, 2d Session, page 8. this section as in later Acts, capital net gain was defined as "the excess of th total capital gain over the sum of capital deductions and capital losses capital losses being defined as the loss resulting from the sale of exchange capital assets. In submitting the proposed Revenue Act of 1924, the Hou committee pointed out that the 1921 Act contained no provision for limitin deduction of capital losses where they exceeded the amount of capital gain 

House Report No. 179, 68th Congress, 1st Session, page 14. This w remedied by providing in Section 208 (c) that the amount by which the t is reduced on account of a capital loss shall not exceed 122% of the capit loss. In commenting on this provision the Committee said, page 20: "If th amount by which the tax is to be increased on account of capital gains limited to 122% of the capital gain it follows logically that the amount b which the tax is reduced on account of capital losses shall be limited to t 122% of the loss." This provision was continued without changes no material until the 1934 Act. Sec. 208 (c) of the 1924 and 1926 Acts; Se 

101 (b) in the 1928 and 1932 Acts, 47 Stat. 191.

Congress thus has given clear indication of a purpose to offset capit gains by losses from the sale of like property and upon the same percentag basis as that on which the gains are taxed. See U. S. v. Pleasants, 305 U.S. 35 

This purpose to treat gains and deductible losses on a parity but with a furth specific provisions provided by section 117 (d) of the 1934 Act, permittin specified percentages of capital losses to be deducted from ordinary incom to the extent of $2,000, would be defeated in a most substantial way if on a percentage of the gains were taxed but losses on sales of like property cou be deducted in full from gross income. This treatment of losses from sales capital assets in the 1924 and later Acts and the reason given for adopting afford convincing evidence that the "sales" referred to in the statute inclu forced sales such as have sufficed, under long accepted income tax practi to establish a deductible loss in the case of non-capital assets. Such sales c equally be taken to establish the loss in the case of capital assets witho

ringing the declared policy of the statute to treat capital gains and losses a parity.

We can find no basis in the language of the Act, its purpose or its legisve history, for saying that losses from sales of capital assets under the 4 Act, more than its predecessors were to be treated any differently ether they resulted from forced sales or voluntary sales. True, courts in interpretation of a statute have some scope for adopting a restricted her than a literal or usual meaning of its words where acceptance of that aning would lead to absurd results, or would thwart the obvious purpose the statute. But courts are not free to reject that meaning where no such sequences follow and where, as here, it appears to be consonant with the pose of the Act as declared by Congress and plainly disclosed by its cture. 

It is not without signifiance that Congress, in the 1934 Act, enlarged the pe of its provisions relating to losses from sales of capital assets by inling within them losses upon the disposition of the taxpayer's property methods other than sale and without reference to the voluntary action he taxpayer. It thus treats as losses from sales or exchanges the loss sused from redemption of stock, section 115 (c), retirement of bonds, sec- 

117 (f), losses from short sales, section 117(e) (1), and loss sustained by are of the holder of an option to exercise it, section 117(e) (2), although e of these transactions involves a loss from a sale. See McClain v. Comsion, 311 U.S. 504.

The scope of the capital loss provisions was still further enlarged by Sec- 22(k) (2) of the Revenue Act of 1938, 52 Stat. 447, which provides if securities, which are capital assets, are ascertained to be worthless, and charged off within the taxable year the loss, with an exception not now erial, shall be considered as a loss arising from a sale or exchange. These visions disclose a consistent legislative policy to enlarge the class of deuctible losses made subject to the capital assets provisions without regard he voluntary action of the taxpayer in producing them. We would lly suppose that Congress would not have made provision for the like ment of losses resulting from a forced sale of the taxpayer's property ired for profit either in the 1934 or 1938 Act, if it had thought that the "sales or exchanges" as used in both acts did not include such sales of taxpayer's property. 

Stone, J., [now C.J.] Helvering v. Hammel, 311 U.S. 504, 1941. ince the cause of action arising in Helvering v. Hammel, supra, how has on 117 (d) been amended so that the taxpayer, in the case of long-term al losses shall be allowed to deduct his loss, but in short-term losses only e extent of his short-term capital gains?

For a such taxable year, paragraph (2) of section 117 (d), prior to its amendm provides for a limitation on deductions for short-term capital losses sustain by taxpayers other than corporations, that is, losses from sales or exchan of capital assets held for 18 months or less shall be allowed as deductions of to the extent of the gains from sales or exchanges of capital assets held

What deduction may a taxpayer take for the loss of useful value of assets of his business as through change in business conditions? 

When, through some change in business conditions, the usefulness in business of some or all of the capital assets is suddenly terminated, so t the taxpayer discontinues the business or discards such assets permanen from use in such business, he may claim as a loss for the year in which takes such action the difference between the basis [adjusted as provided section 113(b) and sections 19.113 (a) (14)-1 and 19.113(b) (1)-1) 

19.113(b) (3)-2, inclusive] and the salvage value of the property. T exception to the rule requiring a sale or other disposition of property in or to establish a loss requires proof of some unforeseen cause by reason of wh the property has been prematurely discarded, as, for example, where an crease in the cost or change in the manufacture of any product makes it nee sary to abandon such manufacture, to which special machinery is exclusiv devoted, or where new legislation directly or indirectly makes the contin profitable use of the property impossible. This exception does not extend a case where the useful life of property terminates solely as a result of the gradual processes for which depreciation allowances are authorized. It d not apply to inventories or to other than capital assets. The exception plies to buildings only when they are permanently abandoned or permaner devoted to a radically different use, and to machinery only when its use such is permanently abandoned. Any loss to be deductible under this ex tion must be fully explained in the return of income. The limitations vided in section 117 with respect to the sale or exchange of capital assets h no application to losses due to the discarding of capital assets.

If the depreciable assets of a taxpayer consists of more than one item and preciation, whether in respect of items or groups of items, is based upon average lives of such assets, losses claimed on the normal retirement of h assets are not allowable inasmuch as the use of an average rate conplates a normal retirement of assets both before and after the average life been reached and there is, therefore, no possibility of ascertaining any ual loss under such circumstances until all assets contained in the group e been retired. In order to account properly for such retirement the entire t or other basis of assets retired, adjusted for salvage, will be charged to depreciation reserve account, which will enable the full cost or other is of the property to be recovered. 

In cases in which depreciable property is disposed of due to causes other n exhaustion, wear and tear, and normal obsolescence, such as casualty, olescence other than normal, or sale, a deduction for the difference been the basis of the property [adjusted as provided in section 113(b) and cles 19.113 (a) (14)-1, 19.113(b)-1, and 19.113(b)-2] and its salvage value /or amount realized upon its disposition may be allowed subject to the itations provided in the Act upon deductions for losses, but only if it is arly evident that such disposition was not contemplated in the rate of reciation.

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.23 (e)-3, page 

81, 1940.

If termites and dry rot destroy a building, may it be claimed as a loss arising n "fire, storms, shipwreck, or other casualty"?

It can be seen that "casualty" may properly be used in the sense of "acent." The latter word is defined by the same source as "an event that takes ce without one's foresight or expectation; an undesigned, sudden, and xpected event." Showing that casualty may have the sense of suddenness ne definition in 1 Bouv. Law Dict., Rawle's 3d Rev., p. 430, as follows: evitable accident. Unforeseen circumstances not to be guarded against human agency, and in which man takes no part." Such definition is in acd with that given in Matheson v. Commissioner Of Internal Revenue, 2 , 54 F. 2d 537, 539.

Since damage by termites or dry rot is not a sudden occurrence but is a elopment over a longer period of time we think the deduction was imper.

United States v. Betty Rogers, Executrix of the Estate of 

Will Rogers, 120 F. 2d 244, 1941.

It is not necessary to say whether or not the word "casualty" should be ted by its context under the doctrine, ejusdem generis. Even though it been used alone we should not have held that it covered such a loss as ; we agree with the Ninth Circuit which held that exactly this kind of ruction was not a "casualty," United States v. Rogers, 9 Čir., 120 F. 2d

That word denotes an accident, a mishap, some sudden invasion by a tile agency; it excludes the progressive deterioration of property through eadily operating cause. Our decision in Matheson v. Commissioner, 2 , 54 F. 2d 537, is very close aboard. There we held that the injury done he taxpayer's house was not a "casualty" when worms had eaten the piles

on which it stood, and water had rusted steel reinforcing bars. Both p and bars were protected by concrete, which water washed away from piers, allowing worms to attack the wood; and which water also penetra to corrode the steel. All this had, however, been a gradual process and that account we held that the loss was not a "casualty." If there be general principle applicable at all, the cases are indistinguishable. 

Fay v. Helvering, 120 F. 2d 253, 1941.

If a taxpayer voluntarily demolishes a building on his property may deduct a loss therefor from his income tax? 

Loss due to the voluntary removal or demolition of old buildings, scrapping of old machinery, equipment, etc., incident to renewals and placements is deductible from gross income. When a taxpayer buys estate upon which is located a building, which he proceeds to raze wit view to erecting thereon another building, it will be considered that the payer has sustained no deductible loss by reason of the demolition of the building, and no deductible expense on account of the cost of such remo the value of the real estate, exclusive of old improvements, being presuma equal to the purchase price of the land and building plus the cost of rem ing the useless building. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.23 (e)-2, page 

81, 1940.

Section 23 (h)-Losses from wagering transations shall be allowed only the extent of the gains from such transactions. 

Internal Revenue Code, Sec. 23 (h). 

If a taxpayer makes a deliberate and intentional payment may it turn to be a loss under the Income Tax Laws? 

The payment of expenditure was deliberate and intentional, made by taxpayer for the insuring of its profits for future years. In the sense that enforceable obligation existed against it the expenditure was voluntary. has been held by this court that money voluntarily paid in settlement of obligation of a third person cannot be said to be a loss to the taxpayer wit the meaning of the statute. White v. Commissioner, 9 Cir., 61 F. 2d 7 

729. The Eighth Circuit Court of Appeals held that the voluntary assumpt of an obligation of another does not come within the revenue statutes as ordinary and necessary business expense or as a business loss not compensa for by insurance or otherwise. Dresser v. United States, Ct. Cl., 55 F. 2d 4 

510, states that "A loss, in order to be deductible under the statute, must an unintentional parting with something of value." We reiterate, the part with the money by the taxpayer here was deliberate and intentional, cording to the testimony introduced by it.

Failure to realize a desired profit is not of itself a loss. If this taxpayer did make the expenditure, there would be no loss, for all that would happen ld be a failure to show a desired profit. 

Garrecht, J., A. Giurland & Bros. v. Com., 119 F. 2d 852, 1941.

Where a person gives his note for a loss can he claim a deduction therefor? f a note is never paid, the taxpayer has parted with nothing more than promise to pay. A promise to pay is not cash, and a deduction is permisonly in the taxable year in which the taxpayer pays cash. 

Hart v. Com., 54 F. 2d 848, quoted by Wilson, in Page v. R. I. Hos. Trust Co., 88 F. 2d 192, 1937. f a loss occurs to a taxpayer and instead of paying for it, he gives his note h postpones the day of payment until another year, when may he report oss in his return? 

Ordinarily, at least, a taxpayer may not deduct from gross receipts a posed loss which in fact is represented by his outstanding note. Eckert om., 283 U.S. 140.... And conversely, a promise to pay indeterminate of money is not necessarily income. "Generally speaking, the income aw is concerned only with realized losses, as with realized gains.' In 

I words, the taxpayer may not take deductions for a loss where what he done is merely to postpone the evil day. He may take the deductions re he has met the evil day and the loss has been realized. The fact that orrows money to meet it is immaterial; the fact that he borrows to meet utstanding obligation out of which the loss arises is immaterial; and the he borrows from the person to whom the obligation is due is immaterial, ided that there is, as there was here, a real payment of such obligation oney or money's worth and not a mere extension or renewal. 

Parker, J., Price v. Com., 106 F. 2d 336, 1939.

How might the court consider the claim of loss by a buyer who paid for 

Is which the seller never delivered?

We do not think that a loss resulting from a buyer's prepayment to a r who proves to be irresponsible is necessarily sustained, in the statutory ning, as soon as the money is paid. The statute was intended to apply not to losses resulting from the physical destruction of articles of value but hose occurring in the operations of trade and business, where the busiman has ventured on a course of action in the reasonable expectation the promised conduct of another will come to pass. Not only the future ess of the business but its present solvency depends on the probable acof his prophecy. Only when events prove the prophecy to have been can it be said that he has suffered. His case is not like that of a man who to learn of the theft of his bonds or the burning of his house until a after the occurrence, but rather resembles the position of the merchant buys in one year, for sale in the next, merchandise which shifting on renders unsaleable in the latter. It may well be that he whose house cy

has been burned has sustained a loss whether he knows it or not and m recover a tax paid in ignorance of that material fact. But we cannot say th the merchant whose action has been based not merely on ignorance of fact but on faith in a prophecy-even though the prophecy is made with full knowledge of the facts-can claim to have sustained a loss before future fails to justify his hopes.... Here... there is nothing in the findin from which we could conclude that the respondent in 1918 had ceased regard his rights under the contract as having value or that there was th reasonable ground to suppose that efforts to enforce them would be fruitle 

Stone, J., [now C.J.] Lewellyn v. Electric Reduction Co., 275 U.S. 243, 1927.

Is a loss deductible which is caused by an adverse judgment in a laws for a breach of contract several years before?

Generally speaking, the income-tax law is concerned only with reali losses, as with realized gains. Weiss v. Weiner, 279 U.S. 333. Exception made however, in the case of losses which are so reasonably certain in f and ascertainable in amount as to justify their deduction, in certain circ stances, before they are absolutely realized. As respects losses occasion by the taxpayer's breach of contract, no definite legal test is provided by statute for the determination of the year in which the loss is to be deduct The general requirement that losses be deducted in the year in which th are sustained calls for a practical, not a legal test. And the direction t net income be computed according to the method of accounting regula employed by the taxpayer is expressly limited to cases where the Com sioner believes that the accounts clearly reflect the net income. Much l tude for discretion is thus given to the administrative board charged w the duty of enforcing the Act. Its interpretation of the statute and the p tice adopted by it should not be interfered with unless clearly unlawful. [The Commissioner had denied the claim.] 

Brandeis, J., Lucas v. American Code Co., 280 U.S. 445, 1930.

How would a reserve set up against the possible loss of a case be conside The prudent business man often sets up reserves to cover contingent bilities. But they are not allowable as deductions. The reserve set up by 

Company was of that character. It cannot be said that the loss actually by the Company in 1923 was, as a matter of law or of undeniable fact, tained in 1919.

Brandeis, J., Lucas v. American Code Co., 280 U.S. 445, 1930.

(g) CAPITAL LOSSES.

Internal Revenue Code 23 (G) and Treasury Regulations 

No. 103, Sec. 19.23 (g)-1, page 184, 1940. 

What is the approved method of taking a loss upon stock?

The right to claim a deduction is a statutory privilege and the burden of oving losses within the statute rests upon the taxpayer. Proof of loss upon ck may be made on two events, (1) when the stock is disposed of, or (2) en it actually becomes worthless. Art. 174, T.R. 77. . . . "Generally aking, the income tax law is concerned only with realized losses, as with lized gains." Lucas v. American Code Co., Inc., 280 U.S. 445. It is innbent upon the taxpayer to prove, therefore, that the stock in question came worthless within the taxable year. "Partial losses are not allowable deductions from gross income so long as the stock has a value and has not en disposed of." Dresser v. U. S., 55 F. 2d 499. Generally, also, the taxver must prove an identifiable event which makes the loss deductible; this y be a single event or a series; and occurs usually when the property in estion is sold or disposed of or its value otherwise extinguished. 

Garrecht, J., Jones v. Com., 103 F. 2d 681, 1939. 

What is meant by an "identifiable event which makes the loss deductible"? "An identifiable event" in the taxing decision, as we understand it, is an ident or occurrence that points to or indicates a loss-an evidence of a s. The evidence need not, though, consist of stereotype plan or scope. It y vary according to circumstances and conditions. We see no reason why might not appear in the assets of a business depreciated or exhausted. . . . deed, the Treasury Regulations declare that while mere shrinkage in the ue of a stock is not deductible as a loss, one does occur when a stock bemes finally worthless. Here the company, after a period of losing money

in the operation of its business, and finding itself heavily in debt, witho hope of recuperation, decided in 1926 to dispose of its lease, sell its properti and assets, and abandon business. It was without credit or assets and for a practical purposes was hopelessly insolvent. The petitioner's investment ini stock was then worthless, too. We think the loss occurred as at that time an not in 1927. 

Industrial Rayon Corp. v. C. I. R., 94 F. 2d 383, quoted by O'Brien, J., in Gaylor E. & M. Co. v. U. S., 33 F. Supp. 

310, 1939. If a taxpayer has stock in a corporation whose charter is revoked and i stock becomes worthless, would that be an event which would enable the ta payer to declare the stock a loss under section 23(e)?

The taxpayer here made no effort to claim a loss until 1935, when the con pany's charter was revoked and it divested itself of all its assets. It then b came known definitely, and we think for the first time, that the stockholde were to suffer a complete loss. The worthlessness of the taxpayer's sto then became a fact susceptible of proof by the identifiable events referred t The statute contemplates the deduction from gross income of losses which a fixed by identifiable events, such as the sale of property, etc. The act do not contemplate, and the regulations specifically forbid, a reduction by t taxpayer for an amount claimed as loss merely on account of shrinkage fluctation in value of capital stock. The income tax law is concerned on with realized losses as it is with realized gains. In dealing with a loss o casioned by the taxpayer's breach of contract, the Supreme Court said, ". no definite legal test is provided by the statute for the determination of t year in which the loss is to be deducted. The general requirement th losses be deducted in the year in which they are sustained calls for a practic not a legal, test.' 

Briggle, J., Dunbar v. Com., 119 F. 2d 367, 1941.

If a deduction is claimed for stocks becoming worthless what must t taxpayer be prepared to prove?

As a deduction is claimed, the burden is on the taxpayer to establish th the stock became worthless in 1933 rather than at an earlier time. That t stock did not become worthless in 1933, but did so prior to that date is a neg tive finding. The Board is not under a duty to make an affirmative findi as to when the stock did in fact become worthless. It is true that sor liberality is accorded to the taxpayer, and this has taken the form of requ ing that worthlessness be established by some identifiable event. This do not mean, however, that the taxpayer can himself fix the identifiable eve by selling a nominal amount of stock that had become worthless in a pri year. Nor can he postpone his claim of loss until only an "incorrigib optimist" would fail to know that the stock had become worthless at earlier date. A finding of worthlessness depends on all the facts of the pa ticular case reasonably leading to that conclusion. 

Swan, J., Keeney v. C. I. R., 116 F. 2d 401, 1940.

Losses are deductible from income for those years only in which the ses are sustained. In order that a loss arising from investment in the ital stock of a corporation may be regarded as "sustained" in any given r, it is ordinarily necessary either that there be a final disposition of the estment as by sale or exchange, or that there be some "identifiable event" which the loss is otherwise clearly evidenced. It is also true that instances y arise in which the value of the shares held has "become finally extinct" in which the taxpayer is justified in claiming deduction; but we are of opinion that the taxpayer is not required to ascertain at his peril the year which such value may become extinct, provided the stock is still held and re may be some future value, and provided further there is an absence of dentifiable event which has been brought to the taxpayer's knowledge and ich clearly evidences the destruction, either then or theretofore, of value. 

Gowen v. Com., 65 F. 2d 923, quoted by O'Brien, J., in 

Gaylor E. & M. Co. v. U. S., 33 F. Supp. 310, 1939. 

What may help a taxpayer in determining the worthlessness of his stock?

In establishing the date of worthlessness, the taxpayer is aided by the rule the date is to be fixed by "identifiable events" which form the basis of sonable grounds for abandoning any hope for the future. The taxpayer not be an "incorrigible optimist" in postponing the time for claiming a uction based on the worthlessness of stock. Keeney v. Commissioner, 2 , 116 F. 2d 401, 402. Nor can he postpone the date in a manner which unts to a manipulation of his deductions to suit his income. DeLoss v. nmissioner, 2 Cir., 28 F. 2d 803, 804; A. R. Jones Oil & O. Co. v. Commiser, 10 Cir., 114 F. 2d 642, 646. In general the taxpayer may, and must, m his deduction in the year in which events are such as to furnish concing evidence that the stock has in fact become worthless in that year. e form of effective evidence, though not necessarily conclusive is the tence of an authoritative balance sheet showing whether assets exceed ilities so as to leave any equity for the stock. See Sterling Morton v. Comsioner, 38 B.T.A. 1270, 1278, affirmed, 7 Cir., 112 F. 2d 320. In the case ar we think the date of worthlessness must be determined from the res of assets and liabilities made from time to time during the pendency he receivership. 

Swan, J., Mahler v. Com., 119 F. 2d 869, 1941.

section 23 (f) unless the claim is made by a corporation, a dealer in stocks securities, and with respect to a transaction made in the ordinary course of business.
