Where a husband sustains a loss on the sale of securities, may it be app to offset the same amount of gain realized by his wife in rendering a j income tax return?

The question in that relation, that is, as to deduction for losses on sale securities, was submitted to the Commissioner of Internal Revenue and answered by him on December 29, 1932, as follows: "The specific question presented is whether the loss sustained by the band may be applied to offset the same amount of gain realized by the wif rendering joint income tax return for the year. In reply you are adv that, in the case of a husband and wife living together who file a joint inc tax return, the tax liability is computed on the aggregate income as provi by section 51(b) (2) of the Revenue Act of 1932, (which provided, income of each shall be included in a single joint return, in which case tax shall be computed on the aggregate income.' Our present law provi 'In the case of a husband and wife living together the income of each ( though one has no gross income) may be included in a single return made them jointly, in which case the tax shall be computed on the aggregate come, and the liability with respect to the tax shall be joint and sever and such joint return is treated as if it was the return of a single individ The aggregate income in such case would of course embrace the gain well as the allowable deductions of each spouse. If it is correctly underst from your letter that the gains and losses in the illustration presented from transactions falling within the same class within the meaning of statute such as sale of securities not held for a period of more than two y the loss sustained by the husband would offset the same amount of realized by the wife from such source." 

This statement by the Commissioner applied the same principle w had previously been followed with respect to deductions in the joint ret of husband and wife, there having been no indication by Congress of different purpose....

We are of the opinion that under the provision of the Act of 1934 as to nt returns of husband and wife, which embodied a policy set forth in stantially the same terms for many years, Congress intended to provide a tax on the aggregate net income and that the losses of one spouse might deducted from the gains of the other; and that this applied as well to dections for capital losses as to other deductions. This, we think, was the aning of the provision of the Revenue Act of 1934 when it was enacted, it was subject to change only by Congress, and not by the Department. [The above excerpt from the opinion of Chief Justice Hughes indicates w the Supreme Court holds the rulings of the Commissioner of Internal venue, when long in use, and not overruled by act of Congress.] 

Hughes, C.J., Helvering v. Janney, 311 U.S. 189, 1940.

CHAPTER  

Definition of debt-Definition of bad debt-Treasury provisions for deduction of bad debts-Examples of bad debts-When bad debts are deductible-Methods of ascertaining worthlessness of debt-Handling bad debts which are subsequently paid-Deductions in good faith-Worthless bonds as bad debts 

What is the meaning of the word "debt"?

The ordinary legal sense of the term "debt" is an obligation for the pa ment of money founded upon a contract, express or implied. 

In the main, the distinguishing characteristic of such an obligation is th it is for a sum certain or a sum readily reducible to a certainty. It is an ob gation to pay a sum certain, or a sum which may be ascertained by a simp mathematical calculation from known facts, regardless of whether the liab ity arises from contract or is implied or imposed by law. 

Quinn, J., City of Newark v. Lehman's Estate, 18 N.J. 

Misc. 510, 14 A. 2d 792, 1940. 

What is usually meant by bad debts?

There are many meanings to such words as "debt" or "indebtedness", a their precise meaning in any given statute must depend upon the purpose the statutory provision. . . .

To be deductible as a bad debt, the debt must have existed in fact, f that which never existed had no value to lose. It has been held that when taxpayer attempts to deduct as bad debts amounts which he has paid as a dorsers or guarantors and for which he has not been reimbursed, it must shown that he was legally bound to pay as the voluntary assumption of ob gations does not justify deductions. 

Mahoney, J., Hamlen v. Welch, 116 F. 2d 413, 1940.

What are the provisions in Section 23 for the deduction of bad debts? (1) GENERAL RULE.-Debts ascertained to be worthless and charged within the taxable year (or, in the discretion of the Commissioner, a reaso able addition to a reserve for bad debts); and when satisfied that a debt is coverable only in part, the Commissioner may allow such debt, in an amo not in excess of the part charged off within the taxable year, as a deducti This paragraph shall not apply in the case of a taxpayer, other than a ba as defined in section 104, with respect to a debt evidenced by a security defined in paragraph (3) of this subsection. [80]

(2) SECURITIES BECOMING WORTHLESS.-If any securities [as defined in graph (3) of this subsection] are ascertained to be worthless and charged within the taxable year and are capital assets, the loss resulting therefrom , in the case of a taxpayer other than a bank, as defined in section 104, the purposes of this chapter, be considered as a loss from the sale or exge, on the last day of such taxable year, of capital assets. 

3) DEFINITION OF SECURITIES.-As used in this subsection the term "se ties" means bonds, debentures, notes, or certificates, or other evidences debtedness, issued by any corporation (including those issued by a govment or political subdivision thereof), with interest coupons or in reged form. 

EC. 19.23 (k)-1. BAD DEBTS. (a) Bad debts may be treated in either of ways- 1) By a deduction from income in respect of debts ascertained to be chless in whole or in part, or 2) By a deduction from income of an addition to a reserve for bad debts. axpayers were given an option for 1921 to select either of the methods ioned for treating such debts. (See article 151, Regulations 62.) The od used in the return for 1921 must be used in returns for all subsequent s unless permission is granted by the Commissioner to change to the method. If the method selected is approved, it must be followed turns for subsequent years, except as permission may be granted by the missioner to change to another method. Application for permission to ge the method of treating bad debts shall be made at least 30 days prior e close of the taxable year for which the change is to be effective. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Sec. 19.23 (k)-1, page 86, 1940.

What are the regulations of the Treasury Department as to when a bad may be deducted and how by a taxpayer? all the surrounding and attending circumstances indicate that a debt is hless, either wholly or in part, the amount which is worthless and ged off or written down to a nominal amount on the books of the taxshall be allowed as a deduction in computing net income. No deduction shall be allowed for the part of a debt tained to be worthless and charged off prior to January 1, 1921, unless ntil the debt is ascertained to be totally worthless and is finally charged is written down to a nominal amount, or the loss is determined in some manner by a closed and completed transaction. Before a taxpayer may e off and deduct a debt in part, he must ascertain and be able to demon- , with a reasonable degree of certainty, the amount thereof which is lectible. Any amount subsequently received on account of a bad debt account of a part of such debt previously charged off and allowed as a ction for income tax purposes, must be included in gross income for the le year in which received. In determining whether a debt is worthless

in whole or in part the Commissioner will consider all pertinent eviden including the value of the collateral, if any, securing the debt and the finan condition of the debtor. Partial deductions will be allowed with respect specific debts only.

Where the surrounding circumstances indicate that a debt is worth and uncollectible and that legal action to enforce payment would in all pr ability not result in the satisfaction of execution on a judgment, a show of these facts will be sufficient evidence of the worthlessness of the debt the purpose of deduction. Bankruptcy is generally an indication of worthlessness of at least a part of an unsecured and unpreferred debt. Act determination of worthlessness in bankruptcy cases is sometimes possible fore and at other times only when a settlement in bankruptcy shall have b had. Where a taxpayer ascertained a debt to be worthless and charged off in one year, the mere fact that bankruptcy proceedings instituted aga the debtor are terminated in a later year, confirming the conclusion that debt is worthless, will not authorize shifting the deductions to such la year. If a taxpayer computes his income upon the basis of valuing his n or accounts receivable at their fair market value when received, which be less than their face value, the amount deductible for bad debts in any is limited to such original valuation. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, page 86, 1940. 

What are examples of bad debts given by the Treasury Regulations?

Worthless debts arising from unpaid wages, salaries, rents, and sim items of taxable income will not be allowed as a deduction unless the inc such items represent has been included in the return of income for the for which the deduction as a bad debt is sought to be made or for a prev year. Only the difference between the amount received in distribution the assets of a bankrupt and the amount of the claim may be deducted bad debt. The difference between the amount received by a creditor decedent in distribution of the assets of the decedent's estate and the amo of his claim may be considered a worthless debt. A purchaser of acco receivable which cannot be collected and are consequently charged off books as bad debts is entitled to deduct them, the amount of deduction to based upon the price he paid for them and not upon their face value. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.23 (k)-2, page 

88, 1940.

Under what conditions are bad debts allowed to be deducted from g income? What should the taxpayer do?

It was clearly the purpose of the Congress to condition allowance of duction for bad debts upon the perpetuation of evidence that they v ascertained to be worthless within the taxable year, and upon some spe act of the taxpayer clearly indicating their abandonment as assets. Undo edly Congress was competent to make such provisions, and their valu provisions, and their value in preventing evasion and fraud is obvious. 

Fairless v. Com., 67 F. 2d 475, quoted in Santa Monica 

M. P. Co. v. U. S., 99 F. 2d 450, 1938.

How is the provision that worthless debts may be deducted in the year they ecome worthless construed? 

The requirement that debts be ascertained to be worthless during the taxole year has been generally construed to allow some latitude to the taxpayer the matter of ascertainment provided only that he exercise good faith and not close his eyes to the obvious." . . . The cases have generally held that ere is no absolute duty on the taxpayer to ascertain worthlessness, at his eril, during the same year when the debt in fact became uncollectible. 

It must be noted that the language of the subsection of the statute relating the deduction for worthless debts differs from that relating to losses, in at the latter must be taken as of the year in which the losses are sustained. his has been held to be fixed by an identifiable event, the point when the hysical loss actually occurred, a very different matter from ascertainment at a debt become worthless. 

Sparks, J., Com. v. MacDonald Eng. Co., 102 F. 2d 942, 1939.

What two elements must occur within the tax year to make a bad debt eductible?

A taxpayer is entitled to a bad debt deduction only if the debt is both certained to be worthless and charged off within the same taxable year. nd the fact that debts are charged off in one year raises no presumption at they were also ascertained to be worthless in that year. The burden is ill on the taxpayer to prove the latter fact, and if he fails to sustain this arden the Commissioner's disallowance will be approved. . . .

It may not be amiss to note, however, the frequent holdings of the Board at where debts ascertained to be worthless in one year are charged off ortly after the close of that year before the books are closed, they may be educted as of the year of ascertainment. 

Brewster, J., Malden Trust Co. v. Com., 110 F. 2d 751, 1940.

When may a taxpayer deduct worthless accounts from his taxable income? 

A man is presumed to know what a reasonable person ought to know om facts brought to his attention. A taxpayer should not be permitted to ose his eyes to the obvious, and to carry accounts on his books as good hen in fact they are worthless, and then deduct them in a year subsequent the one in which he must be presumed to have ascertained their worthlessess. To do so would enable him to withhold deductions in his less proserous years, when they would have little effect in reducing his taxes, and en to apply the accumulation at another time to the detriment of the fisc. his would defeat the intent and purpose of the law. 

Honesty of belief in the taxpayer is not conclusive, not binding on the pard. It is the province of the board to determine, on a review of all the cts and circumstances surrounding the particular debt sought to be dected, whether the taxpayer knew or ought to have known its worthlessness a prior year. 

Avery v. Com., 22 F. 2d 6, quoted in Santa Monica 

Mountain Park Co. v. U. S., 20 F. Supp. 209, 1937.

If a bad debt is entered as a loss in January, may it be deducted from the previous year's return?

We are also of the opinion that the debt was "charged off within the tax able year" within the meaning of the statute. The statutory language has been construed many times without legislative change to allow a deduction for bad debts physically charged off in the first months of the succeeding year as of the preceding taxable year.... Ordinary business practice necessi tates the allowance of a reasonable time after the close of the year for the auditing of the books and the physical notation of the closing entries of profit and loss. It is not the physical act done within the year to which Congress has referred, but to the setting up of evidence as to the ascertainment of worthlessness substantially as of the date of such ascertainment and in con firmation thereof in order that such entries will effectually eliminate the amount of the bad debt from the book assets of the taxpayer. 

Mahoney, J., Hamlen v. Welch, 116 F. 2d 413, 1940. 

Why should a taxpayer not delay in taking an allowance for a worthless debt?

Apparently the Board regarded the provisions allowing deductions of bad debts "ascertained to be worthless and charged off within the taxable year," as meaning that worthless debts in order to be deductible must be charged off whenever the taxpayer either "ascertained," or as a reasonable person should have ascertained, that these debts were worthless. "Such a rule would involve an objective and not a subjective test in respect to ascer tainment by the taxpayer, while our recent decisions indicate a bona fide ascertainment by the taxpayer of the worthlessness of a debt will justify a deduction irrespective of whether its worthlessness could have been ascer tained prior to the year when it was discovered. But it seems manifest to u that when a taxpayer knows the worthlessness of a debt in a certain yea and yet chooses to indulge in hopes that something may still be realized from a claim that at the time has neither collectibility nor market value, he ought not to be allowed to delay taking his deduction until all his hopes or illusion are completely dispelled, for it has ceased to have a commercial value. Whil the test of ascertainment is a subjective one, and the taxpayer will not los his right to deduct a worthless debt through a mere error of judgment, proo that in a particular year the facts known to him indicated the worthlessnes of the debt and that it was then actually worthless seems to justify an infer ence that he then ascertained it to be worthless." 

Hand, J., Curry v. C. I. R., 117 F. 2d 307, 1941.

What are some of the methods of ascertaining the worthlessness of a debt In ascertaining the worthlessness of a debt, sometimes the processes of the law declare a debt to be worthless, as by an unsatisfied execution of judgment, discharge in bankruptcy, and the like, but even these unequivoca declarations of the law are not binding on the taxpayer, and he may ascertai a debt to be worthless and then several years later reduce it to judgmen upon which execution is returned unsatisfied. His later action is not conclu sive as showing that his former was in bad faith. The real question is no

en did the debt become worthless, but when did the taxpayer ascertain to be worthless. In determining good faith, it is only the taxpayer's infortion at the time of the loan and the deduction which is to be considered. - There is no absolute duty on the taxpayer to ascertain worthlessness, at peril, during the same year when the debt did in fact become uncollectible. 

Manton, J., Moore v. Com., 101 F. 2d 704, 1939.

If a taxpayer claims a deduction for a bad debt and the debt is later paid, another year, what will happen?

If the debt should in the future be paid the taxpayer can certainly accept hough he must return it as income in the year received. And the fact that lebt called worthless in one year later is found to be collectible does not stroy the previous ascertainment. 

Mahoney, J., Hamlen v. Welch, 116 F. 2d 413, 1940.

What tests do the courts usually use to ascertain whether the taxpayer imed the debt was bad in good faith?

The first inquiry is whether or not the taxpayer did in fact ascertain a ot to be worthless in the year for which he sought deduction; then, ether the taxpayer acted in good faith. To answer the second question, ne courts have used the test of the prudent business man; if a prudent siness man would have decided as the taxpayer did, then the taxpayer acted nestly. Occasionally too, the prudent business man test is applied in the t inquiry, so that if the taxpayer did not make the same investigation of facts upon which to base his decision that the debt was worthless as a adent business man would have made, it is held that the taxpayer did not certain" the worthlessness of the debt. 

Manton, J., Moore v. Com., 101 F. 2d 704, 1939.

If a taxpayer lends some friend or relative money which he never expected receive back, may he take a deduction for a bad debt whenever he thinks needs to?

The taxpayer, even before it made the advances, never expected to be aid; even before it entered each transaction upon its books as a loan, it d mentally wiped it out as uncollectible. For reasons which seemed good it, the taxpayer thought it worth while to expend this part of its income this way. But it never expected to see this money return; it intended to ninate the so-called loan from its books from the moment it was entered. all the cases where alleged loans have been questioned as to their admisility as bad debt deductions, the primary emphasis has been upon whether not there was an expectation of repayment. Such advances, made with the ief that they would not be repaid, are in the nature of gifts, and are not ductible as bad debts. 

Mahoney, J., W. F. Young, Inc. v. Com., 120 F. 2d 159, 1941.

If the taxpayer took a note for the money in the above case what court say? may th It is urged upon us that there is a legal obligation to repay the advanc which the taxpayer could enforce at law. The Board itself answered the argument in the case of C. B. Hayes v. Commissioner of Internal Revenu 

17 B.T.A. 86, 1929, supra, where it said: "If there was no intention to enfor the note, or if there was no reasonable expectation when given that it wou be paid, the transaction was more in the nature of a gift to the son-in-la than a debt. It is true that a note was given which was legally enforcibl 

But a gift may not be claimed as a deduction for tax purposes by the devi of taking a note for the amount of the gift. And this we deem so, even thoug the note may be legally enforcible, if there is no intent to enforce the no or reason to believe when taken that it cannot be collected." 

Mahoney, J., W. F. Young, Inc. v. Com., 120 F. 2d 159, 1941.

If a debt is worthless when made, may it ever be deducted from gross incom However, it has been several times held that a debt which is worthle when acquired cannot be charged off as a bad debt by the taxpayer. It ma be a loss, but if so it must fulfill the requirements for a loss deduction. W do not believe that Congress, in addition to providing for the deduction certain losses, intended to allow the deduction of other losses as bad deb when the taxpayer knew that the money allegedly lent would never be paid and when the amount was known to be a loss at the moment it was pa over. The words "debts ascertained to be worthless and charged off with the taxable year" seem inescapably to mean debts which were consider good when made but subsequently ascertained to be worthless. So-calle debts which were never expected to be repaid must be deducted as losses not at all. 

Mahoney, J., W. F. Young, Inc. v. Com., 120 F. 2d 159, 1941.

Will a taxpayer lose his right to make a deduction if he makes an error judgment in holding a debt over a year when it became bad to a subsequent yea While the test of ascertainment is a subjective one, and the taxpayer w not lose his right to deduct a worthless debt through a mere error of jud ment, proof that in a particular year the facts known to him indicate t worthlessness of the debt and that it was then actually worthless seems justify an inference that he then ascertained it to be worthless. 

Hand, J., Curry v. Com., 117 F. 2d 307, 1941.

When may a taxpayer treat worthless bonds and other securities as bad del and deduct them from his gross income? 

A bond issued by an individual, if ascertained to be worthless, may treated as a bad debt. A bond (whether or not a security) of an insolve corporation secured only by a mortgage from which on foreclosure nothi

calized for the bondholders is regarded as ascertained to be worthless not r than the year of the foreclosure sale, and no deduction for a bad debt llowable in computing a bondholder's income for a subsequent year. taxpayer (other than a dealer in securities) possessing debts evidenced bonds or other similar obligations cannot deduct from gross income amount merely on account of market fluctuation. If a taxpayer asceris, however, that due, for instance, to the financial condition of the tor, or conditions other than market fluctuation, he will recover upon curity none or only a part of the debt evidenced by the bonds or other ilar obligations (which bonds or other obligations are not securities as ined in this section) and so demonstrates to the satisfaction of the Comsioner, he may deduct in computing net income the uncollectible part of debt evidenced by the bonds or other obligations. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Sec. 19.23 (k)-4, page 89, 1940.

The proper allowance for such depreciation of any prope used in the trade or business is that amount which should be set aside fort taxable year in accordance with a reasonably consistent plan (not necessar at a uniform rate), whereby the aggregate of the amounts so set aside, p the salvage value, will, at the end of the useful life of the property in business, equal the cost or other basis of the property determined in cordance with section 113. Due regard must also be given to expenditures current upkeep. How do the courts construe the provision allowing a taxpayer to deduct a nable allowance for exhaustion, wear and tear of property used in a trade siness? • .. 

The federal income tax is aimed at net income determined from gross ne less items such as necessary expenses incurred or capital consumed rning it. Thus, the controlling statute permits a taxpayer in computing come to deduct a "reasonable allowance for exhaustion, wear and 'While it may more often be that he who is both owner and user bears urden of wear and exhaustion of business property in the nature of al, one who is not the owner may nevertheless bear the burden of exion of capital investment. Where it has been shown that a lessee using erty in a trade or business must incur the loss resulting from depreciation pital he has invested, the lessee has been held entitled to the statutory ction.

What is the meaning of the provision that a taxpayer in making his first must choose the method he shall use in figuring depletion? 

Statutes must be construed in the light of their purpose. A literal readf them which would lead to absurd results is to be avoided when they e given a reasonable application consistent with their words and with gislative purpose." Haggar Co. v. Helvering, 308 U.S. 389, 1940. We it would be an absurd result to construe this statute to require the taxto choose a method of depletion and to state the method when there hing from which to make the deduction.... seems to us that the common sense effect of this provision is that the e was to be made at a time when there would be a depletion allow- The words "first return" must be read in the light of the considerations so read, we think the reasonable meaning is that the taxpayer must make oice in the first taxable year in which it would have a depletion allowunder one of the methods of computation. 

Goodrich, J., Pittston-Duryea Coal Co. v. C. I. R., 117 

F.2d 436, 1941.

What is the Treasury Regulation relative to the kinds of property on w a taxpayer may claim a deduction for the depreciation? 

The necessity for a depreciation allowance arises from the fact that tain property used in the business gradually approaches a point where usefulness is exhausted. The allowance should be confined to property this nature. In the case of tangible property, it applies to that which is s ject to wear and tear, to decay or decline from natural causes, to exhaust and to obsolescence due to the normal progress of the art, as where machin or other property must be replaced by a new invention, or due to the adequacy of the property to the growing needs of the business. It does apply to inventories or to stock in trade, or to land apart from the impro ments or physical development added to it. It does not apply to bodies minerals which through the process of removal suffer depletion, other p visions for this being made in the Internal Revenue Code. [See sect 

23(m) and 114.] Property kept in repair may, nevertheless, be the sub of a depreciation allowance. [See section 19.23 (a)-4.] The deduction of allowance for depreciation is limited to property used in the taxpayer's t or business. No such allowance may be made in respect of automobiles other vehicles used solely for pleasure, a building used by the taxpayer so as his residence, or in respect of furniture or furnishings therein, pers effects, or clothing; but properties and costumes used exclusively in a b ness, such as a theatrical business, may be the subject of a depreciation lowance.

SEC. 19.23 (1)-3. DEPRECIATION OF INTANGIBLE PROPERTY.-Intangib the use of which in the trade or business is definitely limited in duration, be the subject of a depreciation allowance. Examples are patents and co rights, licenses, and franchises. Intangibles, the use of which in the busi or trade is not so limited, will not usually be a proper subject of such allowance. If, however, an intangible asset acquired through capital out is known from experience to be of value in the business for only a limi period, the length of which can be estimated from experience with reas able certainty, such intangible asset may be the subject of a depreciation lowance, provided the facts are fully shown in the return or prior theret the satisfaction of the Commissioner. No deduction for depreciation, cluding obsolescence, is allowable in respect of good will. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, page 92, 1940.

If a man held long term leases on buildings which he sublet, might annually take a deduction for depreciation?

He was allowed all sums paid for repairs, but nothing for the estima obsolescence for which he had not paid. It may be taken for the purposes decision that he undertook to keep the buildings up to their present c dition, to pay rent even if the buildings were destroyed and that his o gations were sanctioned by a liability to forfeiture. It is argued with m elaboration that not only covenants but economic necessity required the spondent to keep the buildings up to the mark and that the amount nee for this purpose should be allowed.

What is meant by obsolescence?

Obsolescence is the state or process of becoming obsolete, and obsolete ■s no longer in use; disused as being antiquated, of a type or fashion no

longer current, or worn out. Webster's New I. Dictionary, 2d ed. The ch in the statute, "including a reasonable allowance for obsolescence" vides for an allowance resulting from the disuse of the property in the b ness. To permit the allowance, therefore, while the property is being tinuously used for its original purpose, and while no intention to discont its use appears to make an allowance for a condition the opposite of o lescence. 

Allen, J., Detroit & W. Ferry Co. v. Woodworth, 115 F. 

2d 795, 1940.

The word is much used, and its meaning depends upon and varies the connections in which it is employed. It has been said to be "the dition of process by which units gradually cease to be useful or profit as a part of the property, on account of changed conditions." Obsolesce is not necessarily confined to particular elements or parts of a plant; the w may become obsolete. Obsolescence may arise as the result of laws regula or forbidding the particular use of the property as well as from change the art, the shifting of business centers, loss of trade, inadequacy, or o causes. 

Butler, J., Burnet v. Niagara Brewing Co., 282 U.S. 648, quoted by Biggs, J., in U. S. v. Real Estate-Land T. & T. 

Co., 102 F. 2d 582, 1939.

What does the Supreme Court of the United States say obsolescence me Now it is true that in the popular sense a thing which is obsolete is which is no longer used, a meaning which gives color to petitioner's c for deduction since there is no question that the title plant here involv no longer utilized to any degree whatsoever. But the term "allowance obsolescence", as used in the Act and in the Treasury Regulations, has a rower or more technical meaning than that derived from the common, tionary definition of obsolete. The Treasury Regulations state the circ stances under which an allowance for obsolescence of physical property be allowed, viz., where such property is "being affected by economic ditions that will result in its being abandoned at a future date prior to end of its normal useful life, so that depreciation deductions alone are in ficient to return the cost (or other basis) at the end of its economic ter usefulness." In general, obsolescence under the Act connotes functi depreciation, as it does in accounting and engineering terminology. M than non-use or disuse is necessary to establish it. . . . not every decision management to abandon facilities or to discontinue their use gives rise claim for obsolescence. For obsolescence under the Act requires that operative cause of the present or growing uselessness arise from exte forces which make it desirable or imperative that the property be place.... ...

Real Estate Title Co. v. U. S., 309 U.S. 13, quoted by 

Woodrough, J., in Becker v. Anheuser-Busch, Inc., 120 

F.2d 403, 1941.

What are some of the recognized causes of obsolescence? "Obsolescence" may arise from changes in the art, shifting of business ters, loss of trade, inadequacy, supersession, prohibitory laws, and other th

93 h, apart from physical deterioration, operate to cause plant elements or lant as a whole to suffer diminution in value. 

U. S. Cartridge Co. v. U. S., 284 U.S. 511, quoted in U. S. 

v. Real Estate Title Co., 102 F. 2d 582, and by Wood rough, J., in Becker v. Anheuser-Busch, Inc., 120 F. 2d 

403, 1941. 

How does the law try to make allowance for obsolescence? he statute contemplates annual allowance for obsolescence just as it for exhaustion, wear and tear. That is necessary in order to determine gain or loss because postponement of deductions to cover obsolescence the property involved became obsolete would distort annual income. well understood that exhaustion, wear, tear or obsolescence cannot be ately measured as it progresses and undoubtedly it was for that reason the statute authorized "reasonable" allowance to cover them in order ly to spread that element of operating expenses through the years. 

Gambrinus Brewery Co. v. Anderson, 282 U.S. 538, quoted by Biggs, J., in U. S. v. Real Estate-Land T. & T. 

Co., 102 F. 2d 582, 1939. n what is obsolescence based? 

We perceive the purpose the light of definitions of obsolescence statute, granting a reasonable allowance by way of obsolescence to the yer, to be the allowance to the taxpayer of deductions based upon plant, inery or equipment becoming inutile due to changing competitive cons, improvements resulting from invention, or from prohibitory statutes. object of the statute is to permit a taxpayer to regain capital invested m in an enterprise, the equipment, machinery or material in which his l has been placed becoming obsolete, because time itself brings imments or changed conditions. To put the matter shortly, obsolescence be claimed when machinery or a plant suffers loss in use not because of or lack of it upon the part of the taxpayer, but because of the effect neral conditions over which he has no control. 

Biggs, J., U. S. v. Real Estate-Land T. & T. Co., 102 F. 

2d 582, 1939. ow may the cost of obsolescence that may be deducted from any one year termined? either the cost of obsolescence nor of accruing exhaustion, wear, and hat is properly chargeable in any period of time can be measured acely. A reasonable approximation of the amount that fairly may be ind in the accounts of any year is all that is required.... There is no hard ast rule, as suggested by the government, that a taxpayer must show is property will be scrapped or cease to be used or useful for any se before any allowance may be made for obsolescence. 

Burnet v. Niagara Falls Brewing Co., 282 U.S. 648, quoted by Duffy, J., in Newport Co. v. U. S., 34 F. Supp. 588, 1940.

How may the amount of depreciation or obsolescence be determined? 

Depreciation allowances must be based on intelligent estimates and mere guesses. Paul & Mertens, Law of Federal Income Taxation, V page 561. There is no burden on the Commissioner to prove the correc of his assessment for, once he has formally determined the amount or ra depreciation and made an assessment based thereon the law presume action right and the assessment prima facie valid. The Commissioner' termination must give way, however, if proof shows his computation roneous. 

Garrecht, J., Southern Cal. F. Lines v. Com., 99 F. 2d 104, 1938. 

How is obsolescence considered in Treasury Regulations?

With respect to physical property the whole or any portion of y is clearly shown by the taxpayer as being affected by economic cond that will result in its being abandoned at a future date prior to the e its normal useful life, so that depreciation deductions alone are insufficie return the cost or other basis at the end of its economic term of usefu a reasonable deduction for obsolescence, in addition to depreciation, m allowed in accordance with the facts obtaining with respect to each ite property concerning which a claim for obsolescence is made. No dedu for obsolescence will be permitted merely because, in the opinion of payer, the property may become obsolete at some later date. This a ance will be confined to such portion of the property on which obsoles is definitely shown to be sustained and can not be held applicable to an property unless all portions thereof are affected by the conditions to v obsolescence is found to be due.

CHAPTER 11 DEDUCTIONS FOR INTEREST AND TAXES

[In computing net income there shall be allowed as deductions:] (b) INTEREST.-All interest paid or secrued within the taxable year on indebtedness, except on indebtedness incurred or continued to purchase or carry obligations (other than obligations of the United States issued after September 24, 1917, and originally subscribed for by the taxpayer) the interest upon which is wholly exempt from the taxes imposed by this title. 

ART. 23(b)-1. INTEREST.-Interest paid or accrued within the year on lebtedness may be deducted from gross income, except that interest on lebtedness incurred or continued to purchase or carry obligations, such municipal bonds, first Liberty loan 32 percent bonds, or (in case of a payer not an original subscriber) obligations of the United States issued er September 24, 1917, the interest upon which is wholly exempt from tax, not deductible. Interest paid or accrued within the year on indebtedness urred or continued to purchase or carry (a) obligations of the United tes issued after September 24, 1917, the interest upon which is not wholly empt from the taxes imposed by Title I of the Act, or (b) (In the case of original subscriber) obligations of the United States issued after Septem- 24, 1917, the interest upon which is wholly exempt from the taxes imsed by Title I of the Act, is deductible in accordance with the general rule. 

Interest paid by the taxpayer on a mortgage upon real estate of which he he legal or equitable owner, even though the taxpayer is not directly liable on the bond or note secured by such mortgage, may be deducted as interest his indebtedness. Payments made for Maryland or Pennsylvania ground ts are not deductible as interest but may, if a proper business expense, be ducted as rent.

Treasury Regulation No. 103, Section 19.23(b)-1, page

DEDUCTIONS FOR INTEREST AND TAXES 

101 es imposed by the authority of the United States, provided they are not ded to and made a part of the expenses of the business or the cost of articles merchandise with respect to which they are paid, in which case they nnot be separately deducted. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.23 (c)-1 and -2, pages 76-77, 1940.

May any credit be taken by the taxpayer for payments of assessments for al improvements?

So-called taxes, more properly assessments, paid for local benefits, such street, sidewalk, and other like improvements, imposed because of and asured by some benefit inuring directly to the property against which assessment is levied, do not constitute an allowable deduction from gross ome. A tax is considered assessed against local benefits when the property ject to the tax is limited to property benefited. Special assessments are deductible, even though an incidental benefit may inure to the public fare. The real property taxes deductible are those levied for the general olic welfare by the proper taxing authorities at a like rate against all perty in the territory over which such authorities have jurisdiction. essments under the statutes of California relating to irrigation and of Iowa ating to drainage, and under certain statutes of Tennessee relating to ees, are limited to property benefited, and if the assessments are so limited, amounts paid thereunder are not deductible as taxes. The above statents are subject to the exception that insofar as assessments against local efits are made for the purpose of maintenance or repair or for the purpose neeting interest charges with respect to such benefits, they are deductible. such cases the burden is on the taxpayer to show the allocation of the ounts assessed to the different purposes. If the allocation cannot be made, e of the amounts so paid is deductible. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.23 (c)-3, page 

77, 1940.

If a taxpayer pays taxes under a statute that is later declared by the courts e unconstitutional, is he still entitled to his deduction from gross income? 

Although it was formerly held that an unconstitutional statute is a nullity nitio, more lately it has been recognized that the consequences of action n or restricted in obedience to the requirements of a statute which subsently is declared unconstitutional are to be appraised and adjudged in the t of the compulsion exerted by the statute prior to its determined indity. In Chicot County Drainage District v. Baxter State Bank et al, 308 

1 371, page 374, Mr. Chief Justice Hughes, speaking for the Supreme rt, after specifically referring to the Norton and Hackett cases cited a, said, "It is quite clear, however, that such broad statements as to the et of a determination of unconstitutionality must be taken with qualiions. The actual existence of a statute, prior to such a determination, is perative fact and may have consequences which cannot justly be ignored. past cannot always be erased by a new judicial declaration. The effect

of the subsequent ruling as to invalidity may have to be considered in var aspects, with respect to particular relations, individual and corporate, particular conduct, private and official. Questions of rights claimed to h become vested, of status, of prior determinations deemed to have finality acted upon accordingly, of public policy in the light of the nature both the statute and of its previous application, demand examination." See Phi et al v. School Dist. of Pittsburgh et al, 3 Cir., 111 F. 2d 393, 395. Nowh is the propriety of recognizing and accrediting a status produced by a stat before its unconstitutionality has been judicially declared more stron indicated than it is in the case of tax statutes. The subject taxpayer is un compulsion to pay due regard to the requirements of the statute until invalidity has been authoritatively adjudicated. The Board of Tax App has so recognized in a number of cases where deductions have been allow for taxes paid under statutes which were later held to be invalid. The f that, in the Board cases just referred to, the taxes had been paid within taxable year rather than accrued is of no moment upon a consideration of effect of a tax liability imposed by a statute prior to an adjudication of its validity. In the case of Cartex Mills, Inc. v. Commissioner, 42 B.T.A. it was aptly said that, "To conclude, in an interpretation of the revenue a that substantial amounts paid pursuant to a taxing statute, vigorously enfor by the sovereign and not declared unconstitutional until a later year, are 'taxes paid or accrued within the taxable year' within the meaning of revenue act would be, by way of understatement, highly impractical." 

Jones, J., J. A. Dougherty's Sons, Inc. v. Com., 121 F. 2d 

700, 1941.

CHAPTER 12 ITEMS NOT DEDUCTIBLE 

List of items not deductible-Personal expenses not deductible What items are not deductible by a taxpayer from his gross income? (a) GENERAL RULE.-In computing net income no deduction shall in any be allowed in respect of- (1) Personal, living, or family expenses; (2) Any amount paid out for new buildings or for permanent improvements or betterments made to increase the value of any property or estate; (3) Any amount expended in restoring property or in making good the exhaustion thereof for which an allowance is or has been made; (4) Premiums paid on any life insurance policy covering the life of any officer or employee, or of any person financially interested in any trade or business carried on by the taxpayer, when the taxpayer is directly or indirectly a beneficiary under such policy; (5) Any amount otherwise allowable as a deduction which is allocable to one or more classes of income other than interest (whether or not any amount of income of that class or classes is received or accrued) wholly exempt from the taxes imposed by this title; or 

(b) Losses From SALES OR EXCHANGES OF PROPERTY.— 

(1) LOSSES DISALLOWED.-In computing net income no deduction shall ny case be allowed in respect of losses from sales or exchanges of property, ctly or indirectly— (A) Between members of a family, as defined in paragraph (2) (D); (B) Except in the case of distributions in liquidation, between an individual and a corporation more than 50 per centum in value of the outstanding stock of which is owned, directly or indirectly, by or for such individual; (C) Except in the case of distributions in Liquidation, between two corporations more than 50 per centum in value of the outstanding stock of each of which is owned, directly or indirectly, by or for the same individual, if either one of such corporations, with respect to the taxable year of the corporation preceding the date of the sale or exchange was, under the law applicable to such taxable year, a personal holding company or a foreign personal holding company; (D) Between a grantor and a fiduciary of any trust; (E) Between the fiduciary of a trust and the fiduciary of another trust, if the same person is a grantor with respect to each trust; or [103]

(F) Between a fiduciary of a trust and a beneficiary of such trust.

(2) STOCK OWNERSHIP, FAMILY, AND PARTNERSHIP RULE. For the purpe of determining, in applying paragraph (1), the ownership of stock- (A) Stock owned, directly or indirectly, by or for a corporati partnership, estate, or trust, shall be considered as being owned p portionately by or for its shareholders, partners, or beneficiaries; (B) An individual shall be considered as owning the stock own directly or indirectly, by or for his family; (C) An individual owning [otherwise than by the application of s paragraph (B)] any stock in a corporation shall be considered as ov ing the stock owned, directly or indirectly, by or for his partner; (D) The family of an individual shall include only his brothers a sisters (whether by the whole or half blood), spouse, ancestors, and li descendants; and 

(E) Constructive Ownership as Actual Ownership.-Stock c structively owned by a person by reason of the application of s paragraph (A) shall, for the purpose of applying subparagraph (A), ( or (C), be treated as actually owned by such person, but stock o structively owned by an individual by reason of the application of s paragraph (B) or (C) shall not be treated as owned by him for purpose of again applying either of such subparagraphs in order to m another the constructive owner of such stock. 

[341B] (c) UNPAID EXPENSES AND INTEREST.-In computing net inco no deduction shall be allowed under section 23 (a), relating to expenses curred, or under section 23 (b), relating to interest accrued- (1) If such expenses or interest are not paid within the taxable or within two and one half months after the close thereof; and (2) If, by reason of the method of accounting of the person to wh the payment is to be made, the amount thereof is not, unless paid, cludible in the gross income of such person for the taxable year in wh or with which the taxable year of the taxpayer ends; and (3) If, at the close of the taxable year of the taxpayer or at any t within two and one half months thereafter, both the taxpayer and person to whom the payment is to be made are persons between wh losses would be disallowed under section 24(b). 

(d) HOLDERS OF LIFE OR TERMINABLE INTEREST.-Amounts paid u the laws of any State, Territory, District of Columbia, possession of United States, or foreign country as income to the holder of a life terminable interest acquired by gift, bequest, or inheritance shall not reduced or diminished by any deduction for shrinkage (by whatever n called) in the value of such interest due to the lapse of time, nor by deduction allowed by this Act [except the deductions provided for in section (1) and (m) of section 23] for the purpose of computing the income of an estate or trust but not allowed under the laws of such S Territory, District of Columbia, possession of the United States, or for country for the purpose of computing the income to which such holde entitled.

CHAPTER 13 CREDITS AGAINST NET INCOME 

Definition of credit-Earned income credit-Personal exemption-Definition of "head of the family"-Credit for dependents-Change of status with regard to credits during taxable year 

What is meant by credits allowed against income?

We know that gross income is reduced by certain statutory deduct (e.g. expenses, interest, taxes, losses, etc.) in order to determine net inco 

Net income is the balance of gross income subject to tax. But before rates are applied further reductions are allowed for normal tax purp only. These additional or supplementary or special deductions are kn as credits.... All of the Revenue Acts provided for such credits, which to be distinguished from credits against the tax. The former type of c reduces the amount of net income on which a taxpayer's normal tax is o puted; the latter diminishes his tax liability because of taxes paid by ot on his behalf or because he has paid income or profit taxes to foreign gov ments or to possessions of the United States. 

Klein, Federal Income Taxation (1929) 926.

What are the credits allowed against net income for the purpose of sessing the normal tax of four percent and not the surtax?

Internal Revenue Code, Sec. 25 (a).

CREDITS AGAINST NET INCOME 

107 How is earned income credit provided for in the Revenue Law? (3) EARNED INCOME CREDIT.- -10 per centum of the amount of the earned income, but not in excess of 10 per centum of the amount of the net ome. 

(4) EARNED INCOME DEFINITIONS.-For the purposes of this section— 

(A) "Earned income" means wages, salaries, professional fees, and other amounts received as compensation for personal services actually rendered, but does not include any amount not included in gross income, nor that part of the compensation derived by the taxpayer for personal services rendered by him to a corporation which represents a distribution of earnings or profits rather than a reasonable allowance as compensation for the personal services actually rendered. In the case of a taxpayer engaged in a trade or business in which both personal services and capital are material income producing factors, a reasonable allowances as compensation for the personal services actually rendered by the taxpayer, not in excess of 20 per centum of his share of the net profits of such trade or business, shall be considered as earned income. (B) "Earned income deductions" means such deductions as are allowed by section 23 for the purpose of computing net income, and are properly allocable to or chargeable against earned income. (C) "Earned net income" means the excess of the amount of the earned income over the sum of the earned income deductions. If the taxpayer's net income is not more than $3,000, his entire net income shall be considered to be earned net income, and if his net income is more than $3,000, his earned net income shall not be considered to be less than $3,000. In no case shall the earned net income be considered to be more than $14,000.

Internal Revenue Code, Sec. 25 (a), (3) and (4). 

How is the earned income credit provision interpreted by Treasury Reguons?

Under section 25 (a) (3) the earned income credit allowable for the pure of computing the normal tax is 10 percent of the amount of the earned income, but not in excess of 10 percent of the amount of the entire net ome. 

The entire amount received as professional fees may be treated as earned ome if the taxpayer is engaged in a professional occupation, such as a tor or a lawyer, even though he employs assistants to perform part or all he services, provided the clients or patients are those of the taxpayer and to the taxpayer as the person responsible for the services performed. he case of a husband and wife domiciled in a so-called community propstate and rendering separate income tax returns on the community ine basis, one-half of the income derived from personal services rendered one spouse may be treated as earned income in the separate return of the er spouse.

In the case of a taxpayer engaged in a trade or business in which b personal services and capital are material income-producing factors, a reas able allowance as compensation for the personal services actually rende by the taxpayer shall be considered earned income, but the total amo which shall be treated as the earned income of the taxpayer from such a tr or business shall, in no case, exceed 20 percent of his share of the net pro of such trade or business. No general rule can be prescribed defining trades or businesses in which personal services and capital are material come-producing factors, but this question must be determined with resp to the facts of the individual cases.

What is meant by the "Head of the Family"? 

Treasury Regulations No. 94, under the 1936 Act (Art. 25-4) described 

'head of a family" as "An individual who actually supports and mainin one household one or more individuals who are closely connected him by blood relationship, relationship by marriage, or by adoption, whose right to exercise family control and provide for these dependent viduals is based upon some moral or legal obligation. In the absence of inuous actual residence together, whether or not a person with delent relatives is a head of a family within the meaning of the Act must nd on the character of the separation. If a father is absent on business child or other dependent is away at school or on a visit, the common e being still maintained, the additional exemption applies. If, morethrough force of circumstances a parent is obliged to maintain his ndent children with relatives or in a boarding house while he lives elsere, the additional exemption may still apply. If, however, without necesthe dependent continuously makes his home elsewhere, his benefactor at the head of a family, irrespective of the question of support. A ent alien with children abroad is not thereby entitled to credit as the of a family." ll of the Federal Internal Revenue Laws since 1918 have allowed an ption to the "head of a family," and the identical regulation has been ulgated under each statute. The Commissioner insists, in view of this, the failure of Congress through all the years to change the terms of the ption is evidence of congressional approval which gives the regulation orce of law. This is unquestionably correct unless the statutory language he case of the head of a family ... a personal exemption of [$1,500] is so plain and unambiguous as to make the regulation both unnecessary erroneous. 

Groner, J., Wade v. Helvering, 117 F. 2d 21, 1940.

The credit is based upon actual financial dependency and not mere le dependency. It may accrue to a taxpayer who is not the head of a fam 

But a father whose children receive half or more of their support from a tr fund or other separate source is not entitled to the credit.

A taxpayer not having status of a head of a family or the status of a married person living w husband or wife shall be considered as having the status of a single pers For the purpose of the apportionment of the personal exemption and cr for dependents a fractional part of a month shall be disregarded unles amounts to more than half a month, in which case it shall be considered month. In general, the personal exemption and credit for dependents all able to any taxpayer will be the sum of the amounts apportioned to several periods of the taxable year during which each status was occupied

CHAPTER 14 DEFICIENCY ASSESSMENTS AND JEOPARDY ASSESSMENTS 

Deficiancy of assessment — Time limitation on deficiency assessments-Routine of deficiency assessment - Jeopardy assessments - Waiver in tax proceedings - Appeal of tax matters 

What is the meaning of a deficiency?

As used in this chapter in respect of a tax imposed by this chapter ciency" meansa) The amount by which the tax imposed by this chapter exceeds the unt shown as the tax by the taxpayer upon his return; but the amount hown on the return shall first be increased by the amounts previously sed (or collected without assessment) as a deficiency, and decreased by amounts previously abated, credited, refunded, or otherwise repaid in ect of such tax; or b) If no amount is shown as the tax by the taxpayer upon his return, no return is made by the taxpayer, then the amount by which the tax eds the amounts previously assessed (or collected without assessment) deficiency; but such amounts previously assessed, or collected without sment, shall first be decreased by the amounts previously abated, credited, nded, or otherwise repaid in respect to such tax.

Internal Revenue Code, Sec. 271. 

BEC. 19.271-1. DEFICIENCY DEFINED.-Section 271 by its definition of the d "deficiency" provides a term which will apply to any amount of tax rmined to be due in respect of any taxable year beginning after Deber 31, 1938, in excess of the amount of tax reported by the taxpayer uch year; or in excess of the amount reported by the taxpayer as adjusted way of prior assessments, abatements, credits, refunds, or collections withassessment. In defining the term "deficiency" section 271 recognizes two es of cases-one, where the taxpayer makes a return showing some tax ity; the other, where the taxpayer makes a return showing no tax liability, here the taxpayer fails to make a return. Additional tax shown on an ended return," so called, is a deficiency within the meaning of the Internal enue Code. 

When a case is considered for the first time, the deficiency is the excess he amount determined to be the correct amount of the tax over the unt shown as the tax by the taxpayer on his return, or, if it is a case re no tax was reported by the taxpayer, the deficiency is the amount mined to be the correct amount of the tax. Subsequent information [111]

sometimes discloses that the amount previously determined to be the com amount of the tax is less than the correct amount, and that a redetermina of the tax is necessary. In such a case the deficiency on redeterminatio the excess of the amount determined to be the correct amount of the over the sum of the amount of tax reported by the taxpayer and the deficie assessed in connection with the previous determination. If it is a case w no tax was reported by the taxpayer, the deficiency is the excess of the am determined to be the correct amount of the tax over the amount of the ficiency disclosed by the previous determination. If the previous determ tion resulted in a credit or refund to the taxpayer, the deficiency upon second determination is the excess of the amount determined to be the con amount of the tax over the amount of tax reported by the taxpayer decre by the amount of the credit or refund. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Sec. 19.271-1, page 526, 1940. 

How long after a return is made may a deficiency tax be assessed?

Assessment shall be made within five years [now three years] after return; "no proceedings in court without assessment for the collectio taxes shall be begun after the expiration of such period." The running of 

Statute of Limitations on assessment or proceedings in court for collectio deficiency shall be suspended for the period during which the commissi is prohibited from making assessment or bringing suit and for 60 days th after. Where the assessment has been made within the period properly plicable thereto, the tax may be collected by distraint or proceeding in c "but only if begun... within six years after the assessment of the tax." 

Butler, J., U. S. v. Continental Bank, 305 U.S. 398, 595, S.Ct. 308, 1938.

Does the provision that no suit or proceeding shall be begun to c income taxes after the expiration of three years after the filing of a re refer to distraint to collect taxes?

There are two methods to compel payment. One is suit, a judicial ceding; the other is distraint, an executive proceeding. The word "pro ing" is aptly and commonly used to comprehend steps taken in pursu either. There is nothing in the language or context that indicates an inter to restrict its meaning, or to use "suit" and "proceeding" synonymously The purpose of the enactment was to fix a time beyond which ste enforce collection might not be initiated. The repose intended would n attained if suits only were barred leaving the collector free at any tim proceed by distraint. 

Bowers v. New York & A. L. Co., 273 U.S. 346, quoted by 

Sanborn, J., in U. S. Havner, 101 F. 2d 161, 1939. 

How is a deficiency assessed and collected?

SEC. 19.272-3. EXTENSION OF TIME FOR PAYMENT OF A DEFICIENCY.

shown to the satisfaction of the Commissioner that the payment of a ciency upon the date or dates prescribed for the payment thereof will in undue hardship to the taxpayer, the Commissioner may grant an exte of time for the payment of the deficiency or any part thereof for a p not in excess of 18 months, and in exceptional cases for a further perio in excess of 12 months. The extension will not be granted upon a g

DEFICIENCY AND JEOPARDY ASSESSMENTS 

115 atement of hardship. The term "undue hardship” means more than an inonvenience to the taxpayer. It must appear that substantial financial loss, or example, due to the sale of property at a sacrifice price, will result to the xpayer from making payment of the deficiency at the due date. If a market rists, the sale of property at the current market price is not ordinarily condered as resulting in an undue hardship. The Internal Revenue Code prodes that no extension will be granted where the deficiency is due to neglience or intentional disregard of rules and regulations or to fraud with intent evade tax.

An application for an extension of time for the payment of a deficiency ould be made under oath on Form 1127 and must be accompanied or suported by evidence showing the undue hardship that would result to the taxayer if the extension were refused. A sworn statement of assets and lialities of the taxpayer and an itemized statement under oath showing all eceipts and disbursements for each of the three months immediately preeding the month in which falls the date prescribed for the payment of the eficiency are required and should accompany the application. The applition, with the evidence, must be filed with the collector, who will transmit to the Commissioner with his recommendations as to the extension. When is received by the Commissioner, it will be examined and, if possible, within O days will be denied, granted, or tentatively granted subject to certain contions of which the taxpayer will be notified. The Commissioner will not onsider an application for an extension of time for the payment of a defiency unless request therefor is made to the collector on or before the date rescribed for payment thereof, as shown by the notice and demand from e collector, or on or before the date or dates prescribed for payment in y prior extension granted.

As a condition to the granting of such an extension, the Commissioner ill usually require the taxpayer to furnish a bond on Form 1127B in an nount not exceeding double the amount of the deficiency or to furnish her security satisfactory to the Commissioner for the payment of the ability on or before the date or dates prescribed for payment in the extenon, so that the risk of loss to the Government will not be greater at the end the extension period than it was at the beginning of the period. If a bond required it shall be conditioned upon the payment of the deficiency, inrest, and additional amounts assessed in connection therewith in accordance ith the terms of the extension granted, and shall be executed by a surety ompany holding a certificate of authority from the Secretary of the Treasy as an acceptable surety on Federal bonds, and shall be subject to the ap- -oval of the Commissioner. In lieu of such a bond, the taxpayer may file a ond secured by deposit of bonds or notes to the United States as provided section 1126 of the Revenue Act of 1926 (paragraph 63 of the Appendix these regulations). The amount of the deficiency and additions thereto all be paid on or before the expiration of the period of the extension withat the necessity of notice and demand from the collector. Payment of the eficiency and additions thereto before the expiration of the extension will ot relieve the taxpayer from paying the entire amount of interest provided r in the extension. (See section 296.)

How is a jeopardy assessment made? If the Commissioner believes that the assessment or collection of a defi ciency will be jeopardized by delay, he is required to assess such deficiency immediately, together with the interest and other additional amounts provided by law. If a deficiency is assessed on account of jeopardy after the de cision of the Board of Tax Appeals is rendered, the jeopardy assessment may be made only with respect to the deficiency determined by the Board. The 

Commissioner is prohibited from making a jeopardy assessment after a de cision of the Board has become final (see section 1140, set forth in paragraph 39 of the Appendix to these regulations) or after the taxpayer has filed a petition for review of the decision of the Board. 

If notice of a deficiency was mailed to the taxpayer [see section 272 (a)] before it was discovered that delay would jeopardize the assessment or collec tion of the tax, a jeopardy assessment may be made in an amount greater of less than that included in the deficiency notice. On the other hand if the assessment on account of jeopardy was made without mailing the notice re quired by section 272(a), the Commissioner must within 60 days after the making of the assessment send the taxpayer notice of the deficiency by reg istered mail. The taxpayer may file a petition with the Board for a redeter mination of the amount of the deficiency within 90 days (not counting Sunday or a legal holiday in the District of Columbia as the ninetieth day after such notice is mailed. The Commissioner may, at any time before th decision of the Board is rendered, abate such assessment, or any unpaid por tion thereof, to the extent that he believes the assessment to be excessive i amount. If the petition of the taxpayer is filed with the Board, either befor or after the making of the jeopardy assessment, the Commissioner is require to notify the Board of such assessment or abatement, and the Board has juris diction to redetermine the amount of the deficiency together with all othe amounts assessed at the same time in connection therewith. [See sectio 273(c).] 

After a jeopardy assessment has been made, the list showing such assess ment will be immediately transmitted to the collector. Upon receipt of th list containing the assessment, the collector is required to send notice an demand to the taxpayer for the amount of the jeopardy assessment. Regard less of whether the taxpayer has filed a petition with the Board, he is require to make payment of the amount of such assessment (to the extent that it ha not been abated) within 10 days after the sending of notice and demand b the the collector, unless before the expiration of such 10-day period he file with the collector a bond on Form 1129 of the character hereinafter pre scribed. The bond must be in such amount, not exceeding double the amour for which the stay is desired, as the collector deems necessary and must b executed by sureties satisfactory to the collector. It must be conditione upon the payment of so much of the amount included therein as is not abate by a decision of the Board which has become final, together with the intere on such amount provided for in section 297. If the bond is given before th taxpayer has filed his petition with the Board, it must contain a further co dition that if a petition is not filed before the expiration of the 90-day perio provided for the filing of such petition, the amount stayed by the bond w be paid upon notice and demand at any time after the expiration of su period, together with interest thereon at the rate of 6 percent per annu

Any bond filed after the expiration of 10 days from the date the jeopardy notice and demand is not such a bond as is contemplated by tion 273 (f), and, while the collector may in his discretion accept the bond d stay collection of the deficiency, the taxpayer will not be relieved from yment of interest on the amount of the deficiency at the rate of 6 pernt per annum from the date of the jeopardy notice and demand to the te of payment.

After the Board has rendered its decision and such decision has become al, the collector will be notified of the action taken. The collector will en send notice and demand for the unpaid portion of the amount deterned by the Board, the collection of which has been stayed by the bond. e collector is required to include in the notice and demand for the unpaid rtion, interest at the rate of 6 percent per annum from the date of the pardy notice and demand to the date of the notice and demand referred to this paragraph. If the amount of the jeopardy assessment is less than the ount determined by the Board, the difference, together with interest as ovided in section 292, will be assessed, and collected as part of the tax upon tice and demand from the collector. If the amount included in the notice d demand made after the decision of the Board is not paid within 10 days er such notice and demand, there shall be collected as part of the tax, inest as provided in section 294(b). If the amount of the jeopardy assessment n excess of the amount determined by the Board, the unpaid portion of h excess will be abated. If any part of the excess amount has been paid, vill be credited or refunded to the taxpayer as provided in section 322.

What is meant by jeopardy assessment of deficiency taxes?

If the commissioner believes that assessment or collection of deficiency 1 be jeopardized by delay he shall immediately assess the deficiency and

"notice and demand shall be made by the collector for the payment there Jeopardy assessment may be made whether or not the taxpayer has filed p tion with the board. If it is made after the board's decision, it may be for the deficiency determined by the decision. The taxpayer may obt stay of collection of a jeopardy assessment. 

Butler, J., U. S. v. Continental Bank, 305 U.S. 398, 1938. 

What is meant by a "waiver" in tax adjustment?

Waivers are of quite general use in the adjustment of federal taxes. Th need not be given prior to the expiration of the statutory period of limitati or such period as extended by valid waivers, but at any time with the eff of reviving liability. Their purpose is to give a longer period for the det mination of tax liability where the time under the applicable statute of li tations is insufficent. The courts have tried to carry out this purpose, a while holding that the waiver is not a contract, they have in constru waivers considered the intention of the parties as an important factor. T taxpayer waives the time limitation, and while the signature of the Co missioner of Internal Revenue is required only for administrative purpos the government under the waiver gives up its present right to collect, a its intention as well as that of the taxpayer is entitled to consideration. 

U. S. v. Southern Lumber Co., 51 F. 2d 956, quoted by 

Sanborn, J., in U. S. Havner, 101 F. 2d 161, 1939. 

What is the purpose of the Board of Tax Appeals? 

The purpose of creating the Board of Tax Appeals was to provide a m convenient, more equitable, and more speedy remedy to a person agai whom a tax is improperly assessed and the provision is to be liberally co strued. Prior thereto, no matter how arbitrary, unjust or illegal the action the Commissioner may have been, the person charged with the tax had or the remedy of paying the tax, asking for a refund, and then suing the collec to recover it. [If an additional assessment of tax is made the taxpayer is entitled to ha the assessment reviewed by the Board of Tax Appeals. The losing par before the Board of Tax Appeals may appeal to the United States Circ Court of Appeals for the circuit in which he resides. In certain cases the jud ments of the Circuit Courts of Appeal may be reviewed by the Supreme Co of the United States.] 

Houston St. Corp. v. Commissioner of I. R., 84 F. 2d 821, quoted by Stephens, J., in Baldwin v. Com., 94 F. 2d 357, 1938.

How is the gross income of a farmer figured according to Treasury Reg- Cons?

A farmer reporting on the basis of receipts and disbursements (in which nventory to determine profits is used) shall include in his gross income the taxable year (1) the amount of cash or the value of merchandise or r property received during the taxable year from the sale of live stock produce which were raised during the taxable year or prior years, (2) profits from the sale of any live stock or other items which were pured, and (3) gross income from all other sources. The profit from the of live stock or other items which were purchased after February 28, , is to be ascertained by deducting the cost from the sales price in the in which the sale occurs, except that in the case of the sale of animals hased as draft or work animals or solely for breeding or dairy purposes not for resale, the profit shall be the amount of any excess of the sales e over the amount representing the difference between the cost and the eciation theretofore allowed (but not less than the amount allowable) spect of such property as a deduction in computing net income. n the case of a farmer reporting on the accrual basis (in which an invenis used to determine profits), his gross profits are ascertained by adding e inventory value of live stock and products on hand at the end of the the amount received from the sale of live stock and products, and misneous receipts for hire of teams, machinery, and the like, during the , and deducting from this sum the inventory value of live stock and ucts on hand at the beginning of the year and the cost of live stock products purchased during the year. In such cases all live stock raised urchased for sale shall be included in the inventory at their proper valuadetermined in accordance with the method authorized and adopted for purpose. Also live stock acquired, for draft, breeding, or dairy purposes not for sale, may be included in the inventory, instead of being treated pital assets subject to depreciation, provided such practice is followed istently by the taxpayer. In case of the sale of any live stock included inventory their cost must not be taken as an additional deduction in eturn of income, as such deduction will be reflected in the inventory. n every case of the sale of machinery, farm equipment, or other capital s purchased after February 28, 1913 (which are not to be included in an atory if one is used to determine profits), any excess over the cost thereof [119]

less the amount of depreciation theretofore allowed (but not less than amount allowable) in respect of such property as a deduction in compu net income, shall be included as gross income. If farm produce is exchan for merchandise, groceries, or the like, the market value of the article rece in exchange is to be included in gross income. Rents received in crop sh shall be returned as of the year in which the crop shares are reduce money or the equivalent of money. Proceeds of insurance, such as hail fire insurance on growing crops, should be included in gross income to amount received in cash or its equivalent for the crop injured or destro If a farmer is engaged in producing crops which take more than a year f the time of planting to the time of gathering and disposing, the income th from may, with the consent of the Commissioner (see article 41-2), be c puted upon the crop basis; but in any such cases the entire cost of produ the crop must be taken as a deduction for the year in which the gross inc from the crop is realized. 

As herein used the term "farm" embraces the farm in the ordinarily cepted sense, and includes stock, dairy, poultry, fruit, and truck farms; plantations, ranches, and all land used for farming operations. All individ partnerships, or corporations that cultivate, operate, or manage farms for or profit, either as owners or tenants, are designated as farmers. A pe cultivating or operating a farm for recreation or pleasure, the result of w is a continual loss from year to year, is not regarded as a farmer. 

Form 1040F should be filled in and attached to his income tax retur every farmer who either keeps no records or only records of cash rec and bisbursements; its use is optional with other farmers. [See fur articles 23(a)-11, 23 (e)-5, and 23 (1)-10.] 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Section 19.22 (a) -7, page 32, 1940.

Whe farmer is engaged in producing crops which take more than a year from time of planting to the process of gathering and disposal, expenses dedu may, with the consent of the Commisioner be determined upon the crop and such deductions must be taken in the year in which the gross inc from the crop has been realized. The cost of farm machinery, equipm and farm buildings represents a capital investment and is not an allow deduction as an item of expense. Amounts expended in the developmen farms, orchards, and ranches prior to the time when the productive sta reached may be regarded as investments of capital. Amounts expende purchasing work, breeding, or dairy animals are regarded as investment capital, and may be depreciated unless such animals are included in an in tory.
