What is the rule of taxing money in a trust?

Plainly the section (44 Stat. 32 Rev. Act of 1926) contemplat taxation of the entire net income of the trust. Plainly, also, the fiduci computing net income, is authorized to make whatever appropria ductions other taxpayers are allowed by law. The net income ascer by this operation, and that only, is the taxable income. This the fid may be required to accumulate, or, on the other hand, he may be u duty currently to distribute it. If the latter, then the scheme of the a treat the amount so distributable, not as the trust's income, but as the ficiary's. But as the tax on the entire net income of the trust is to b by the beneficiaries or partly by each, the beneficiary's share of the i is considered his property from the moment of its receipt by the estate treatment of the beneficiary's income is necessary to prevent the pos of postponement of the tax to a year subsequent to that in which the i was received by the trustee. If it were not for this provision the trustee pay on part of the income in one year and the beneficiary on the rem in a later year. For the purpose of imposing the tax the act regards ship, the right of property in the beneficiary, as equivalent to physical sion. The test of taxability to the beneficiary is not receipt of incor the present right to receive it. Clearly an overpayment to a benefici mistake of law or fact, would render him liable for the taxable year consideration, not on the amount paid, but on that payable. 

Freuler v. Helvering, 291 U.S. 35, quoted by Whaley, J., in Sewell v. U. S., 19 F. Supp. 567, 1937.

May Congress tax the income from a trust fund to the maker of the It is competent for Congress, in order to avoid the evasion of tax clare that when one has placed his property in trust subject to a revocation in himself and another who is not the beneficiary he shall theless, be deemed to control the property in such sense that the therefrom shall be treated as his income for the levying of a tax. S [124]

e an irrevocable trust is established to pay for insurance of the settler's to collect the policy upon his death, and to hold or apply the proceeds he benefit of his dependents, Congress may declare the income of the fund taxable to the settler as part of his own income. 

Roberts, J., Helvering v. City Bank Farmers Trust Co., 296 U.S. 85, 1935. ow are capital gains in a fiduciary account treated? tax upon such gains is a tax upon capital transactions the substantial it of which goes to capital. It is an expense incident to dealings in l and not an expense incident to the collection of income. If a taxpayer transfers his funds to trustees, in trust to pay the income wife for life and then to his children, with the power to revoke or alter the any time he pleases, would he have to pay a tax on the income of the fun Taxation is not so much concerned with the refinement of title with actual command over the property taxed-the actual benefit for the tax is paid. If a man directed his bank to pay over income as recei a servant or friend, until further orders, no one would doubt that he be taxed upon the amounts so paid. It is answered that in that case he have a title, whereas here he did not. But from the point of view of ta there would be no difference. The title would merely mean a right to the payment before it took place. The same right existed here althoug not called a title; it is called a power. The acquisition by the wife income became complete only when the plaintiff failed to exercise the that he reserved. Still speaking with reference to taxation, if a man di of a fund in such a way that another is allowed to enjoy the income it is in the power of the first to appropriate it does not matter wheth permission is given by assent or by failure to express dissent. The in that is subject to a man's unfettered command and that he is free to at his own option may be taxed to him as his income, whether he sees enjoy it or not. We consider the case too clear to need help from the law of New York or from arguments based on the power of Congr prevent escape from taxes or surtaxes by devices that easily might be a to that end.

How is a revocable trust considered with respect to income and corp taxing purposes? the Where at any time the power to revest in the grantor title to any pa corpus of the trust is vested- (1) in the grantor, either alone or in conjunction with any perso having a substantial adverse interest in the disposition of such part corpus or the income therefrom, or (2) in any person not having a substantial adverse interest in th position of such part of the corpus or the income therefrom, the income of such part of the trust shall be included in computing th income of the grantor.

Internal Revenue Code, Sec. 166. 

SEC. 19.166-1. TRUSTS WITH RESPECT TO THE CORPUS OF WHICH GRANTOR IS REGARDED AS REMAINING IN SUBSTANCE THE OWNER. (a) S 

-If the grantor of a trust is regarded, within the meaning of the Int Revenue Code, as remaining in substance the owner of the corpus the the income therefrom is not taxable in accordance with the provisio sections 161, 162, and 163 but remains attributable and taxable to the gra This section deals with the taxation of such income. As used in this se the term "corpus" means any part or the whole of the property, re personal, constituting the subject matter of the trust. (b) Test of taxability to grantor.-Section 166 defines with particu instances in which the grantor is regarded as in substance the own

orpus by reason of the fact that he has retained power to revest the us in himself. For the purposes of this section the grantor is deemed ve retained such power if he, or any person not having a substantial est in the corpus or the income therefrom adverse to the grantor, or may cause the title to the corpus to revest in the grantor. A bare interest, such as that of a trustee, is never substantial and never ad- . If the title to the corpus will revest in the grantor upon the exercise power, the income of the trust is attributed and taxable to the grantor dless of ch (1) whether such power or ability to retake the trust corpus to the cantor's own use is effected by means of a power to revoke, to terminate, alter or amend, or to appoint; (2) whether the exercise of such power is conditioned on the predent giving of notice, or on the elapsing of a period of years, or on 

Le happening of a specified event; (3) the time at which the title to the corpus will revest in the grantor possession and enjoyment, whether such time is within the taxable ear or not, or whether such time be fixed, determinable, or certain to me; (4) whether the power to revest in the grantor title to the corpus in the grantor, or in any person not having a substantial interest in the orpus or income therefrom adverse to the grantor, or in both; (5) when the trust was created. at the provisions of section 166 are not to be regarded as excluding taxation to the grantor the income of other trusts, not specified therein, nich the grantor is, for the purposes of the Internal Revenue Code, rly regarded as remaining in substance the owner of the corpus. The or is regarded as in substance the owner of the corpus, if, in view of sential nature and purpose of the trust, it is apparent that the grantor iled to part permanently and definitively with the substantial incidents nership in the corpus. determining whether the grantor is in substance the owner of the s, the Internal Revenue Code has its own standard, which is a subal one, dependent neither on the niceties of the particular conveyancing used nor on the technical description which the law of property gives estate or interest transferred to the trustees or beneficiaries of the trust. at determination, among the material factors are: The fact that the s is to be returned to the grantor after a specific term; the fact that the s is or may be administered in the interest of the grantor; the fact that ticipated income is being appropriated in advance for the customary ditures of the grantor or those which he would ordinarily and naturally and any other circumstance bearing on the impermanence and inteness with which the grantor has parted with the substantial incidents nership in the corpus. hus, the grantor is regarded as being in substance the owner of the s if, in any case, the trust amounts to no more than an arrangement by the grantor, in the ordering of his affairs, finds it expedient to enfor a period the title to, and custody or management of, certain of his rty to a trustee, the income from such property to be used by the trustee

during such period to make those expenditures which the grantor y customarily or ordinarily or naturally make and to which the grantor ch to commit himself in advance, while the corpus is to be held intact, fo turn in due course to the grantor. In such a case, it is immaterial that, time of the creation of the trust, an irrevocable disposition or consumm gift was made of those property rights which consist of the right to expected future income of the corpus for the specified period. On the hand, if the grantor, incident to a definitive and permanent dispositi certain of his property, creates the trust in order to conserve the prop not for himself but for the donees, who will ultimately enjoy it, the visions of sections 161, 162, and 163 are applicable.

For example, a grantor is regarded as remaining in substance the o of the corpus of the trust, if he has placed it in trust for his son, John, (A) for the term of three years, at the end of which time the might be extended for a like period at the option of the grantor and cessively thereafter, but in the absence of such an extension the ti once more to revest in the grantor in possession and enjoyment; or (B) for the term of a year and a day, then to be distributed to w soever the wife of the grantor shall by deed appoint (the wife not h a substantial adverse interest in the disposition of the corpus or th come therefrom); or (C) for the term of the grantor's life, then to be distributed to the grantor reserving, however, the right to alter, amend, or revok provision of the trust instrument, upon notice of a year and a day. 

In these typical cases the grantor is regarded as having retained the stantial incidents of ownership with respect to the income-prod property since the corpus will or may once more revest in himself in upon the expiration of the trust period if the grantor does not exerci option to extend the trust, in (B) upon the designation of the grant distributee, by a person not substantially and adversely interested, and in upon the revocation of the trust instrument or an alteration or amend thereof, resulting in the designation of the grantor as distributee. 

If, however, the grantor strips himself of the substantial inciden attributes of ownership in the corpus retained by him so that he ceases regarded as in substance the owner of the corpus, the income thereof rea after the effective date of such divesting is not taxable to the grantor I taxable as provided in sections 161, 162, and 163.

A person may have an interest that is both substantial and adverse t grantor in the disposition of only part of the corpus or the income there If the power to revest title in the grantor is vested in him in conjunction such person, or is vested solely in such person, there is to be exclud computing the net income of the grantor only the income of such part (c) Income and Deductions. If the grantor is regarded as remaini substance the owner of the corpus the gross income of such corpus sh included in the gross income of the grantor, and he shall be allowed deductions with respect to the corpus as he would have been entitled t the trust not been created. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Sec. 19.166-1, 1940.

How is income for the benefit of a grantor taxed? a) Where any part of the income of a trust- (1) is, or in the discretion of the grantor or of any person not having substantial adverse interest in the disposition of such part of the income nay be, held or accumulated for future distribution to the grantor; or (2) may, in the discretion of the grantor or of any person not having substantial adverse interest in the disposition of such part of the income, e distributed to the grantor; or (3) is, or in the discretion of the grantor or of any person not having substantial adverse interest in the disposition of such part of the income may be, applied to the payment of premiums upon policies of insurance on the life of the grantor [except policies of insurance irrevocably payble for the purposes and in the manner specified in section 23 (o), relatng to the so-called "charitable contribution" deduction]; such part of the income of the trust shall be included in computing the ncome of the grantor. b) As used in this section, the term "in the discretion of the grantor" as "in the discretion of the grantor, either alone or in conjunction with person not having a substantial adverse interest in the disposition of the of the income in question.' 

Internal Revenue Code, Sec. 167. 

ART. 19.167-1. TRUSTS IN THE INCOME OF WHICH THE GRANTOR RETAINS 

NTEREST.—(a) Scope.-Section 167 prescribes that the income, or any of the income, of certain trusts shall be taxed to the grantor, not because grantor has retained a certain interest in the corpus of the trust (as in on 166), but because of his retention of a certain interest in the income e trust. This article deals with the taxation of such income. The term ome," as used in this article, means any part or the whole of the income e trust. b) Test of taxability to the grantor.-The test of the sufficiency of the tor's retained interest in the trust income, resulting in the taxation of income to the grantor, is whether the grantor has failed to divest himpermanently and definitively, of every right which might, by any posty, enable him to have the income, at some time, distributed to him, lly or constructively. Such a distribution to the grantor occurs if the ne is paid to him or to another according to the grantor's direction or if, gh paid to another pursuant to the terms of the trust, the benefit of the ne inures to the grantor. The income so inures if it is or may be applied tisfaction of a legal obligation of the grantor, does or may increase his vorth, does or may in any way enrich him, or does or may enable him y way to enjoy, in substance, such income. Such a distribution also rs if the income is applied in payment of premiums upon policies of ance on the grantor's life. the grantor has retained any such interest in the income, such income cable to the grantor regardless of— (1) whether it may be distributed currently or accumulated for future istribution;

(2) whether such distribution, either current or subject to acc lation, is fixed by the trust instrument or is dependent on an exerci discretion; (3) whether, if such distribution is in any way effected by of pendent on an exercise of discretion, the person exercising the discr is the grantor or a person not having a substantial interest in the ine adverse to the grantor, or both. A bare legal interest, such as that trustee, is never substantial and never adverse; (4) the time or times of such distribution, whether within or wit the taxable period, whether conditioned on the precedent giving of no or on the elapsing of an interval of time, or on the happening of a spec event, or otherwise; (5) when the trust was created. 

Thus, the inclusion of any trust within the scope of section 167 is b on the fact that the grantor has retained an interest in the income there by which he is, or may be enabled at some time, to receive its benefits. the provisions of section 167 are not to be regarded as excluding from tax to the grantor the income of other trusts, not specified therein, in whic grantor is, for the purposes of the Internal Revenue Code, similarly rega as remaining in substance the owner of the trust income. If, for exa trust income is applied in satisfaction of the grantor's legal obligation wh to pay a debt, to support dependents, to pay alimony, to furnish mainter and support, or otherwise, such income is in all cases taxable to the gra If the grantor strips himself permanently and definitely of every interest retained by him, the income of the trust realized after such dive takes effect is not taxable to the grantor but is taxable as provided in sec 

161 and 162.

A person may have an interest that is both substantial and adverse t grantor in the disposition of only part of the income. There is to be excl in computing the net income of the grantor only that part of the trus come in the disposition of which such person has a substantial interest ad to the grantor. (c) Income and deductions.-If, as to any of the income, the test of ability to the grantor is satisfied, such income shall be included in the income of the grantor, and he shall be allowed those deductions with re to such income as he would have been entitled to had such income distributable currently to him. 

Treasury Regulations No. 103, Sec. 19.167-1, 1940.

CHAPTER 17  

Failure to make oath of verification-Remedy for overpayment of taxes-Abatements -Interest for non-payment of taxes- Penalty for non-payment of taxes - Misunderstandings in tax law-Incorporation to reduce personal taxes - Avoidance of taxes – 1941 tax on manufactured goods — 1941 tax on transportation-1941 tax on use of automobiles and motorboats-1941 tax on liquors Treasury Department's Tax Saving Plan 

f the taxpayer fails to take an oath of verification what may the Court say e Commissioner assessing 25% additional tax?

However lightly some persons may deem the taking of an oath in veriion of an accounting, Congress must have regarded as necessary for tax sment and collection that the taxpayer assume the risk of a charge of ury as a sanction for great care in his return. Congress must have conred it an essential aid to the Commissioner of Internal Revenue in deining the many doubtful and border line returns of an account. To to give the Commission such a required aid is a proper reason for the alty. The fact that in a particular case all the items of the accounting car in the "so-called return" does not give the Commissioner the assurrequired and does not warrant an exception to the application of a genrule of essential requirement in the return. The Board's decision uping the Commissioner's proposed assessment of the 25% additional tax firmed. 

Denman, J., Uhl Estate v. C. of I. R., 116 F. 2d 403, 1940. f a taxpayer overstates his income or fails to make proper deductions, what he promptly do? n the administration of the Internal Revenue Laws, we are concerned substance and not form. The prerequisite refunding statutes requiring ns for refund before suit, should receive a practical construction to efuate their purpose. The income tax law is complex and does not always nit a determination of the taxpayer's liability in the first instance. Inable mistakes occur. Statutory provision is made for the adjustment of tax where understated by the taxpayer and as a corollary a statutory hod is provided to relieve a taxpayer from over statements. It is vital to functions of government that taxes be collected promptly and if errors [137]

in returns are made that they be expeditiously corrected. To this end statute requires the taxpayer to make a timely charge of overpayment grounds therefor, that the government may make investigation and re the amount due if any, without being subjected to the delay and expens litigation and any timely claim showing intention to ask a refund of allegedly overpaid and the grounds therefor is sufficient to answer purpose. [Some state laws require a request for abatement to be made on f prescribed by the commissioner of taxation, as in Massachusetts.] 

Hamilton, J., Kales v. U. S., 115 F. 2d 497, 1940.

If more than is justly due is collected what will the courts do? 

In matters of taxation the essential thing is that the amount justly due be collected, neither more or less; and courts are always anxious to give e to this principle. There are, however, certain formal requirements w must be observed before there can be any recovery of overpaid taxes. of these is that a proper claim for refund was filed. It is settled that a general claim for refund is not sufficient. "The claim for refund" w sec. 1218 (Revenue Act 1921) make prerequisite to suit, obviously rel to the claim, which may be asseted by the suit. Hence, quite apart from provisions of the Regulations the statute is not satisfied by the filing paper which gives no notice of the amount or nature of the claim for w the suit is brought and refers to no facts upon which it may be founded. 

Stone, J., [now C.J.] U. S. v. Felt Tarrant Co., 283 U.S. 

269, quoted by Court in United Shoe Machinery Corp. v. 

White, 91 F. 2d 342, 1937. Where a statute prescribes the time within which an abatement mus asked why must that time be respected?

The rule is well settled in this country that whenever a statute gran right which did not exist at common law, and prescribes the time wi which the right must be exercised, the limitation thus imposed does not fect the remedy merely, but is of the essence of the right itself, and one seeks to enforce such right must show affirmatively that he has brought action within the time fixed by the statute; and if he fails in this regard fails to disclose any right to relief under the statute. 

Dolenty v. Broadwater County, 45 Mont. 261, quoted by 

Zinn, In re Blatt, 67 Pac. 2d 293, N. Mex. 1937. 

What is the effect of a tax abatement on Form 53?

It should also be noted in this connection that, while the word "ab ment" usually refers to a cancellation or an annulment of a tax or assessm which has been changed on application of the taxpayer, the particular ab ment in this case was an altogether different matter. It was a claim filed the collector on form 53 to abate an uncollectible tax so far as the colled was concerned. This form of abatement of claim is used by collectors the purpose of clearing their books of a tax which they have determined

uncollectible, and for the purpose of advising the bureau of this condition. does not cancel or annul the tax, but, on the contrary, the Treasury Regutions provide in substance that the obligation to pay the tax still remains on the person assessed, and it is the collector's duty to use diligence to llect it if opportunity arises. 

Kioyer v. U. S., 55 F. 2d 495, quoted by Kirkpatrick, J., in Sugar Run Coal Co. v. U. S. 21 F. Supp. 10, 1937. 

How is interest for non-payment of taxes considered?

We have heretofore held that the increased percentage and other burdens escribed by the Legislature for non-payment of taxes, are in the nature of nalties, and are not part of the taxes. ... They may have been prescribed a means of inducing the taxpayers to pay promptly, but they are disctive from the tax itself. Taxes are a contribution prescribed by the tute and levied by the authorities for the support of the government, the alties are somewhat in the nature of a fine upon a delinquent taxpayer his delay in paying his taxes. They are not levied by the counties as part the taxes, but are creatures of the statute, and, what the statute has imsed by way of penalty, the statute can remit. 

State v. Coos County, 115 Ore. 300, 237 Pac. 678, quoted by Carter, J., in Tukey v. Douglas County, 277 N.Ŵ. 57, Nebra. 1938.

How do the courts consider the government charging 12% a year on paid taxes in addition to a 5% penalty?

The tax in this case is one on income; a burden imposed for the support the government. Interest is put upon it and so denominated, distinguished m the 5% penalty, clearly intended to compensate the delay in the paynt of taxes-the detriment of its nonpayment, to be continued during the e of its nonpayment-compensation not punishment. 

New York v. Jersawit, 263 U.S. 493, quoted by Wilbur, J., in Unemployment Reserves Com. v. Meilink, 116 F. 2d 

330, 1941. 

How do the courts consider methods to reduce taxation by an individual?

It is clear that while motive to escape taxation may be of no importance, he methods used are in reality those intended by the law, it is not always ugh that the letter of the taxing statute is followed. The decisive thing whether or not what has been done is "the thing which the statute inded." The taxpayer must bring himself within the intent of the statute n which he relies. 

Com. v. Riggs, 78 F. 2d 1004, quoted by Biggs, J., in 

Com. v. W. F. Trimble & Sons Co., 98 F. 2d 853, 1938.

A taxpayer may resort to any legal method available to him to diminish amount of his tax liability. 

Com. v. Eldridge, 79 F. 2d 629, also quoted in the above case.

Do the courts consider every misunderstanding of the tax law as crimin Congress did not intend that a person, by reason of a bona fide n understanding as to his liability for the tax, as to his duty to make a retu or as to the adequacy of the records he maintained, should become a crimi by his mere failure to measure up to the prescribed standard of conduct. A the requirement that the omission in these instances must be willful, to criminal, is persuasive that the same element is essential to the offense of f ing to supply information.

U. S. v. Murdock, 290 U.S. 289, quoted by Miller, J., in 

Townsend v. U. S., 95 F. 2d 352, 1938. How are the statutes to prevent fraud on revenue construed? 

By the now settled doctrine of this court... statutes to prevent fra upon the revenue are considered as enacted for the public good, and to s press a public wrong, and therefore although they impose penalties forfeitures not to be construed, like penal laws generally; strictly in fa of the defendant; but they are to be fairly and reasonably construed so to carry out the intention of the legislature. 

United States v. Stowell, 133 U.S. 1, quoted in U. S. v. 

One 1936 Model Ford Dump Truck, 25 F. Supp. 600, 1938.

If a taxpayer organizes a corporation to receive his income how may courts consider the plan as far as the tax is concerned? 

The fact that the petitioner organized two corporations, owned and o trolled entirely by him, in anticipation of diverting to them the income knew he would earn, instead of receiving these earnings himself, cannot a him to escape a proper tax. A taxpayer cannot enter into a contract un which he assigns to another income to be earned and thereby shift the on that income from himself. Here the assignment was made to a corp tion the petitioner himself controlled. 

Groves v. Commissioner, 99 F. 2d 179, quoted by Major, J., in C. of I. R. v. Griffiths, 103 F. 2d 110, 1939.

May the owner of a negotiable bond avoid liability for tax on the inte by detaching the interest coupons before their due date and making a gif them?

Although the donor here, by the transfer of the coupons has preclu any possibility of his collecting them himself he has nevertheless, by his procured payment of the interest, as a valuable gift to a member of family. Such a use of his economic gain, the right to receive income, to cure a satisfaction which can be obtained only by the expenditure of mo or property, would seem to be the enjoyment of the income whether satisfaction is the purchase of goods at the corner grocery, the paymen his debt there, or such non-material satisfactions as may result from the ment of a campaign or community chest contribution or a gift to his fav

n. Even though he never receives the money he derives money's worth om the disposition of the coupons which he has used as money or money's orth in the procurement of a satisfaction which is procurable only by the penditure of money or money's worth. The enjoyment of the economic nefit accruing to him by virtue of his acquisition of the coupons is realized completely as it would have been if he had collected the interest in dollars d expended them for any of the purposes named. 

Stone, C.J., Helvering v. Horst, 61 S.Ct. 144, 1940, quoted by Gardner, J., in Rand v. Helvering, 116 F. 2d 929, 1941.

If a husband and wife make an agreement whereby one half of all the aries, fees, and royalties earned by the husband belong to the wife, may the sband be taxed for his whole income or only for one half? very forcible argument is presented to the effect that the statute seeks tax only income beneficially received, and that taking the question more hnically the salary and fees became the joint property of the husband and fe on the very first instant on which they were received. We well might itate upon the latter proposition, because however the matter might stand ween husband and wife he was the only party to the contract by which salary and fees were earned, and it is somewhat hard to say that the step in the performance of those contracts could be taken by anyone but self alone. But this case is not to be decided by attenuated subtleties. It ns on the import and reasonable construction of the taxing act. There is doubt that the statute could tax salaries to those who earned them and vide that the tax could not be escaped by anticipatory arrangements and tracts however skillfully devised to prevent the salary when paid from ting even for a second in the man who earned it. That seems to us the port of the statute before us and we think that no distinction can be taken ording to the motives leading to the arrangement by which the fruits are ibuted to a different tree from that on which they grew. 

Holmes, J., Lucas v. Earl, 281 U.S. 111, 1930.

May a taxpayer who pays a high surtax reduce his tax liability by making outright gift of the income producing property? 

The power to dispose of income is the equivalent of ownership of it. exercise of that power to procure the payment of income to another is enjoyment, and hence the realization, of the income by him who exercises We have had no difficulty in applying that proposition where the assignt preceded the rendition of the services, Lucas v. Earl, supra; Burnet v. inger, supra, for it was recognized in the Leininger case that in such a the rendition of the service by the assignor was the means by which income was controlled by the donor and of making his assignment efive. But it is the assignment by which the disposition of income is coned when the service precedes the assignment, and in both cases it is the cise of the power of disposition of the interest or compensation, with resulting payment to the donee, which is the enjoyment by the donor of me derived from them.

This was emphasized in Blair v. Commissioner, 300 U.S. 5, on which spondent relies, where the distinction was taken between a gift of incor derived from an obligation to pay compensation and a gift of incon producing property. In the circumstances of that case, the right to inco from the trust property was thought to be so identified with the equital ownership of the property, from which alone the beneficiary derived his rig to receive the income and his power to command disposition of it, that a g of the income by the beneficiary became effective only as a gift of his own ship of the property producing it. Since the gift was deemed to be a gift the property, the income from it was held to be the income of the owner the property, who was the donee, not the donor-a refinement which was necessary if respondent's contention here is right, but one clearly in plicable to gifts of interest or wages. Unlike income thus derived from obligation to pay interest or compensation, the income of the trust was garded as no more the income of the donor than would be the rent from lease or a crop raised on a farm after the leasehold or the farm had be given away. 

Stone, J., [now C. J.] Helvering v. Horst, 311 U.S. 112, 1940.

How may a court consider a plan of a taxpayer forming a partnership w his wife and infant son, by which he divided his earnings and income tax th ways?

In form, petitioner was a member of partnerships from January 1, 19 to January 1, 1931. But the income tax statutes regard substance rather th form. United States v. Phellis, 257 U.S. 156, 42. Tax laws deal with reali and look at the entire transaction. Looking at the substance of the partn ship transactions and disregarding mere form, it is obvious that the t partnerships had no business functions. Neither the wife nor the infant contributed either capital, services or anything of value to the partnersh The partnerships, were merely patent devices "to exalt artifice above realit 

Gregory v. Helvering, 293 U.S. 465. The partnerships in this case amoun in essence to no more than an attempt by petitioner to separate his inco for tax purposes, in one partnership, into two parts and in the other partn ship into three parts. It is fundamental that income is taxable to the per who earns it. This Court holds with the Board that there was no valid ex ing partnership under the income tax laws. The device of a partnership without legal effect, so far as the income tax law is concerned, and the ta were properly assessed against petitioner. Jones v. Page, 5 Cir., 102 F. 2d 1 

Woodward, J., Tinkoff v. C. I. R., 120 F. 2d 564, 1941.

What is meant by saying that the courts look to matters of substance and mere form in applying the tax laws?

As stated in the language of the Supreme Court: "Questions of taxa must be determined by viewing what was actually done, rather than the clared purpose of the participants; and when applying the provisions of 

Sixteenth Amendment and income laws enacted thereunder we must reg matters of substance and not mere form." Weiss v. Stearn, Com. 265 U.S.

This court has applied the rule in a number of cases, notably in Labrot v. met, Commissioner, 61 App. D.C. 47, 57 F. 2d 413. In that case a husband d wife had acquired a farm as partners. Subsequently they organized a rporation controlled by themselves to which they sold the farm for money a loss. The Commissioner there insisted that the sale was an exchange under e provisions of a revenue act identical with the one under consideration. e Board sustained the Commissioner, and we sustained the Board, holding at in transactions of that nature substance and not form determines the plicability of the taxing act. 

Groner, J., Louis W. Gunby, Inc. v. Helvering, 122 F. 

2d 203, 1941. 

How do the courts consider the collection of taxes?

It has frequently been pointed out that a tax lawfully imposed is not to regarded as an ordinary debt, but is an obligation which is to be regarded paramount to all other demands, although the law imposing the tax does t expressly provide that it is to have priority. These cases proceed upon e theory that the maintenance of the government and the public welfare so dependent upon the collection of taxes that payment should have predence over all other claims; and it is thought that taxes levied for the suprt of government are founded upon a higher obligation than other demands. 

Liberty Mutual Ins. Co. v. Johnson Shipyards Corp., 6 F. 

2d 752, quoted by Thompson, J., in In re Morris Estate, 99 P. 2d 294, Cal. 1940.

What is the 1941 tax imposed on transportation of persons? (B) Chapter 30 of the Internal Revenue Code is amended by inserti after section 3468 the following new subchapter: 

"SUBCHAPTER C-TRANSPORTATION OF PERSONS "SEC. 3469. TAX ON TRANSPORTATION OF PERSONS, ETC. 

"(a) TRANSPORTATION.-There shall be imposed upon the amount p within the United States, on or after October 10, 1941, for the transportati on or after such effective date, of persons by rail, motor vehicle, water, air, within or without the United States, a tax equal to 5 per centum of amount so paid. Such tax shall apply to transportation by motor vehic having a passenger seating capacity of less than 10 adult passengers, includi the driver, only when such vehicle is operated on an established line. 

"(b) EXEMPTION OF CERTAIN TRIPS.-The tax imposed by subsection shall not apply to amounts paid for transportation which do not exceed cents, to amounts paid for commutation or season tickets for single trips less than thirty miles, or to amounts paid for commutation tickets for month or less. "(c) SEATS, BERTHS, ETC.-There shall be imposed upon the amount p within the United States for seating or sleeping accommodations in conn tion with transportation with respect to which a tax is imposed by s section (a) a tax equivalent to 5 per centum of the amount so paid. "(d) RETURNS AND PAYMENT.-The taxes imposed by this section sh be paid by the person making the payment subject to the tax. Each pers receiving any payment specified in subsection (a) or (c) shall collect amount of the tax imposed from the person making such payment, and sh on or before the last day of each month, make a return, under oath, for preceding month, and pay the taxes so collected to the collector in the trict in which his principal place of business is located, or if he has no pr cipal place of business in the United States, to the collector at Baltimo

aryland. Such returns shall contain such information and be made in such inner as the Commissioner. with the approval of the Secretary may by gulations prescribe. 

"(e) EXTENSIONS OF TIME.-The Commissioner may extend the time for king returns and paying the taxes collected, under such rules and regulans as he shall prescribe with the approval of the Secretary, but no such cension shall be for more than ninety days. 

"(f) EXEMPTIONS.— “(1) GOVERNMENTAL EXEMPTION.-The tax imposed by this section shall not apply to the payment for transportation or facilities furnished to the United States, or to any State or Territory, or political subdivision thereof, or the District of Columbia. 

“(2) EXEMPTION OF MEMBERS OF MILITARY AND NAVAL SERVICE.—The tax imposed by this section shall not apply to the payment for transportation or facilities furnished under special tariffs providing for fares of not more than 14 cents per mile applicable to round trip tickets sold to personnel of the United States Army, Navy, Marine Corps, and Coast Guard traveling in uniform of the United States at their own expense when on official leave, furlough, or pass, including authorized cadets and midshipmen, issued on presentation of properly executed certificate. 

(C) STAMP TAX ON PASSAGE TICKETS NOT TO APPLY.-No tax shall be posed under Chapter 11 of the Internal Revenue Code on a ticket sold or ed for passage the amount paid for which is taxable under section 3469 

the Internal Revenue Code. 

Internal Revenue Code, Sec. 3469. 

What is the 1941 tax imposed on the use of automobiles and motorboats? 

The Internal Revenue Code is amended by inserting after Chapter 33 

following new chapter: 

"CHAPTER 33A-USE OF MOTOR VEHICLES AND BOATS "SEC. 3540. TAX ON USE OF MOTOR VEHICLES AND BOATS. 

(a) IMPOSITION OF TAX.-There shall be imposed upon the use of motor icles and boats a tax, with respect to each year in which such use occurs, he following rates: 

"(1) MOTOR VEHICLES.-$5. 

"(2) BOATS.- "Over-all length 16 feet or over but not over 28 feet, $5. "Over-all length over 28 feet but not over 50 feet, $10. "Over-all length over 50 feet but not over 100 feet, $40. "Over-all length over 100 feet but not over 150 feet, $100. "Over-all length over 150 feet but not over 200 feet, $150. "Over-all length over 200 feet, $200.

Such tax, in the case of a motor vehicle, shall be paid by the person in wh name the motor vehicle is, or is required to be, registered under the law the State, Territory, or the District of Columbia in which such motor vehi is, or is required to be, registered. Such tax, in the case of a boat, shall paid by the owner of the boat. The tax imposed by this section shall apply to any use before February 1, 1942, and use before such date s not be considered to be use within the meaning of this section. 

"(b) DEFINITIONS.-For the purpose of this section- 

"(1) The term 'year' means the year beginning July 1. "(2) The term 'motor vehicle' means all motor vehicles of the k chiefly used for highway transportation. "(3) The term 'boat' means all boats propelled by machinery, s or both, measuring 16 feet or more in over-all length, owned by a citi or resident of the United States. Such term does not include boats u chiefly for trade, or commercial fishing, or boats used without profit any benevolent, charitable, or religious organization exclusively for f nishing aid, comfort, or relief to seamen, or boats used by the Sea Sco department of the Boy Scouts of America chiefly for training scout seamanship. "(4) The term 'use' in the case of the use of a motor vehicle me use on the public highways. "(c) PRORATION OF TAX.-If in any year the first use of the motor veh or boat is after July 31 the tax shall be reckoned proportionately from first day of the month in which such use occurs to and including the 3 day of June following. 

"(d) ONE PAYMENT PER YEAR.-If the tax imposed by this section paid with respect to any motor vehicle or boat for any year no further shall be imposed for such year with respect to such motor vehicle or b 

"(e) EVIDENCE OF TAX PAYMENT.-The payment of the tax imposed this section shall be evidenced by such suitable stamp, sticker, or tag of s form, which shall be affixed to the motor vehicle or boat in such manner the Commissioner, with the approval of the Secretary, may by regulati prescribe. 

"(f) MANNER OF COLLECTION.-The place, time, and manner of mak payment of the tax, and of furnishing such stamp, sticker, or tag shall such as may be provided in regulations prescribed by the Commissio with the approval of the Secretary. 

Internal Revenue Code, Sec. 3540. 

SEC. 558. EFFECTIVE DATE OF PART V. 

This Part shall take effect on October 1, 1941.

What is the Treasury Department's Tax Saving Plan? 

It is a plan started August 1, 1941, to help the taxpayer set aside money, uring the year in which earned, to pay income tax bills due the next year, at is, bonds bearing interest may be bought in 1941 and used to pay the x when it becomes due March 15, 1942. 

Q. Who can buy the notes issued under the Treasury's new "TAX

AVINGS PLAN"? 

A. Only those who pay Federal income taxes should buy these notes. 

The Series A notes, however, are especially designed for the small taxpayer. eries B notes are designed for larger taxpayers, either individual or corporate. 

Q. Can I use these notes to pay any kind of taxes?

A. You can use them only to pay Federal income taxes (current and ack personal and corporation taxes, excess-profits taxes). 

Q. What happens to a person's Tax Savings notes if he dies?

A. The notes can be presented by the estate for the payment of income xes due from the decedent, or they can be redeemed for the amount paid or them. For further instructions consult the Federal Reserve Bank that sued the notes. 

Q. Are these Tax Savings notes transferable? 

A. No. Nor can they be used as collateral. 

Q. Will the Tax Savings notes be registered?

· 

A. No, but they will have the owner's name and address inscribed there so that the Collector of Internal Revenue can compare the owner's nar with that on his tax return. 

Q. Are the Treasury's Tax Savings notes taxable?

A. The income from these notes is not exempt from Federal taxatio 

Q. Can I redeem my Treasury Tax Savings notes for cash?

A. Yes. Series A notes can, at the option of the owner, be redeemed the price you paid for them at any time without advance notice. Series notes can be redeemed at the price you paid for them any time after 60 da from the time of issue, upon 30 days' notice. 

Q. Where can I redeem them?

A. At the Federal Reserve Bank that issued them, in accordance wi instructions on the back of each note. 

For how much can you buy these Treasury Tax Savings Notes and for h much can they be cashed in or how much taxes will they pay when used?
